Extremising eigenvalues of the GJMS operators in a fixed conformal class Thanks: E.H. is supported by the project Einstein-PPF ( ANR-23-CE40-0010 ), funded by the French National Research Agency. B.P. is supported by the Fonds Thélam, by an ARC Avancé 2020 grant and by an EoS FNRS grant.
Emmanuel Humbert, Romain Petrides, Bruno Premoselli

Abstract
. Let ( M , g ) (M,g) be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 . If s s is a positive integer satisfying 2 ​ s < n 2s<n , we let P g s P_{g}^{s} be the GJMS operator of order 2 ​ s 2s in M M . We investigate in this paper the extremal values taken by fixed eigenvalues of P h s P_{h}^{s} as h h runs through the whole conformal class [ g ] [g] . Because of the conformal covariance of P g s P_{g}^{s} , two variational problems are of particular interest: the problem of maximising negative eigenvalues of P h s P_{h}^{s} and the one of minimising positive eigenvalues of P h s P_{h}^{s} , as h ∈ [ g ] h\in[g] . These extremal values – that we call throughout the paper conformal eigenvalues – are conformal invariants of ( M , g ) (M,g) and optimisers for these problems, when they exist, are known to not be smooth metrics in general. In this paper we develop a general framework that allows us to address the the existence theory for extremals of conformal eigenvalues. We define and investigate eigenvalues for singular conformal metrics, that we call generalised eigenvalues . We develop a new variational framework for renormalised eigenvalues of any index over the set of admissible (singular) conformal factors: we obtain semi-continuity results and Euler-Lagrange equations for local extremals. Using this framework we prove, under mild assumptions on ( M , g ) (M,g) and s s , several new (non)-existence results for extremals of renormalised eigenvalues over [ g ] [g] . These include, among other results, a maximisation result for negative eigenvalues, the minimisation of the principal eigenvalue of P g s P_{g}^{s} and the analysis of the conformal eigenvalues of the round sphere ( 𝕊 n , g 0 ) (\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0}) . We also establish a strong connection between the existence of optimisers and (nodal) solutions of prescribed Q Q -curvature equations. Our analysis allows any order s ≥ 1 s\geq 1 and allows P g s P_{g}^{s} to have kernel. Previous results only covered the cases s = 1 , 2 s=1,2 and k = 1 , 2 k=1,2 . Our work strongly generalises these results to any s ≥ 1 s\geq 1 and to eigenvalues of any order.



1.
Introduction and Statement of the results

1.1.
Conformal eigenvalues

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemmanian manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
, that is compact without boundary. Throughout this paper
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
will denote a positive integer satisfying
s<\frac{n}{2}
, and we will denote by
P_{g}^{s}
the celebrated GJMS operator of order
2s
in
M
. It is an elliptic self-adjoint differential operator of order
2s
in
M
which was discovered by Graham, Jenne, Mason and Sparling in
[
19
]
and plays a central role in conformal geometry.
P_{g}^{s}
is conformally covariant in the following sense: if
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
u>0
and
\hat{g}=u^{\frac{4}{n-2s}}g\in[g]
, then

P_{\hat{g}}^{s}(f)=u^{-\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}P_{g}^{s}(uf)\quad\text{ for all }f\in C^{\infty}(M).

When
s=1
,
P_{g}^{1}
is the celebrated conformal laplacian

P_{g}^{1}=\Delta_{g}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}S_{g},

where we have let
\Delta_{g}=-\text{div}_{g}(\nabla\cdot)
and where
S_{g}
denotes the scalar curvature of
(M,g)
, while for
s=2
the operator
P_{g}^{2}
is the so-called Paneitz-Branson operator
[
6
,
45
]
. We refer to subsection
2.2
below for more detailed properties of these operators.
Since
M
is closed the spectrum of
P_{g}^{s}
is discrete and consists in a non-decreasing sequence of eigenvalues that we will denote by

{\lambda}_{1}(g)\leq{\lambda}_{2}(g)\leq...\leq{\lambda}_{k}(g)\rightarrow_{k\to+\infty}+\infty.

These eigenvalues obey the famous Rayleigh characterisation:

{\lambda}_{k}(g)=\inf_{\underset{\dim V=k}{V\subset H^{s}(M)}}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}v\,dv_{g}}{\int_{M}v^{2}\,dv_{g}},

and they possess a Hilbert basis of
L^{2}(M)
-normalised associated eigenfunctions satisfying
P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{k}=\lambda_{k}(g)\varphi_{k}
in
M
for every
k\geq 1
. Throughout this paper we will use the following notation:

k_{-}=\max\big\{k\geq 1,{\lambda}_{k}(g)<0\big\}\;\hbox{ and }k_{+}=\min\big\{k\geq 1,{\lambda}_{k}(g)>0\big\}.

It is well-known (see e.g.
[
7
]
and also
[
14
]
) that
k_{-}
and
k_{+}
are conformal invariants, hence only depend on
[g]
. With these notations we have
\dim{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=k_{+}-k_{-}-1
and, if
{\lambda}_{1}(g)\geq 0
, we set by convention
k_{-}=0
.

In this article we address the following question: how do the eigenvalues of the operators
P_{h}^{s}
, for
s\geq 1
, behave as
h
runs through the whole conformal class
[g]
? More precisely, we are interested in the problem of determining the extremal values taken by eigenvalues of
P_{h}^{s}
when
h
runs through
[g]
. The case of zero eigenvalues, when they exist, is easily dealt with: if
k_{+}-k_{-}\geq 2
and
k_{-}<k<k_{+}
is fixed, the conformal covariance of
P_{g}^{s}
shows that
\lambda_{k}(h)=0
for every
h\in[g]
, and that
h\in[g]\mapsto\lambda_{k}(h)
is constant null. We will thus focus in this paper in the non-trivial cases
k\leq k_{-}
and
k\geq k_{+}
of, respectively, negative and positive eigenvalues of
P_{g}^{s}
. As a simple observation, the Rayleigh characterisation of the eigenvalues shows that for any metric
h
in
M
and for
\mu>0
we have
{\lambda}_{k}(\mu h){\mathop{\rm Vol}}(M,\mu h)^{\frac{2s}{n}}={\lambda}_{k}(h){\mathop{\rm Vol}}(M,h)^{\frac{2s}{n}}
. In order to avoid trivial degenerations to
0
or
\pm\infty
the relevant quantity that we will investigate are the
renormalised
eigenvalues given by

h\in[g]\mapsto{\lambda}_{k}(h){\mathop{\rm Vol}}(M,h)^{\frac{2s}{n}}\quad\text{ for some }k\geq 1.

For each
k\geq 1
fixed, one may wonder what the least and largest value of this functional in
[g]
are, and this leads to two distinct optimisation problems. A surprising observation is that one of these two problems is always easily solved: we indeed have

(1.1)

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in[g]}{\lambda}_{k}(h){\mathop{\rm Vol}}(M,h)^{\frac{2s}{n}}=+\infty

\displaystyle\hbox{ if }

\displaystyle k\geq k_{+},

\displaystyle\inf_{h\in[g]}{\lambda}_{k}(h){\mathop{\rm Vol}}(M,h)^{\frac{2s}{n}}=-\infty

\displaystyle\hbox{ if }

\displaystyle k\leq k_{-}.

In other words, positive eigenvalues of
P_{h}^{s}
are unbounded from above, and negative eigenvalues of
P_{h}^{s}
are unbounded from below, as
h
runs through
[g]
. Property (
1.1
) is an involved consequence of the conformal covariance of
P_{g}^{s}
: we prove it in Proposition
4.3
by combining the results in
[
2
]
and
[
10
]
. In view of (
1.1
) we are thus left with considering the problems of minimising positive eigenvalues and of maximising negative eigenvalues of
P_{h}^{s}
as
h\in[g]
. We introduce for this the following variational problems:

(1.2)

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=\left\{\begin{aligned} \inf_{h\in[g]}{\lambda}_{k}(h){\mathop{\rm Vol}}(M,h)^{\frac{2s}{n}}&\quad\hbox{ if }&k\geq k_{+},\\
\sup_{h\in[g]}{\lambda}_{k}(h){\mathop{\rm Vol}}(M,h)^{\frac{2s}{n}}&\quad\hbox{ if }&k\leq k_{-}.\end{aligned}\right.

We will say that
\Lambda_{k}(M,[g])
is the
k
-th conformal eigenvalue
of
(M,[g])
. Equivalently, since the renormalised eigenvalues are scale-invariant,

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=\left\{\begin{aligned} \inf_{h\in[g],{\mathop{\rm Vol}}(M,h)=1}{\lambda}_{k}(h)&\quad\hbox{ if }&k\geq k_{+},\\
\sup_{h\in[g],{\mathop{\rm Vol}}(M,h)=1}{\lambda}_{k}(h)&\quad\hbox{ if }&k\leq k_{-}.\end{aligned}\right.

In the case where
k_{+}-k_{-}\geq 2
and
k_{-}<k<k_{+}
we may similarly define
\Lambda_{k}(M,[g])=0
, although this is of little interest: this invariant is trivially attained by the previous discussion. For every
k\geq 1
,
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is a conformal invariant of
(M,g)
. In the following we will thus restrict our attention to the cases
k\geq k_{+}
or
k\leq k_{-}
. In this paper we thoroughly investigate (
1.2
): we aim at determining conditions under which
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained as well as precisely describing the geometric properties of their extremals, when they exist.

Problem (
1.2
) is inspired by similar eigenvalue-optimisation problems in conformal geometry, which have attracted a lot of attention in recent years. Most of the available litterature deals with the problem of maximising eigenvalues of the Laplace-Beltrami operator in a conformal class: this was investigated for instance in
[
49
,
48
,
51
,
43
,
44
,
30
,
32
]
on surfaces (that is, when
n=2
) or more recently in
[
31
,
50
]
on closed manifolds of dimension
n\geq 3
. Concerning the optimisation of eigenvalues of the GJMS operator
P_{g}^{s}
few results on the existence of extremals (
1.2
) are available in the literature and they only deal with the cases
s=1,2
and
k=1,2
: see
[
1
,
5
,
14
,
24
,
53
]
. No results are available when
s\geq 3
or when
k\geq 3
. Due to the conformal covariance of
P_{g}^{s}
problem (
1.2
) is of a different nature than the eigenvalue-optimisation of Laplace-Beltrami eigenvalues (investigated e.g. in
[
31
,
50
]
) and exhibits very different features : (
1.1
) shows, for instance, that
positive
eigenvalues of
P_{h}^{s}
are not bounded from above in
[g]
, in stark contrast with the eigenvalues of the Laplace-Beltrami operator (see
[
61
,
37
,
34
,
27
,
12
,
15
]
). In another direction,
[
1
]
shows that extremals for the second (positive) conformal eigenvalue of
P_{g}^{1}
, when they exist, are always simple; this is something that cannot happen in general for eigenvalues of the Laplace-Beltrami operator.

In this paper we perform a systematic investigation of problem (
1.2
). We develop a general variational formalism that allows us to tackle (
1.2
) at any order
s\geq 1
and for any eigenvalue
k\geq 1
and takes into account possibly singular metrics. Our aim is twofold: on the one hand we aim at understanding the specific features of problem (
1.2
) in the broader context of eigenvalue-optimisation problems in conformal classes in dimensions
n\geq 3
; we are confident that the techniques that we develop here will prove useful in related contexts. On the other hand we aim at studying the properties of
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
from the perspective of conformal geometry: the sequence
(\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g]))_{k\geq 1}
provides a new family of conformal invariants of
(M,g)
, and we believe that the spectral-theoretic perspective that we propose in this paper may provide additional insights in the conformal geometry of
(M,g)
.

1.2.
Generalised eigenvalues of GJMS operators

Throughout the paper we will assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies the following:

(1.3)

\displaystyle\text{ If }

\displaystyle\varphi\in C^{\infty}(M)\text{ satisfies }P_{g}^{s}\varphi=0\text{ and vanishes on a set }

\displaystyle\text{ of positive measure, then }\varphi=0.

Property (
1.3
) is a unique continuation property for kernel elements of
P_{g}^{s}
. It is trivially satisfied when
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
: this is the case, for instance, if
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive. When
s=1
, notably, (
1.3
) is true regardless of whether
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{1})
is trivial or not: this follows from classical unique continuation results for second-order operators (see e.g.
[
26
]
). In Proposition
2.2
below we give instances of
(M,g,s)
for which (
1.3
) is satisfied: when
s\geq 2
these include the case of analytic Riemannian manifolds and of locally conformally Einstein manifolds. To the best of our knowledge, however, (
1.3
) is not known to hold true in general when
s\geq 2
. The results that we prove in this paper all assume (
1.3
): all our results will in particular be true under the following assumptions (which imply (
1.3
)) :

when
s=1
, regardless of the geometry of
(M,g)
, and [when
s\geq 1
and
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
]
.

It is well-known that extremals for eigenvalue-optimisation problems, if they exist, may not be smooth metrics. When
n=2
it was highlighted in
[
33
,
48
]
that extremal metrics for the Laplace-Beltrami operator on surfaces may have a finite number of conical singularities. When
n\geq 3
, for the conformal Laplacian
P_{g}^{1}
in the case
s=1
, it was observed in
[
1
,
24
]
that the singular set of extremal metrics can be much wilder. In order to develop a comprehensive variational theory for eigenvalue functionals, that we want to rely on to determine when the invariants
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
are attained, we thus first need to generalise the notion of eigenvalues of
P_{g}^{s}
to non-smooth metrics
g
, in order to include the possible limiting cases of non-smooth metrics. We do this in the following way: if
k\geq 1
is an integer and
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)\backslash\{0\}
is a nonnegative function, we will let in what follows

{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)=\inf_{V\in\mathcal{G}^{\beta}_{k}(C^{\infty}(M))}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}v\,dv_{g}}{\int_{M}\beta v^{2}\,dv_{g}}.

Here
\mathcal{G}^{\beta}_{k}(C^{\infty}(M))
denotes the set of linear subspaces
V=\text{Span}(v_{1},\cdots,v_{k})
of smooth functions such that
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}v_{1},\cdots,\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}v_{k}
are linearly independent. When
\beta=1
, this is simply the classical min-max definition of eigenvalues of
P_{g}^{s}
. But as
\beta
varies this gives rise to a subtler definition: since we do not assume that
\beta>0
a.e. in
M
,
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
may very well be equal to
-\infty
when
k\leq k_{-}
. We will say that
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
is
the
k
-th generalised eigenvalue associated to
\beta
. Its geometric interpretation is as follows: if
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
u>0
, the conformal covariance properties of
P_{g}^{s}
and the Rayleigh characterisation of eigenvalues show that

{\lambda}_{k}(u^{\frac{4}{n-2s}}g)=\inf_{V\subset H^{s}(M),\dim V=k}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}v\,dv_{g}}{\int_{M}u^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}v^{2}\,dv_{g}}.

By a direct analogy,
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
should then be formally understood as the
k
-th eigenvalue of
P_{h}^{s}
for a formal metric
h=\beta^{\frac{1}{s}}g
. We thoroughly investigate the properties of
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
in Section
3
below: we show that it is well-defined provided (
1.3
) holds, and that one can associate generalised eigenfunctions to
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. In Proposition
3.5
below we prove in particular that the following holds:

(1.4)

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=\left\{\begin{aligned} \inf_{\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)\backslash\{0\},\beta\geq 0}{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}&\quad\hbox{ if }k\geq k_{+},\\
\sup_{\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)\backslash\{0\},\beta\geq 0}{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}&\quad\hbox{ if }k\leq k_{-}.\end{aligned}\right.

When
\beta
positive and smooth we have
{\mathop{\rm Vol}}(M,\beta h)^{\frac{2s}{n}}=\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}
: thus, (
1.4
) is simply a natural reformulation of problem (
1.2
) that allows
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
to be attained by singular metrics. In the rest of the paper we investigate whether
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained or not by solely focusing on problem (
1.4
). Following (
1.4
) we will say that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is
attained
if there exists
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)\backslash\{0\}
,
\beta\geq 0
, such that

\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=\lambda_{k}\big(\beta\big)\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}.

We will equivalently say that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained at the generalised metric
\beta^{\frac{1}{s}}g
. If
\beta
vanishes somewhere in
M
, this metric becomes singular on
\{\beta=0\}
.

1.3.
Statement of our main results

Our main results address the existence of extremals for
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
, and we show that the situation is radically different between negative and positive eigenvalues.

We first consider the case
k\leq k_{-}
, where we maximise negative generalised eigenvalues. Our first result shows that, in this case, a possibly singular maximal metric always exists in
[g]
:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
M
and we assume that (
1.3
) holds and that
k_{-}\geq 1
. Let
k\leq k_{-}
be an integer. Then
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<0
and
\Lambda_{k}(M,[g])
is attained at a generalised metric
\beta^{\frac{1}{s}}g
, where
\beta
is a nonnegative nonzero function and
\beta\in C^{0,\alpha}(M)
for some
0<\alpha<1
.

A simple consequence of Theorem
1.1
is in particular that
\Lambda_{k_{-}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained when
k_{-}\geq 1
and (
1.3
) holds. The assumption
k_{-}\geq 1
is necessary in order for negative eigenvalues to exist. Theorem
1.1
was previously proven in the case
s=1
and
k=2
in
[
24
]
, under the additional assumption
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
. Our analysis only assumes (
1.3
) and when
s=1
we are able to deal with every eigenvalue of index
k\leq k_{-}
without assuming that
P_{g}^{1}
has kernel. We point out that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<0
is a consequence of our analysis, and not an assumption.

We now turn to the case
k\geq k_{+}
and we consider the problem of minimising positive eigenvalues in
[g]
. It turns out that this case is harder since concentration phenomena may occur. In order to rule them out, we introduce an additional conformal invariant. If
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
denotes the round sphere we will denote, throughout this paper,
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n},[g_{0}])
simply by
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
. For
k\geq k_{+}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
we define

(1.5)

X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=\min\Bigg\{\Big({\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\cdots+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{r}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}\Big)^{\frac{2s}{n}}\Bigg\},

where the minimum is taken over the set of all indexes
r,\ell_{0}\in\doba{N}
and
\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}\in\doba{N}^{*}
such that

(1)

\ell_{0}\in\{0\}\cup\{k_{+},\cdots,k-1\}
, where by convention we let
\Lambda_{0}^{s}(M,[g])=0
;

(2)

\ell_{0}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=k
if
\ell_{0}\geq k_{+}
and
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=k-k_{+}+1
if
\ell_{0}=0
;

(3)

{\Lambda}_{\ell_{i}}(\doba{S}^{n})
,
1\leq i\leq r
are attained, and
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained if
\ell_{0}>0
.

The main result of our paper establishes that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
, when
k\geq k_{+}
, is attained provided it lies strictly below
X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
. It states as follows:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
and let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
s<\frac{n}{2}
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
M
. Let
k\geq k_{+}
be an integer. Then:

(1)

We have
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\leq X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
.

(2)

Assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). Then
\Lambda_{k}(M,[g])>0
. If in addition
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
then
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained at a generalised metric
\beta^{\frac{1}{s}}g
, where
\beta
is a nonnegative nonzero function and
\beta\in C^{0,\alpha}(M)
for some
0<\alpha<1
.

The gap condition
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
in Theorem
1.2
is reminiscent of similar existence results for conformal eigenvalues
[
48
,
49
,
51
,
50
,
43
,
32
]
. In the proof of Theorem
1.2
we prove that the invariant
X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
appears as an energy threshold for suitably constructed minimising sequences for
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
: more precisely, the assumption
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
, as is now well-understood, prevents these sequences to blow-up. Theorem
1.2
greatly generalises all the available existence results for (
1.2
) and had previously only been proven in very specific settings: in
[
1
]
when
s=1
,
k=2
and when
\lambda_{1}(g)\geq 0
, in
[
14
]
when
s=1
,
k=2
and
\lambda_{1}(g)<0
, and in
[
5
]
when
s=2
,
k=2
,
\lambda_{1}(g)\geq 0
and
(M,g)
is Einstein. Note also that Theorem
1.2
implies the results in
[
59
]
. One of the main novelties of our approach in Theorem
1.2
is that
X_{k}(M,[g])
only involves invariants
\Lambda_{\ell}^{s}(M,[g])
and
\Lambda_{\ell}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
of lower-order that are
themselves
attained. This is an important observation and it will allow us to obtain by induction many new (non)-existence results for extremals of the invariants
\Lambda_{k}(M,[g])
that we state in the next subsections (see e.g. Theorems
1.10
,
1.7
and
1.11
). An immediate consequence of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, that we state for clarity, is the following:

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
and let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
s<\frac{n}{2}
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
M
. If
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
) then

\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=0\Longleftrightarrow k_{-}<k<k_{+}.

1.4.
Consequences of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

We state in this subsection many important consequences of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
. We obtain (non)-existence results on the invariants
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
– in particular when
(M,g)
is the round sphere – as well as structural properties on the behavior of possible extremals for
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
.

1.4.1.
Simplicity of extremals in the threshold cases
k=k_{-}
and
k=k_{+}
.

The cases
k=k_{-}
and
k=k_{+}
in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, corresponding respectively to the largest negative and least positive eigenvalue, are of particular interest. In these cases we can prove that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
, when attained, is
simple
, and we make the statement of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
more precise. We first consider the case
k=k_{-}
:

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
M
and we assume that (
1.3
) holds and that
k_{-}\geq 1
. Then the generalised eigenspace associated to
\Lambda_{k_{-}}^{s}(M,[g])
is one-dimensional and there is
\varphi\in C^{2s,\alpha}(M)
,
0<\alpha<1
, a generalised eigenfunction which satisfies
\|\varphi\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}}=1
and

P_{g}^{s}\varphi=\Lambda_{k_{-}}^{s}(M,[g])|\varphi|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}{\varphi}\quad{in}\quad M.

Furthermore,
\Lambda_{k_{-}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained at the generalised metric
|\varphi|^{\frac{4}{n-2s}}g
. If in addition
s=1
and
k_{-}\geq 2
,
\varphi
changes sign.

If
s=1,k_{-}\geq 2
and
P_{g}^{1}
satisfies (
1.3
), Corollary
1.4
shows in particular that there exists a sign-changing solutions of the constant (negative) prescribed scalar curvature equation in
M
. This generalises the results in
[
24
]
. The case
k=k_{+}
is similar, provided we assume that
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained:

Corollary 1.5
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
M
and we assume that (
1.3
) holds. Assume that
{\Lambda}_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])<\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
. Then
{\Lambda}_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained, the generalised eigenspace associated to it is one-dimensional and there is
\varphi\in C^{2s,\alpha}(M)
,
0<\alpha<1
, a generalised eigenfunction which satisfies
\|\varphi\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}}=1
and

P_{g}^{s}\varphi=\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])|\varphi|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}{\varphi}\quad{in}\quad M.

Furthermore,
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained at the generalised metric
|\varphi|^{\frac{4}{n-2s}}g
. If in addition
s=1
and
k_{+}\geq 2
,
\varphi
changes sign.

The precise definition of generalised eigenfunctions will be given in Section
3
below. That the functions
\varphi
obtained in Corollaries
1.4
and
1.5
satisfy the (constant) prescribed
Q
-curvature equation should not be surprising. In the case where
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive, indeed, minimising
\Lambda_{1}^{s}(M,[g])
is equivalent to minimising the Rayleigh quotient associated to
P_{g}^{s}
(see Proposition
4.8
below). The problem of attaining the invariants
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
can thus be seen as an involved generalisation of the classical Yamabe problem for the
Q
-curvature, and Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
show that there is a direct analogy: negative eigenvalues are unconditionally attained (provided
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
)), while positive ones require the strict inequality
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
, which should be understood as a generalisation of the celebrated Aubin threshold criterion for the Yamabe equation
[
3
]
. This provides yet another motivation to investigate the invariants
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
in addition to their spectral theoretic relevance. We point out that the simplicity of
\Lambda_{k_{\pm}}^{s}(M,[g])
, when attained, is an indirect consequence of the conformal invariance of
P_{g}^{s}
and is specific to (
1.2
): by contrast, if one eigenvalue of the Laplace-Beltrami operator is attained by a maximal metric in the conformal class, then it is never simple for this metric.

1.4.2.
Conformal eigenvalues of the round sphere

The conformal eigenvalues
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
of the round sphere
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
are of paramount importance since, in view of (
1.5
) and Theorem
1.2
, they arise as energy thresholds for the existence theory of extremals of
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
on a general manifold
(M,g)
. In
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
it is well-known that
\lambda_{1}(g_{0})>0
: thus
k_{-}=0
,
k_{+}=1
, and (
1.3
) holds for
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
. For any
s\geq 1
, by the conformal covariance of
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
, it is easily seen that
\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is the optimal Sobolev constant in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and is attained at any round metric in
[g_{0}]
(see Proposition
4.8
below). Suprisingly, however,
\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
is never attained:

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
1\leq s<\frac{n}{2}
. Then

\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}=2\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}

and
\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is not attained.

In view of Theorem
1.6
it may be tempting to believe that the set of indexes
k\geq 1
for which
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is attained reduces to
\{1\}
. If
n\geq 2s+5
we are able to prove that this is not the case. This is the content of the next result:

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
s\geq 1
and assume that
n\geq 2s+5
. There exists
k\in\{3,\cdots,n+1\}
such that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is attained. Furthermore,
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
is not attained at a round metric.

Estimating precisely the value of the minimal such
k
for any
n
seems difficult. As the dimension
n
grows to infinity we are nevertheless able to obtain a much better bound than
k\leq n+1
and to prove that it remains bounded. We refer to Proposition
7.3
below. The question of determining precisely the set of indexes
k\geq 1
for which
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is attained remains widely open.

1.4.3.
Extremals of
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
.

We now focus on the special case
k=k_{+}
of the least positive eigenvalue. If
(M,g)
is a closed manifold, we provide sufficient conditions on
(M,g)
for
{\Lambda}_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
to be attained. We first assume that
P_{g}^{s}
has no kernel:

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
M
. We assume that
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}P_{g}^{s}=\{0\}
and that one of the following conditions is satisfied:

•

n\geq 2s+4
and
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat

•

[
2s+1\leq n\leq 2s+3
or
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat] and there exists
\xi\in M
such that
m(\xi)>0
, where
m(\xi)
is the mass of the Green’s function of
P_{g}^{s}
at
\xi
.

Then
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])<\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
and
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained.

When Theorem
1.8
applies, Corollary
1.5
then shows that
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
has a one-dimensional generalised eigenspace. When
2s+1\leq n\leq 2s+3
or when
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat, the so-called
mass
at
\xi\in M
is defined as the constant term in the expansion of the Green’s function of
P_{g}^{s}
at
\xi
: we refer to (
9.3
) below for its definition. When
k_{+}\geq 2
, Theorem
1.8
is new except in the special case
s=1
and
k_{+}=2
where it was proven in
[
14
]
. When
k_{+}=1
, that is when
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive, Theorem
1.8
amounts to minimising the Rayleigh quotient associated to
P_{g}^{s}
(see Proposition
4.8
). As such, it had already been proven in the context of the Yamabe equation when
s=1
in
[
3
,
58
]
, in the context of the Paneitz equation when
s=2
in
[
13
,
16
,
22
,
23
,
25
]
and for
s\geq 2
in
[
40
]
. The novelty in Theorem
1.8
consists in dealing with every eigenvalue of index
k_{+}\geq 1
: we develop for this a new approach by test-function computations. When
k_{+}=1,s=1
, and provided
3\leq n\leq 5
or
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat and not conformal to
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
, the celebrated positive mass theorem (
[
57
,
36
]
) asserts that the mass function is positive at every point. When
s=2
and
5\leq n\leq 7
or
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat, the mass function is positive in
M
provided
k_{+}=1
,
g
has positive Yamabe invariant and semi-positive
Q
-curvature and
(M,g)
is not conformally diffeomorphic to
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
, see
[
25
]
(and previous results by
[
23
,
28
,
42
]
). To our knowledge there are no results that ensure positivity of the mass of the Green’s function of
P_{g}^{s}
in
M
when
s\geq 3
. We point out however that Theorem
1.8
only requires the positivity of the mass at one point and can thus be applied in specific examples.

We now consider the case where
P_{g}^{s}
is allowed to have kernel, which fits into the framework of Theorem
1.2
provided (
1.3
) is satisfied. We prove the following analogue of Theorem
1.8
:

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
, and we assume that
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})\neq\{0\}
. Assume that
n\geq 4s+5
and that
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat. Then

\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])<\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n},[g_{0}]).

As a consequence, if
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
),
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained.

When
P_{g}^{s}
has kernel test-function computations for
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
become more complicated to perform, and this is the reason why the assumptions of Theorem
1.9
are stronger than those of Theorem
1.8
. Theorem
1.9
is the first existence result for minimisers of
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
for any
k\geq k_{+}
and for a general
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})\neq\{0\}
, and is new even in the case
s=1
.

1.4.4.
Further results

We finally state a few additional consequences of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
. The first one is the following strict monotonicity result for the sequence
(\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g]))_{k}
, which can be seen as a generalised spectral gap for the conformal eigenvalues of
(M,g)
:

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
. Assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). Let
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
with
k<k^{\prime}
and such that
\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])\Lambda^{s}_{k^{\prime}}(M,[g])\not=0
. Then
\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])<\Lambda^{s}_{k^{\prime}}(M,[g])
.

Together with Corollary
1.3
, this result shows that the map
k\mapsto\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])
is strictly increasing in
\{1\leq k\leq k_{-}\}
and in
\{k\geq k_{+}\}
. Note that we do not require the invariants
\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])
to be attained in Theorem
1.10
.

The second result addresses the case of coercive operators. Let
(M,g)
a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
. If
\lambda_{1}(g)>0
(or, equivalently, if
k_{+}=1
) we will say that
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive; in this case
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
and (
1.3
) is trivially satisfied. If
s\geq 1
and
n\geq 2s+5
we denote by
k_{n}\in\{3,\cdots,n+1\}
the smallest integer
k
for which
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
is attained, and whose existence follows from Theorem
1.7
. As a surprising consequence of Theorem
1.7
and of the recursive approach enabled by Theorem
1.2
we prove that, in large dimensions,
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained for any
k<k_{n}
if
(M,[g])
has nontrivial geometry and
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive:

Theorem 1.11
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
M
. Assume that
k_{+}=1
, that
n\geq 2s+9
and that
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat. Then
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained for every
k\leq k_{n}-1
.

Note that, under the assumptions of Theorem
1.11
, it already followed from Theorem
1.8
that
\Lambda_{1}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained. Since
k_{n}\geq 3
, a consequence of Theorem
1.11
is in particular that, if
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive,
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat and
n\geq 2s+9
,
\Lambda_{1}^{s}(M,[g])
and
\Lambda_{2}^{s}(M,[g])
are both attained. It has been recently proven in
[
54
]
that, when
s=1
, Theorem
1.11
is sharp for
k=2
: the result of
[
54
]
indeed shows that when
3\leq n\leq 10
there is an open neighbourhood
U
of the round metric
g_{0}
in
\mathbb{S}^{n}
in a strong topology such that for
g\in U
,
\Lambda_{2}^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{n},[g])
is never attained and satisfies
\Lambda_{2}^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{n},[g])^{\frac{n}{2}}=\Lambda_{1}^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{n},[g])^{\frac{n}{2}}+\Lambda_{1}^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2}}
. The result of
[
54
]
is to this day the only non-existence result for extremals of
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
when
(M,g)
is not conformally equivalent to
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
.

1.5.
Strategy of proof and outline of the paper

We introduce in Section
2
several technical results that are used throughout the paper: we define the weighted function spaces for our analysis, introduce the GJMS operators and state some of their properties. In Proposition
2.2
we provide conditions under which (
1.3
) is satisfied. We also prove a key lemma that is repeatedly applied in the paper, Lemma
2.2
, that describes the asymptotic behaviour of sequences of generalised eigenfunctions. Since
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
may be nontrivial, sequences of generalised eigenfunctions need not be bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. Lemma
2.2
describes their possible behaviors, and assumption (
1.3
) crucially comes into play to treat the
H^{s}(M)
-unbounded case.

In Section
3
we define the generalised eigenvalues and prove their main properties. We investigate the renormalised eigenvalue functionals

\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}\mapsto\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{p}},

where
\mathcal{A}_{p}=\{\beta\in L^{p}(M)\setminus\{0\};\beta\geq 0\}
is the set of admissible weights and
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
. These functionals are dilation-invariant and can thus be investigated over
\{\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p},\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}\geq 1\}
, which is a complete metric space for the
L^{p}
distance. The geometric problem (
1.2
) corresponds to
p=\frac{n}{2s}
, but we also investigate the subcritical setting
p>\frac{n}{2s}
. The main challenge that we face – in particular when
P_{g}^{s}
is not coercive – is to define generalised eigenvalues when
\beta
may vanish on a set of positive measure. We prove that for
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
all but a finite number of generalised eigenvalues exist (Propositions
3.1
and
3.4
) and investigate their continuity properties (Propositions
3.2
,
3.3
and
3.5
). All these results rely on (
1.3
). Proposition
3.5
in particular proves the equivalence of (
1.2
) and (
1.4
), which justifies our choice to study the new variational problem (
1.4
).

In Section
4
we develop a variational theory for generalised eigenvalues which is of independent interest. The renormalised eigenvalue functionals are neither convex nor differentiable in general: we therefore develop and
ad hoc
analysis based on so-called
non-negative variations
, which first appeared in dimension 2 in
[
52
,
51
]
. Precisely, in Proposition
4.1
below we compute right derivatives at
0
of

t\mapsto\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta+tb)

for non-negative variations
b\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
, so that
\beta+tb\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. This allows us, in Proposition
4.2
below, to show that
if

\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
is a local extrema of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
there is a family of generalised eigenfunctions
(v_{1},\cdots,v_{m})
associated to
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
such that

(1.6)

\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}^{2}=\beta^{p-1}\quad\text{ a.e. in }M.

Equation (
1.6
) is the Euler-Lagrange equation associated to local extrema of
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}\mapsto\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
and we crucially use it throughout the paper. An important byproduct of our analysis is that we are able to bound the number
m
in (
1.6
) solely in terms of the index of the eigenvalue: this was observed in
[
47
]
and generalises previous results for
k=1,2
in
[
1
,
24
]
. A novelty of our approach is also that (
1.6
) holds regardless of the measure of the set
\{\beta=0\}
: this is a notable improvement with respect to previous results in
[
1
,
24
]
1
1

1

In these references partial variational theories for
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
,
k=1,2
, were established by considering internal variations of the form
t\mapsto\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta(1+t{\varphi}))
for
{\varphi}\in L^{\infty}(M)
and differentiating them at
t=0
. This, however, only proves (
1.6
) in
\{\beta\neq 0\}
, which is not known a priori to be of full measure
. We more generally consider in Section
4
a notion of approximated extremals for
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
, which generalises the classical notion of Palais-Smale sequence for regular functionals. By adapting the approach of
[
52
,
51
]
we use Ekeland’s variational principle to construct in Propositions
4.4
and
4.5
families of almost-optimisers for
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
. We then show that, when
p>\frac{n}{2s}
is fixed, these families of almost-optimisers converge to extremals of
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}\mapsto\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
(Proposition
4.6
). This is another advantage of our new approach: the problems
\beta\mapsto\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
for
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
are a natural family of relaxations of (
1.4
) that fit into the same variational framework and do not require us to penalise
\{\beta=0\}
as was done in
[
24
]
.

In Section
5
we prove Theorem
1.1
. The proof goes through an asymptotic analysis of almost maximisers for
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
constructed in Proposition
4.5
. Possible blow-up of such sequences is easily ruled out by Lemma
2.2
since
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<0
.

Section
6
contains the proof of our main result, Theorem
1.2
. The proof goes again through an asymptotic analysis of a suitable minimising sequence: for
p>\frac{n}{2s}
we consider the minimiser
\beta_{p}
of
\beta\mapsto\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
given by Proposition
4.6
, normalised so that
\|\beta_{p}\|_{p}=1
. The family
(\beta_{p})_{p>\frac{n}{2s}}
satisfies (
1.6
) and provides, as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, a well-behaved approximating sequence for
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
,
whose asymptotic behavior we investigate as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Let
v_{p}=(v_{1}^{p},\cdots,v_{m}^{p})
be the generalised eigenfunctions given by (
1.6
) and
|v_{p}|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(v_{i}^{p})^{2}
. Then, as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, we have
\beta_{p}=|v_{p}|^{\frac{2}{p-1}}
and
v_{p}
satisfies

P_{g}^{s}v_{p}=\lambda_{p}|v_{p}|^{\frac{2}{p-1}}v_{p}\quad\text{ in }M,\text{ and }\quad\int_{M}|v_{p}|^{\frac{2p}{p-1}}dv_{g}=1,

where
\lambda_{p}=\lambda_{k}(\beta_{p})
. This system is asymptotically critical since
\frac{2}{p-1}\to\frac{4s}{n-2s}
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
: since in addition
\lambda_{p}>0
(because
k\geq k_{+}
) bubbling phenomena for
v_{p}
, and thus for
\beta_{p}
, may occur as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. We perform an asymptotic analysis of
v_{p}
and show that if it blows-up then
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\geq X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
, where
X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is as in (
1.5
), which contradicts the assumptions of Theorem
1.2
. Since
k\geq k_{+}
is any integer, there can be arbitrarily many bubbles involved and a general multi-bubble analysis can be difficult to manage. We bypass this difficulty by proving a one-bubble extraction result that splits the energy in
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
into a weak-limit part in
(M,g)
(that may be equal to zero) and a bubbling part in
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
. This is proven in Theorem
6.2
below and is the core of the analysis in the proof of Theorem
1.2
; it is also one of the originalities of our approach. The crucial point here is that the energy of the weak limit part, if non-zero, is given by some
\Lambda_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])
, with
\ell_{0}\leq k-1
,
which is itself attained
. This justifies the definition of
X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
in (
1.5
). We then conclude the proof by recursively applying our bubble-extraction result to the bubbling part itself in
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
. Again, a challenge in the proof of Theorem
6.2
is the possible kernel of
P_{g}^{s}
, and the unboundedness of
v_{p}
in
H^{s}(M)
that may ensue. This situation may only occur when
\beta_{p}\rightharpoonup 0
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
and we analyse it separately. If
w_{i}^{p}
denotes the orthogonal projection of
v_{i}^{p}
onto
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})^{\perp}
, our main observation is that
(w_{1}^{p},\cdots,w_{m}^{p})
provides an asymptotically better competitor for
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
: we therefore perform an asymptotic analysis on
w_{i}^{p}
in this case.

Section
7
focuses on the case where
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies the maximum principle. We prove the simplicity of the first generalised eigenfunction in this case (Proposition
7.1
) and, if
\beta
is an extremal of
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}\mapsto\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
, we provide conditions ensuring that some generalised eigenspaces are one-dimensional (Proposition
7.2
). These results strongly rely on the Euler-Lagrange relation (
1.6
). As a consequence of these results and of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
we prove Corollaries
1.4
and
1.5
and
Theorems
1.6
and Theorem
1.7
.

Section
8
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.10
. We show that the strict monotonicity of the sequence
(\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g]))_{k}
follows from (
1.6
) and from a recursive argument that relies on Theorem
1.2
.

Finally, Section
9
is devoted to the proof of Theorems
1.8
,
1.9
and
1.11
. In each case we prove that the strict inequality in the statement of Theorem
1.2
holds. We do this for arbitrary values of
k
and
k_{+}
by developing new test-functions arguments. We adapt the test-function approaches developed for the prescribed
Q
-curvature equation (see for instance
[
40
]
) to the setting of a generalised eigenvalue of arbitrary index.

2.
Technical results

2.1.
Function spaces

We now introduce the function spaces that we will need to define eigenvalues of generalised metrics. Let
p\geq 1
. If
\beta\in L^{p}(M)\backslash\{0\}
satisfies
\beta\geq 0
a.e we let

\displaystyle L^{2}(M,\beta dv_{g})

\displaystyle=\Big\{v:M\to\mathbb{R}\text{ measurable such that }\int_{M}v^{2}\beta dv_{g}<+\infty\Big\},

that we endow with the semi-norm

\|v\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}}=\left(\int_{M}v^{2}\beta dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

We now set

(2.1)

K_{\beta}=\Big\{v:M\to\mathbb{R}\text{ measurable such that }\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}v=0\text{ a.e. in }M\Big\}

Then
L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
, defined by

\displaystyle L^{2}_{\beta}(M)=L^{2}(M,\beta dv_{g})/K_{\beta}

and endowed with the scalar product
(v,w)_{L^{2}_{\beta}(M)}=\int_{M}vw\beta dv_{g}
associated to
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}}
, defines a Hilbert space. Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
be a non-zero integer such that
n>2s
. We will let throughout this paper

\Delta_{g}=-\text{div}_{g}(\nabla\cdot)

be the Laplace-Beltrami operator. Let
H^{s}(M)
be the closure of
C^{\infty}(M)
for the norm

(2.2)

\|u\|_{H^{s}}=\left(\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}u\big|^{2}dv_{g}+\int_{M}u^{2}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad u\in C^{\infty}(M)

where we have let

\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}u=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{g}^{m}u&\text{ if }s=2m\text{ is even},\\
&\nabla\Delta_{g}^{m}u&\text{ if }s=2m+1\text{ is odd}.\end{aligned}\right.

It is well-known that
H^{s}(M)
defines a Hilbert space whose norm is given by the following scalar product:

(2.3)

\langle u,v\rangle_{H^{s}}=\langle u,\Delta_{g}^{s}v\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle u,v\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Equivalently,
H^{s}(M)
is also the completion of
C^{\infty}(M)
with respect to the norm

\|u\|_{W^{s,2}}=\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{s}\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}u\big|^{2}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad u\in C^{\infty}(M),

and the latter is equivalent to (
2.2
). For simplicity we will always work with (
2.2
). For
p\geq 1
we define the following metric space:

(2.4)

\mathcal{A}_{p}=\{\beta\in L^{p}(M)\setminus\{0\},\beta\geq 0\text{ a.e }\},

endowed with the distance associated to the
L^{p}
norm and the associated topology. Sobolev embeddings ensure that
H^{s}(M)
continuously embeds into
L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}(M)
when
n>2s
. As a consequence, if
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
,
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
and
v\in H^{s}(M)
, we have
\beta v^{2}\in L^{1}(M)
by Hölder’s inequality. For
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
we may thus define

\|u\|_{H^{s}_{\beta}}=\left(\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}u\big|^{2}dv_{g}+\int_{M}\beta u^{2}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{ for }u\in H^{s}(M).

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
2s<n
, and assume that
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
.

(1)

For all
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
, there is an open neighborhood
U_{\beta}
of
\beta
in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
and a constant
C_{\beta}=C(n,s,p,g,\beta)>0
such that

\forall\tilde{\beta}\in U_{\beta},C_{\beta}^{-1}\|u\|_{H^{s}}\leq\|u\|_{H^{s}_{\tilde{\beta}}}\leq C_{\beta}\|u\|_{H^{s}}\quad\text{ for all }u\in H^{s}(M).

(2)

If
(v_{i})_{\geq 0}
is a bounded sequence in
H^{s}(M)
and
\beta_{i}\to\beta
in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
then, up to a subsequence,
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
weakly converges to
v
in
H^{s}(M)
and

(2.5)

\int_{M}(v_{i}-v)^{q}\beta_{i}dv_{g}\to 0\quad\text{ as }i\to+\infty.

for any
1\leq q\leq 2
.

(3)

Let
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
such that
\beta>_{a.e}0
. If
T
is a differential operator of order at most
2s-1
with smooth coefficients on
M
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
, then there is an open neighborhood
V_{\beta}
of
\beta
in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
and a constant
K=K(p,T,\epsilon_{0},\beta)
such that for all
\tilde{\beta}\in V_{\beta}
,

\forall v\in H^{s}(M),\left|\int_{M}vTvdv_{g}\right|\leq\epsilon_{0}\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v\big|^{2}dv_{g}+K\int_{M}\tilde{\beta}v^{2}dv_{g}

If
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
for some
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
, Proposition
2.1
shows that
u\mapsto\|u\|_{H^{s}_{\beta}}
is a norm on
H^{s}(M)
. We can thus define a space
H^{s}_{\beta}(M)
as the closure of
C^{\infty}(M)
for
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}_{\beta}}
and Proposition
2.1
show that
H^{s}(M)=H^{s}_{\beta}(M)
and that the norms
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}_{\beta}}
are equivalent on
H^{s}(M)
. Clearly,
H^{s}_{\beta}(M)
is a Hilbert space for the scalar product

\langle\cdot,\rangle_{H^{s}_{\beta}}=\langle\cdot,\Delta_{g}^{s}\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}_{\beta}}.

Proof.

We first prove (2). Let
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a bounded sequence in
H^{s}(M)
and
\beta_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
such that
\beta_{i}\to\beta
in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
. Up to taking a subsequence,
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
weakly converges to some function
v
in
H^{s}(M)
. Let
R>0
. Then, if
1\leq q\leq 2
is fixed,

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{i}(v_{i}-v)^{q}=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\beta_{i}-\beta\right)(v_{i}-v)^{q}dv_{g}+\int_{\{\beta\leq R\}}\beta(v_{i}-v)^{q}dv_{g}

\displaystyle+\int_{\{\beta>R\}}\beta(v_{i}-v)^{q}dv_{g}.

Up to passing to a subsequence again
v_{i}\to v
in
L^{q}(M)
, so the second integral in the right-hand side converges to
0
. The first and last integrals are estimated with the continuous embedding
H^{s}(M)\subset L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}(M)
. Since
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}
is bounded we obtain

\int_{M}\left(\beta_{i}-\beta\right)(v_{i}-v)^{q}dv_{g}\leq C\Big(\int_{M}(\beta-\beta_{i})^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}\Big)^{\frac{2s}{n}}\to 0

as
i\to+\infty
since
\beta_{i}\to\beta
in
L^{p}(M)
and
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
. As a consequence,

\limsup_{i\to+\infty}\int_{M}\beta(v_{i}-v)^{q}dv_{g}\leq C\Big(\int_{\{\beta>R\}}\beta^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}\Big)^{\frac{2s}{n}}

for some
C>0
independent of
R
. Letting
R\to+\infty
proves (
2.5
).

We now prove (1). Let
\beta,\tilde{\beta}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
be such that
\|\tilde{\beta}-\beta\|_{L^{p}}\leq\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}
, then for
v\in H^{s}(M)

\int_{M}\tilde{\beta}v^{2}\leq\|\tilde{\beta}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}\|v\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}}^{2}\leq C\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}\|v\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}}^{2}\leq C\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}\|v\|_{H^{s}}^{2}

where we used the classical Sobolev embedding
H^{s}(M)\subset L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}(M)
. Therefore,

\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\tilde{\beta}}}^{2}\leq\left(1+C\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}\right)\|v\|_{H^{s}}^{2}

Let’s prove the reverse inequality. We assume by contradiction that there is
\beta_{i}\to\beta
and a sequence
v_{i}\in H^{s}(M)
such that
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}=1
and
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}_{\beta_{i}}}\to 0
as
i\to+\infty
. Since
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
, up to the extraction of a subsequence it converges to
v
weakly in
H^{s}(M)
and strongly in
L^{2}(M)
. The assumption
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}_{\beta_{i}}}\to 0
implies that

\|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\to 0,

which in turn implies that
\int_{M}v_{i}^{2}dv_{g}\to 1
and hence that
\int_{M}v^{2}dv_{g}=1
. This implies that
v
is a non-zero constant function. Notice that by (
2.5
), we also have that up to a subsequence,

\int_{M}(v-v_{i})^{2}\beta_{i}dv_{g}\to 0

as
i\to+\infty
, which implies that
\int_{M}\beta v^{2}dv_{g}=0
, and hence that
\int_{M}\beta dv_{g}=0
, which is a contradiction.

We finally prove (3) by contradiction. Assume that there is a sequence
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
such that
\beta_{i}\to\beta
in
L^{p}(M)
and a sequence
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in
H^{s}(M)
such that

(2.6)

\left|\int_{M}v_{i}Tv_{i}dv_{g}\right|>\epsilon_{0}\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}+i\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}^{2}dv_{g}.

up to dividing
v_{i}
by
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}
, we can assume that
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}=1
. Then, up to taking a subsequence
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
weakly converges to
v
in
H^{s}(M)
and by compact Sobolev embeddings and by (2),

\int_{M}v_{i}Tv_{i}dv_{g}\to\int_{M}vTvdv_{g}\text{ and }\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}^{2}dv_{g}\to\int_{M}\beta v^{2}dv_{g}

as
i\to+\infty
. Passing to the limit in (
2.6
), we obtain that
\int_{M}\beta v^{2}=0
. Since
\beta>_{a.e}0
, this means that
v=0
. Then by (
2.6
) and compact Sobolev embeddings,

\epsilon_{0}\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}\leq\int_{M}v_{i}Tv_{i}dv_{g}\to 0

as
i\to+\infty
, so that
v_{i}
converges strongly to
0
in
H^{s}(M)
, contradicting
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}=1
.
∎

2.2.
Generalities on GJMS operators

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
, that is compact without boundary, and let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}=\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
with
s<\frac{n}{2}
. In the following we will denote by
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
M
. It is a differential operator in
M
, introduced in Graham-Jenne-Sparkling-Mason
[
19
]
, which is conformally covariant in the following sense: if
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
u>0
and
\hat{g}=u^{\frac{4}{n-2s}}g
is a metric conformal to
g
,

(2.7)

P_{\hat{g}}^{s}(f)=u^{-\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}P_{g}^{s}(uf)\quad\text{ for all }f\in C^{\infty}(M).

The operators
P_{g}^{s}
are self-adjoint in
L^{2}(M)
(see e.g.
[
20
]
) and their leading-order term is the
s
-th power of
\Delta_{g}
: precisely (see again
[
19
]
) we have

(2.8)

P_{g}^{s}=\Delta_{g}^{s}+A_{g}^{s}

where
\Delta_{g}=-\text{div}_{g}(\nabla\cdot)
and where
A_{g}^{s}
is a differential operator with smooth coefficients of order at most
2s-1
which ensures the conformal invariance. If
s=1
and
n\geq 3
,
P_{g}^{1}
is the celebrated conformal Laplacian:

P_{g}^{1}=\Delta_{g}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}S_{g},

where
S_{g}
is the scalar curvature of
(M,g)
. If
s=2
and
n\geq 5
,
P_{g}^{2}
is the Paneitz-Branson operator
[
6
,
45
]
, whose expression is

P_{g}^{2}u=\Delta_{g}^{2}u-\text{div}_{g}\left[\left(a_{n}S_{g}g-\frac{4}{n-2}\text{Ric}_{g}\right)(\nabla u,\cdot)\right]+\frac{n-4}{2}Q_{g}u

for
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Here we have let
a_{n}=\frac{(n-2)^{2}+4}{2(n-1)(n-2)}
,
\text{Ric}_{g}
denotes the Ricci tensor of
(M,g)
and
Q_{g}
is the so-called
Q
-curvature whose expression is given by

Q_{g}=\frac{1}{2(n-1)}\Delta_{g}S_{g}+\frac{n^{3}-4n^{2}+16n-16}{8(n-1)^{2}(n-2)^{2}}S_{g}^{2}-\frac{2}{(n-2)^{2}}|\text{Ric}_{g}|_{g}^{2}.

An expression of
P_{g}^{3}
can be found in
[
29
]
, but explicit formulae for
P_{g}^{s},s\geq 4
, for a general metric
g
do not exist, since the lower-order terms in (
2.8
) grow exponentially complex in terms of the geometry of
(M,g)
. Recursive formulae have however been obtained in
[
29
]
. An explicit formula exists if we assume that
(M,g)
is an Einstein manifold: it is indeed proven in Fefferman-Graham
[
17
, Proposition 7.9]
that if
\text{Ric}(g)=\frac{S}{n}g
, then

(2.9)

P_{g}^{s}=\prod_{j=1}^{s}\Big(\Delta_{g}+\frac{(n+2j-2)(n-2j)}{4n(n-1)}S\Big).

2.3.
Unique continuation for GJMS operators

As mentioned in the introduction we will assume throughout this paper that the operator
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies the unique continuation property (
1.3
). We recall the definition of the kernel of
P_{g}^{s}
:

{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\big\{v\in H^{s}(M),P_{g}^{s}v=0\big\}.

By standard elliptic theory, any
v\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
is smooth in
M
. As will be explained in Section
3
(see e.g. Proposition
3.1
below) assumption (
1.3
) is crucial in order to be able to properly define the generalised eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
when
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)\backslash\{0\}
is only a nonnegative function. Assumption (
1.3
) is obviously true when
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
, which happens for instance if
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive (see e.g. (
4.31
) below). When
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})\neq\{0\}
, (
1.3
) ensures that non-zero functions of
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
vanish on a set of measure zero in
M
. The following result lists several important situations where
P_{g}^{s}
may have kernel and where (
1.3
) holds true:

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
(M,g)
be a smooth closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
and let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, with
2s<n
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator introduced in subsection
2.2
. Assume that one of the following assumptions is satisfied:

•

s=1

•

s\geq 1
and
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}

•

s\geq 1
and
M
and
g
are analytic

•

s\geq 1
and
(M,g)
is locally conformally Einstein.

Then
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
).

Remark that the locally conformally Einstein case covers the case where
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat.

Proof.

If
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
(
1.3
) is obvious. If
s=1
,
P_{g}^{1}=\Delta_{g}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}S_{g}
is the conformal laplacian and (
1.3
) follows from the unique continuation property for second-order operators which is proven for instance in Hardt-Simon
[
26
, Theorem 1.10]
. If
s\geq 1
and
M
and
g
are analytic this follows from the results in
[
35
]
.

We finally assume that
s\geq 1
and
(M,g)
is locally conformally Einstein. We let
\varphi\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
and assume that
\{\varphi=0\}
has positive measure in
M
. Since
M
is compact there exists
x\in\{\varphi=0\}
such that
\{\varphi=0\}\cap B_{g}(x,\delta)
has positive measure for all
\delta>0
. Let
U
be a neighbourhood of
x
such that
\{\varphi=0\}\cap U
has positive measure and such that
g
is conformal to an Einstein metric in
U
:
g=u^{\frac{4}{n-2s}}\tilde{g}
in
U
for some positive
u\in C^{\infty}(U)
, where
\tilde{g}
is Einstein. By the conformal covariance (
2.7
) of
P_{g}^{s}
,
v=u\varphi
satisfies
P_{\tilde{g}}^{s}v=0
in
U
and
|U\cap\{v=0\}|>0
. Since
\tilde{g}
is an Einstein metric,
P_{\tilde{g}}^{s}
is the product of second-order Schrödinger operators by (
2.9
). It is then easily seen that (
1.3
) follows from the next lemma, which we now prove:

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold, let
m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and let
\Omega\subseteq M
be an open set. We set
P_{m}=L_{m}\circ\cdots\circ L_{1}
where for all
1\leq i\leq m
,
L_{i}=\Delta_{g}+T_{i}
and
T_{i}
is a differential operator of order
\leq 1
with smooth coefficients. Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
be such that
P_{m}u=0
in
\Omega
and such that
\{u=0\}
has positive measure. Then
u\equiv 0
in
\Omega
.

Proof.

By a simple covering argument we may assume that
{\Omega}
is a chart domain in
M
, and that the operators
L_{i}
can be written in coordinates as elliptic operators with smooth coefficients in open sets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We prove the result by induction on
m
. For
m=1
the result follows from the unique continuation results in Hardt-Simon
[
26
, Theorem 1.10]
. Let
m\geq 2
and assume that the property holds for
m-1
. Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
be such that
P_{m}u=0
in
\Omega
and
|\{u=0\}|>0
. A standard consequence of the chain rule for Sobolev functions is that
\nabla u=0
a.e. in
\{u=0\}
and thus, iteratively, that
\nabla^{2m-2}u=0
a.e. in
\{u=0\}
. Therefore, setting

v=(L_{m-1}\circ\cdots\circ L_{1})u,

we get that
v
vanishes in
\{u=0\}
, which has positive measure in
\Omega
. The assumption
P_{m}u=0
implies that
L_{m}v=0
in
\Omega
, which implies that
v\equiv 0
in
\Omega
, again by
[
26
, Theorem 1.10]
. Hence
(L_{m-1}\circ\cdots\circ L_{1})u=0
in
\Omega
and the induction assumption applied to
u
shows that
u\equiv 0
in
\Omega
.
∎

∎

Remark 2.1
.

It is likely that (
1.3
) also holds true for any closed manifold
(M,g)
, when
s\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
2s<n
. Indeed, the conformal covariance (
2.7
) of
P_{g}^{s}
shows that the zero sets of kernel elements are conformal invariants of
(M,g)
, and it is enough to prove property (
1.3
) for any fixed metric in
[g]
. Around any point of
M
, and up to a conformal change of
g
, one can use conformal normal coordinates (as introduced in
[
36
]
) where
\det g\equiv 1
. In this coordinate chart
P_{g}^{s}
rewrites as
P_{g}^{s}=\Delta_{g}^{s}+T
where
T
is a differential operator of order
\leq 2s-2
: this is proven for instance in
[
40
, Equation (2.7)]
. Now, in open sets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
there is a well-established unique continuation theory for polyharmonic operators, due to
[
56
]
(see also
[
39
]
). It states that, if
T
is a differential operator with smooth coefficients of order
\leq[\frac{3s}{2}]
, solutions
u
of equations of the form
\Delta_{\xi}^{s}u+Tu=0
in a ball
B
that vanish at infinite order at a point must satisfy
u=0
in
B
. By analogy with these results we may expect that the same unique continuation property will hold true for
P_{g}^{s}
provided the assumptions of
[
39
,
56
]
are satisfied, that is provided
2s-2\leq[\frac{3s}{2}]
, which is equivalent to
s\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. Note however that the analysis in
[
39
,
56
]
is only written for the flat Laplacian and one would have to carefully check that the arguments adapt for general Laplace-Beltrami operators to conclude.

2.4.
Convergence of generalised eigenfunctions

In the whole paper we will need to study the behavior of sequences of functions
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in
H^{s}(M)
satisfying eigenfunction-type equations like

P_{g}^{s}v_{i}=\lambda_{i}\beta_{i}v_{i}\quad\text{ in }M,

where
P_{g}^{s}
is the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
M
(see subsection
2.2
),
(\lambda_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is a sequence of real numbers and
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
a sequence of functions that are uniformly bounded in some
L^{p}(M)
space. We prove in this subsection two technical results that will be used throughout the paper.

The first result addresses the
a priori
boundedness of
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in
H^{s}(M)
. This question is easily answered when
P_{g}^{s}
has no kernel but the problem becomes more subtle in the presence of a non-trivial kernel. We recall that

{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\big\{v\in H^{s}(M),P_{g}^{s}v=0\big\}.

By standard elliptic theory, any
v\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
is smooth in
M
. In the following
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})^{\perp}
will denote the orthogonal of
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
with respect to the
H^{s}(M)
-scalar product defined by (
2.3
).

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
2s<n
,
(M,g)
be a closed manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
and
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
on
(M,g)
. We assume that (
1.3
) holds true. Let
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence in
H^{s}(M)
,
(\lambda_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of real numbers,
(p_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of positive numbers satisfying
p_{i}\geq\frac{n}{2s}
for all
i\geq 0
and, for any
i\geq 0
, let
\beta_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{p_{i}}
be a nonnegative nonzero function. Assume that we have, for all
i\geq 0
,

(2.10)

P_{g}^{s}v_{i}=\lambda_{i}\beta_{i}v_{i}\quad\text{ in }M,

\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}^{2}=1
,
\int_{M}\beta_{i}^{p_{i}}dv_{g}=1
,
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
and
\limsup_{i\to+\infty}|\lambda_{i}|<+\infty
. For all
i\geq 0
we decompose
v_{i}
as

v_{i}=k_{i}+w_{i}

where
k_{i}\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
and
w_{i}\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})^{\perp}
. Then

(1)

(w_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)

(2)

There is
c>0
such that, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\left|\lambda_{i}\right|\geq c
and
\|w_{i}\|_{H^{s}}\geq c

(3)

If
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}\to+\infty
as
i\to+\infty
then,
p_{i}\to\frac{n}{2s}
from above,
\lambda_{i}\geq c
for
i
large enough,
(w_{i})_{i\geq 0}
weakly converges to
0
in
H^{s}(M)
,
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
weakly converges to
0
in
L^{q}(M)
for all
q\leq\frac{n}{2s}
and we have up to the extraction of a subsequence that

v_{i}=\gamma_{i}\big(K+o(1)\big)\quad\text{ as }i\to+\infty,

where
K
is a non-zero element of
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
, where
\gamma_{i}=\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}
and where
o(1)
denotes a sequence that strongly converges to
0
in
H^{s}(M)
.

Proof.

A simple observation, that easily follows from standard elliptic theory, is that there exists
C>0
such that, for any
u\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})^{\perp}
,

(2.11)

\|u\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\|P_{g}^{s}u\|_{H^{-s}},

where
H^{-s}(M)
denotes the dual space of
(H^{s}(M),\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}})
and
\|\cdot\|_{H^{-s}}
is the norm canonically defined on it. Since
k_{i}\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
,
w_{i}\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})^{\perp}
satisfies

(2.12)

P_{g}^{s}w_{i}=\lambda_{i}\beta_{i}v_{i}\quad\text{ in }M

for all
i\geq 0
, so that by (
2.11
) we have

(2.13)

\|w_{i}\|_{H^{s}}\leq|\lambda_{i}|\|\beta_{i}v_{i}\|_{H^{-s}}.

Since
\beta_{i}\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
, Sobolev’s inequality shows that, for all
i\geq 0
,
\lambda_{i}\beta_{i}v_{i}\in L^{\frac{2n}{n+2s}}(M)\subset H^{-s}(M)
and that
\|\beta_{i}v_{i}\|_{H^{-s}}\leq C\|\beta_{i}v_{i}\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n+2s}}}
. Hölder’s inequality and the assumptions of the lemma then show that, for any
i\geq 0
,

\|\beta_{i}v_{i}\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n+2s}}}^{\frac{2n}{n+2s}}\leq\left(\int_{M}\beta_{i}^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n+2s}}\left(\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}^{2}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n}{n+2s}}\leq C.

Going back to (
2.13
), and since
(\lambda_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded, proves that
(w_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. This proves the first bullet of the lemma.

We now claim that we have for some positive constant
c>0

(2.14)

\|w_{i}\|_{H^{s}}\geq c.

We first integrate (
2.10
) against
k_{i}
. Since
k_{i}\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
and
P_{g}^{s}
is self-adjoint we have

0=\int_{M}(P_{g}^{s}k_{i})v_{i}dv_{g}=\int_{M}(P_{g}^{s}v_{i})k_{i}dv_{g}=\lambda_{i}\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}k_{i}dv_{g},

from which we deduce that

(2.15)

\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}k_{i}dv_{g}=0

for all
i\geq 0
since
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
by assumption. Hence, since
w_{i}=v_{i}-k_{i}
, we have

(2.16)

\int_{M}\beta_{i}w_{i}^{2}dv_{g}=\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}^{2}dv_{g}+\int_{M}\beta_{i}k_{i}^{2}dv_{g}\geq 1

by assumption and since
\beta_{i}\geq 0
. By Hölder and Sobolev inequalities and since
p_{i}\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and
\int_{M}\beta_{i}^{p_{i}}dv_{g}=1
this proves (
2.14
). A straightforward consequence of (
2.13
) is also that, up reducing
c
,
|\lambda_{i}|\geq c
. This proves the second bullet of the lemma.

For the last part of the Lemma we assume,
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}\to+\infty
as
i\to+\infty
. We let, for
i\geq 0
,
V_{i}=\frac{v_{i}}{\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}},K_{i}=\frac{k_{i}}{\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}}
and
W_{i}=\frac{w_{i}}{\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}}
. Since
(w_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
,
W_{i}\to 0
strongly in
H^{s}(M)
. Hence
\|K_{i}\|_{H^{s}}=1+o(1)
as
i\to+\infty
. The sequence
(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
and belongs to
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
which is finite-dimensional so, up to the extraction of a subsequence, it strongly converges towards
K\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
satisfying
\|K\|_{H^{s}}=1
. The previous arguments show that
V_{i}=K+o(1)
in
H^{s}(M)
as
i\to+\infty
.
Coming back to (
2.15
) and dividing by
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}
then shows, since
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}(M)
and by Sobolev’s inequality, that

\int_{M}\beta_{i}K^{2}dv_{g}=o(1)

as
i\to+\infty
. We let
\beta
denote the weak limit of
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
, up to taking a subsequence. The latter shows that
\int_{M}\beta K^{2}dv_{g}=0
. Since
K\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
and
\|K\|_{H^{s}}=1
it is nonzero a.e. by (
1.3
), and hence
\beta=0
. We now prove that

(2.17)

w_{i}\rightharpoonup 0\quad\text{ in }H^{s}(M)

as
i\to+\infty
, up to taking a subsequence. Since
(w_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
we can let
w
be its weak limit, up to taking a subsequence. Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Testing (
2.12
) against
\varphi
yields

\int_{M}P_{g}^{s}w\varphi dv_{g}+o(1)=\lambda_{i}\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}\varphi dv_{g}.

Since
(\lambda_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded we have, by Hölder’s inequality,

\left|\lambda_{i}\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}\varphi dv_{g}\right|^{2}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{M}\beta_{i}dv_{g}\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}^{2}dv_{g}=o(1)

as
i\to+\infty
, since
\beta_{i}\rightharpoonup 0
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
. Hence
P_{g}^{s}w=0
and thus, since
w\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})^{\perp}
,
w=0
, which proves (
2.17
). By uniqueness of the subsequential weak limits of
(w_{i})_{i\geq 0}
and
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, we obtain that
(w_{i})_{i\geq 0}
weakly converges to
0
in
H^{s}(M)
and
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
weakly converges to
0
in
L^{q}(M)
for all
q\leq\frac{n}{2s}
.

We now prove that
p_{i}\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Assume by contradiction that, up to taking a subsequence,
\lim_{i\to+\infty}p_{i}>\frac{n}{2s}
and let
p\in(\frac{n}{2s},\lim_{i\to+\infty}p_{i})
. Then
\frac{2p}{p-1}<\frac{2n}{n-2s}
and by (
2.17
) and Sobolev’s inequality,
w_{i}\to 0
in
L^{\frac{2p}{p-1}}(M)
. Hence,

\int_{M}\beta_{i}w_{i}^{2}dv_{g}\leq\|w_{i}\|_{L^{\frac{2p}{p-1}}}^{2}\|\beta_{i}\|_{L^{p_{i}}}=o(1),

which contradicts (
2.16
).

To conclude the proof of the Lemma it remains to show that if
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is not bounded in
H^{s}(M)
, then
\lambda_{i}\geq c
for
i
large enough (recall that
|\lambda_{i}|\geq c
by (2)). We proceed by contradiction and assume that, up to the extraction of a subsequence,
\lambda_{i}\leq 0
for all
i\geq 0
. Integrating (
2.10
) against
w_{i}
and using (
2.8
) and (
2.17
) we then obtain

\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}w_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}+o(1)=\lambda_{i}\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}w_{i}dv_{g}\leq 0,

where we used (
2.15
) and (
2.16
) to write that

\int_{M}\beta_{i}v_{i}w_{i}dv_{g}=\int_{M}\beta_{i}w_{i}^{2}dv_{g}\geq 1.

This gives in the end

\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}w_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}=o(1),

which, by (
2.17
), is a contradiction with (
2.14
). This concludes the proof of Lemma
2.2
.
∎

The second result that we prove deals with the possible blow-up behavior of
H^{s}(M)
-bounded sequences
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
satisfying (
2.10
). Before stating it we introduce some notations. We let
K_{n,s}
be the optimal constant for Sobolev’s inequality in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is given by

(2.18)

\frac{1}{K_{n,s}^{2}}=\inf_{u\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big|\Delta_{\xi}^{\frac{s}{2}}u\big|^{2}\,dv_{\xi}}{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}\,dv_{\xi}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}},

where
\Delta_{\xi}=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}^{2}
is the nonnegative Euclidean laplacian. Let
({\beta}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of nonnegative functions that is uniformly bounded in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
. We define the
set of concentration points
of
({\beta}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
by

(2.19)

A=\Biggl\{x\in M\,\Big|\,\forall{\delta}>0,\;\limsup_{i\to+\infty}\int_{B_{g}(x,{\delta})}{\beta}_{i}^{\frac{n}{2s}}\,dv_{g}>\frac{1}{2}K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n}{s}}\Biggr\},

where
K_{n,s}
is given by (
2.18
) and where
B_{g}(x,{\delta})
denotes the Riemannian ball for
g
of center
x
and radius
{\delta}
. Since
({\beta}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
,
A
is finite and may be empty.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
2s<n
,
(M,g)
be a closed manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
and
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
on
(M,g)
. Let
({\beta}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of nonnegative functions that is bounded in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
and let
A
be given by (
2.19
). Let
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of functions in
H^{s}(M)
that satisfy, for all
i\geq 0
,

(2.20)

P_{g}^{s}v_{i}={\beta}_{i}v_{i}\quad\text{ in }M.

Assume that
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
, and up to the extraction of a subsequence, let
v
be its weak limit. Then,
v_{i}\to v
strongly in
H^{s}_{loc}(M\backslash A)
as
i\to+\infty
.

Proof.

Up to passing to a subsequence we may assume that
v_{i}
converges to
v
weakly in
H^{s}(M)
and strongly in
H^{s-1}(M)
and that
\beta_{i}
converges weakly to
\beta
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
. By Sobolev embeddings
v_{i}\to v
in
L^{\frac{n}{n-2s}}(M)
, so that passing (
2.20
) to the limit we obtain that
v
satisfies

(2.21)

P_{g}^{s}v={\beta}v\quad\text{ in }M

in a weak sense. Let
x\in M\backslash A
and let
\delta>0
be small enough so that
B_{g}(x,{\delta})\subset M\backslash A
and

(2.22)

\limsup_{i\to+\infty}\int_{B_{g}(x,{\delta})}{\beta}_{i}^{\frac{n}{2s}}\,dv_{g}\leq\frac{1}{2}K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n}{s}}.

Let
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{g}(x,{\delta}))
and let in what follows

w_{i}=\eta(v_{i}-v).

Straightforward computations show that for any
\ell\geq 1
we have, pointwise,

\big|\nabla^{\ell}w_{i}\big|_{g}=\eta\big|\nabla^{\ell}(v_{i}-v_{0})\big|_{g}+O\big(\sum_{1\leq r\leq\ell-1}\big|\nabla^{r}(v_{i}-v)\big|_{g}\big),

so that in particular
w_{i}\to 0
strongly in
H^{s-1}(M)
as
i\to+\infty
, and

(2.23)

P_{g}^{s}w_{i}=\eta P_{g}^{s}(v_{i}-v)+O\big(\sum_{1\leq r\leq 2s-1}\big|\nabla^{r}(v_{i}-v)\big|_{g}\big)\quad\text{ in }M.

Using (
2.20
) and (
2.21
) we have

P_{g}^{s}(v_{i}-v)=\beta_{i}(v_{i}-v)+(\beta_{i}-\beta)v,

so that integrating (
2.23
) against
w_{i}
and using the latter and (
2.8
) gives, since
w_{i}\to 0
in
H^{s-1}(M)
,

(2.24)

\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}w_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}+o(1)=\int_{M}\beta_{i}w_{i}^{2}dv_{g}+\int_{M}\eta(\beta_{i}-\beta)vw_{i}dv_{g}.

Since
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
, (
2.21
) and standard regularity results (see for instance Mazumdar
[
41
, Theorem 5]
) show that
v\in L^{q}(M)
for all
q\geq 1
. Independently, by compact Sobolev’s embeddings,
w_{i}
strongly converges to
0
in
L^{p}(M)
for all
p<\frac{2n}{n-2s}
. Hölder’s inequality then shows that
vw_{i}
strongly converges to
0
in
L^{\frac{n}{n-2s}}(M)
as
i\to+\infty
. Since
\beta_{i}-\beta
is bounded in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
we thus have

(2.25)

\int_{M}\eta(\beta_{i}-\beta)vw_{i}dv_{g}=o(1)

as
i\to+\infty
. We now estimate the first integral in the right-hand side in (
2.24
). We first recall the following Sobolev’s inequality for
P_{g}^{s}
in
M
, proven in Mazumdar
[
41
, Theorem 2]
: for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
B_{\epsilon}>0
such that, for any
u\in H^{s}(M)
,

(2.26)

\left(\int_{M}|u|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}\leq\big(K_{n,s}^{2}+\epsilon\big)\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}u\big|^{2}dv_{g}+B_{\epsilon}\|u\|_{H^{s-1}}^{2},

where
K_{n,s}
is given by (
2.18
). Note that one can also choose
\epsilon=0
as very recently proven in
[
9
]
but we will not need this here. Let
\epsilon=\frac{1}{2}K_{n,s}^{2}
and apply (
2.26
) to
u=w_{i}
: this gives

\left(\int_{M}|w_{i}|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}\leq\frac{3}{2}K_{n,s}^{2}\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}w_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}+o(1)

as
i\to+\infty
. Hölder’s inequality and (
2.22
) then imply that

(2.27)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{i}w_{i}^{2}dv_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{B_{g}(x,{\delta})}{\beta}_{i}^{\frac{n}{2s}}\,dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n}}\|w_{i}\|_{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{4}\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}w_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}+o(1)

as
i\to+\infty
. Combining (
2.27
) with (
2.24
) and (
2.25
) then shows that

\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}w_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}\to 0

as
i\to+\infty
, and hence that
v_{i}\to v
in
B_{g}(x,\delta)
. A simple covering argument then implies that
v_{i}\to v
in
H^{s}_{loc}(M\backslash A)
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

A careful inspection of the proofs of Lemmas
2.2
and
2.3
shows that the results remain true if
P_{g}^{s}
is replaced by any differential operator of the form

P=\Delta_{g}^{s}+A,

where
A
is a self-adjoint operator of order less than
2s-1
. Conformal invariance is not needed here.

3.
Generalised eigenvalues of
P_{g}^{s}

3.1.
Definition of generalised eigenvalues.

In this subsection we define generalised eigenvalues of
P_{g}^{s}
. As mentioned in the introduction, these generalised eigenvalues extend the definition of eigenvalues of
P_{h}^{s}
when
h
is only formally a metric defined by
h=\beta g
for some
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
. Throughout this section
(M,g)
is again a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
and
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
satisfies
2s<n
.
If
{\beta}\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
,
v,w\in H^{s}(M)
, we define

(3.1)

\displaystyle G(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}vP^{s}_{g}vdv_{g}

\displaystyle\Gamma(v,w)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}vP^{s}_{g}wdv_{g}=\int_{M}wP^{s}_{g}vdv_{g}

\displaystyle Q(\beta,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\beta v^{2}dv_{g}

\displaystyle B(\beta,v,w)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\beta vwdv_{g}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{G(v)}{Q(\beta,v)}\text{ when }Q(\beta,v)\neq 0.

Let
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
for some
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
, where
\mathcal{A}_{p}
is as in (
2.4
), and let
V
be a finite-dimensional subspace of
L^{2}(M,\beta dv_{g})
. We let

\dim_{\beta}V=\dim\text{Span}\{\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}v;v\in V\}

which, equivalently, is the dimension of the projection of
V
in
L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
(see definition after (
2.1
)). Note in particular that
\dim_{\beta}V\leq\dim V
. Let
k\geq 0
be an integer. We denote by
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{\beta}(H^{s}(M))
the set of linear subspaces
V
of
H^{s}(M)
such that
\dim_{\beta}(V)=k
. We define the
k
-th generalised eigenvalue of
P_{g}^{s}
as follows:

(3.2)

\lambda_{k}(\beta)=\inf_{V\in\mathcal{G}_{k}^{\beta}(H^{s}(M))}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v),

where
\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)
is as in (
3.1
). We briefly explain the heuristic behind definition (
3.2
). Assume that
\hat{g}=u^{\frac{4}{n-2s}}g
for some positive
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
. If
k\geq 1
, the classical Rayleigh characterisation of eigenvalues shows that

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(P_{\hat{g}}^{s})

\displaystyle=\inf_{V\in\mathcal{G}_{k}(H^{s}(M))}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\int_{M}vP_{\hat{g}}^{s}vdv_{\hat{g}}}{\int_{M}v^{2}dv_{\hat{g}}}

\displaystyle=\inf_{V\in\mathcal{G}_{k}(H^{s}(M))}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}vdv_{g}}{\int_{M}\beta v^{2}dv_{g}},

where we have used the conformal covariance of
P_{g}^{s}
given by (
2.7
) and where we have let
\beta=u^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}
. Definition (
3.2
) thus formally extends the definition of
\lambda_{k}(P_{h}^{s})
when
h
is a singular metric of the form
h=\beta g
, and the assumption
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
ensures that
h
has finite volume. Equivalently, (
3.2
) can be seen as the
k
-th eigenvalue of
P_{g}^{s}
over the weighted space
L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
. The first and second generalised eigenvalues were first defined in
[
1
]
when
s=1
and
P_{g}^{1}
is coercive, and were used in
[
24
]
when
s=1
and
\beta>0
a.e. We provide here a general framework for every value of
s\geq 1
and every
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. If
K_{\beta}
is as in (
2.1
) we will let

K_{s,\beta}=H^{s}(M)\cap K_{\beta}.

We define its orthogonal space with respect to the bilinear form
\Gamma
in (
3.1
) as follows:

H_{\beta}=K_{s,\beta}^{\perp_{\Gamma}}=\{v\in H^{s}(M);\forall w\in K_{s,\beta},\Gamma(v,w)=0\}.

As a first result, we prove that if
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
for some
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
, the assumption
\lambda_{1}(\beta)>-\infty
is enough to guarantee that every
k
-th generalised eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
, for
k\geq 1
, is attained, and to construct generalised eigenfunctions associated to
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*},2s<n
and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
(M,g)
. Let
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
for some
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
. We assume either that
\beta>0
a.e or that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). Then, the following assumptions are equivalent:

(i)

\forall v\in H^{s}(M)\setminus\{0\},Q(\beta,v)=0\Rightarrow G(v)>0

(ii)

There is
\Lambda\geq 0
such that if we let, for all
v\in H^{s}(M)
,

N_{\beta}(v)^{2}=\Lambda Q(\beta,v)+G(v),

N_{\beta}
defines a norm on
H^{s}(M)
which is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}
.

(iii)

\lambda_{1}(\beta)>-\infty
.

If any of these three assumptions is satisfied we have

(3.3)

K_{s,\beta}\oplus H_{\beta}=H^{s}(M),

where this splitting is orthogonal for the scalar product associated to
N_{\beta}
, and we have the following min-max characterization of
\lambda_{k}(\beta)

(3.4)

\lambda_{k}(\beta)=\inf_{V\in\mathcal{G}_{k}(H_{\beta})}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v).

In addition, for any
k\geq 1
, there exists
\varphi\in H_{\beta}
that satisfies

(3.5)

P_{g}^{s}\varphi=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\beta\varphi\quad\text{ in }M,

and the vector space
E_{k}(\beta)
of solutions of (
3.5
) is finite-dimensional. Finally if, for any
k\geq 1
,
(\varphi_{k,\ell}(\beta))_{1\leq\ell\leq\dim E_{k}(\beta)}
is an orthonormal family of
E_{k}(\beta)
with respect to the scalar product associated to
N_{\beta}
then
\big(\varphi_{k,\ell}(\beta)\big)_{k\geq 1,1\leq\ell\leq\dim E_{k}(\beta)}
is a Hilbert basis of
H_{\beta}
for the same scalar product.

Remark 3.1
.

Notice that if
\beta>0
a.e, assumption (i) is obviously satisfied since condition
Q(\beta,v)=0
implies
v=0
.

Functions satisfying (
3.5
) will be called generalised eigenfunctions associated to
\beta
. In the rest of this paper we will often work with families
(\varphi_{i}(\beta))_{i\geq 0}
of generalised eigenfunctions normalised as follows:

B(\beta,\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j})=\int_{M}\beta\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}dv_{g}=\delta_{ij}.

Remark that in the particular case
k=1
definition (
3.2
) rewrites as follows:

\lambda_{1}(\beta)=\inf_{v\in H^{s}(M),\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}v\neq 0}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v).

Proof.

It is clear that
(ii)\Rightarrow(i)
.

Let’s prove
(i)\Rightarrow(iii)
: we assume that
(iii)
does not hold, so that
\lambda_{1}(\beta)=-\infty
. We can thus let
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence in
H^{s}(M)
such that
Q(\beta,v_{i})=1
and
G(v_{i})\to-\infty
as
i\to+\infty
. By Proposition
2.1
we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{M}v_{i}A_{g}^{s}(v_{i})dv_{g}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(Q(\beta,v_{i})+\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=C\left(1+\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}\right)

for some
C>0
that is independent of
i
, where
A_{g}^{s}
is given by (
2.8
). Since by (
2.8
) we have

\int_{M}v_{i}A_{g}^{s}(v_{i})dv_{g}=G(v_{i})-\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g},

the assumption
G(v_{i})\to-\infty
thus implies that we have
