\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}\to+\infty
as
i\to+\infty
and since
Q(\beta,v_{i})=1
, that
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}_{\beta}}^{2}=(1+o(1))\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}
. We can thus let, for
i\geq 0
,
N_{i}=\big(\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\tilde{v}_{i}=\frac{v_{i}}{N_{i}}
. Then
(\tilde{v}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
and by Proposition
2.1
, up to a subsequence, there is a function
\tilde{v}\in H^{s}(M)
such that
\tilde{v}_{i}
converges to
\tilde{v}
weakly in
H^{s}(M)
and strongly both in
H^{s-1}(M)
and in
L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
. Passing to the limit the normalisation assumptions gives
Q(\beta,\tilde{v})=0

so that
\tilde{v}\in K_{\beta}
and

G(\tilde{v})\leq\liminf_{i\to+\infty}G(\tilde{v}_{i})\leq 0.

Independently, for any
i\geq 0
we have, by (
2.8
),

\int_{M}\tilde{v}_{i}A_{g}^{s}(\tilde{v}_{i})dv_{g}=G(\tilde{v}_{i})-1\leq-1

so that
\int_{M}\tilde{v}A_{g}^{s}(\tilde{v})dv_{g}\leq-1
and
\tilde{v}\neq 0
. This implies that
(i)
does not hold.

We now prove that
(iii)\Rightarrow(ii)
. We assume
(iii)
.
Then we immediately have

\forall v\in H^{s}(M)\setminus K_{s,\beta},-\lambda_{1}(\beta)Q(\beta,v)+G(v)\geq 0.

Since
H^{s}(M)\setminus K_{s,\beta}
is dense in
H^{s}(M)
we obtain

(3.6)

\forall v\in H^{s}(M),-\lambda_{1}(\beta)Q(\beta,v)+G(v)\geq 0.

Let
v\in H^{s}(M)
that attains equality in (
3.6
). Let’s first prove that
v\in K_{s,\beta}\Rightarrow v=0
. This is clear if
\beta>_{a.e}0
. Assume now that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
) and that
v\in K_{s,\beta}
, so that
G(v)=0
by (
3.6
). Then we have for any
w\in H^{s}(M)\setminus K_{s,\beta}
, and
t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}

\lambda_{1}(\beta)\leq\frac{G(v+tw)}{Q(\beta,v+tw)}.

Since
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}v=0
a.e and
G(v)=0
the latter becomes

t^{2}\lambda_{1}(\beta)Q(\beta,w)\leq 2t\Gamma(v,w)+t^{2}G(w).

Choosing first
t>0
, dividing by
t
and letting
t\to 0
gives
\Gamma(v,w)\geq 0
. Choosing then
t<0
, dividing by
t
and letting
t\to 0
gives
\Gamma(v,w)\leq 0
.
Thus

\forall w\in H^{s}(M)\setminus K_{s,\beta},\quad\Gamma(v,w)=0.

Again by density of
H^{s}(M)\setminus K_{s,\beta}
this equality passes to the limit and gives

P_{g}^{s}v=0\quad\text{ in }M.

If
v\neq 0
, by (
1.3
) it only vanishes on a set of measure zero. Since we assumed
Q(\beta,v)=0
and since
\beta
is nonnegative,
\beta
is supported in
v^{-1}(\{0\})
, which is impossible since
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}\backslash\{0\}
. This shows that
v=0
. We have thus shown that if equality holds in (
3.6
) for some
v\neq 0
, then
Q(\beta,v)\neq 0
, so that (ii) is satisfied for
\Lambda=-\lambda_{1}(\beta)+1
and
N_{\beta}(v)=(-\lambda_{1}(\beta)+1)Q(\beta,v)+G(v)
. To conclude the proof of (ii) we need to prove the equivalence of the norms
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}
and
N_{\beta}
. First, by proposition
2.1
, there exists
C>0
(depending on
\beta
and
\Lambda
) such that

N_{\beta}\leq C\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}.

To prove the reverse inequality we assume by contradiction that

\inf_{v\in H^{s}(M)\setminus\{0\}}\frac{N_{\beta}(v)}{\|v\|_{H^{s}}}=0

and we let
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of functions in
H^{s}(M)
such that
\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}=1
and
N(v_{i})\to 0

as
i\to+\infty
. Since
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
there is a function
v\in H^{s}(M)
such that
v_{i}
converges to
v
weakly in
H^{s}(M)
and, by Proposition
2.1
, strongly both in
H^{s-1}(M)
and in
L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
, up to a subsequence. By lower semi-continuity of
N_{\beta}
, we have

N_{\beta}(v)\leq\liminf_{i\to+\infty}N_{\beta}(v_{i})=0,

and hence
v=0
since
N_{\beta}
is a norm. Then
v_{i}\to 0
in
H^{s-1}(M)
and thus

1=\|v_{i}\|_{H^{s}}^{2}=\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}+o(1)

as
i\to+\infty
. Since
A_{g}^{s}
is an operator of order at most
2s-1
this leads to

0=\lim_{i\to+\infty}\int_{M}v_{i}A_{g}^{s}v_{i}dv_{g}=\lim_{i\to+\infty}\left(N_{\beta}(v_{i})^{2}-\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v_{i}\big|^{2}dv_{g}-\Lambda Q(\beta,v_{i})\right)\leq-1

which is a contradiction. Thus
N_{\beta}
and
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}
are equivalent and this concludes the proof of
(i)\Leftrightarrow(ii)\Leftrightarrow(iii)
.

Assume now that any of the three properties (i), (ii) or (iii) are satisfied. First, we easily see that
N_{\beta}
is a Hilbert norm on
H^{s}(M)
. By definition of
H_{\beta}
we also immediately deduce (
3.3
) from the following equality:

H_{\beta}=K_{s,\beta}^{\perp_{\Gamma}}=K_{s,\beta}^{\perp_{N_{\beta}}},

where with a slight abuse of notations we denoted by
K_{s,\beta}^{\perp_{N_{\beta}}}
the orthogonal of
K_{s,\beta}
for the bilinear form associated to
N_{\beta}
. We now prove that (
3.4
) holds. We denote by
\tilde{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)
the expression in the right-hand side of (
3.4
). We let
\pi_{\beta}:H^{s}(M)\to H_{\beta}
be the orthonormal projection with respect to the scalar product associated to
N_{\beta}
. For any subspace
V
of
H^{s}(M)
we then have

V\in\mathcal{G}_{k}^{\beta}(H^{s}(M))\Leftrightarrow\pi_{\beta}(V)\in\mathcal{G}_{k}(H_{\beta}).

Observe first that

\forall v\in H^{s}(M),\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)=\mathcal{R}(\beta,\pi_{\beta}(v))+G\big(v-\pi_{\beta}(v)\big)\geq\mathcal{R}(\beta,\pi_{\beta}(v))

where the last inequality follows from (i). Let
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k}^{\beta}(H^{s}(M))
. As a consequence of the latter inequality we have

\max_{v\in V\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)\geq\max_{v\in V\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,\pi_{\beta}(v))=\max_{v\in\pi_{\beta}(V)\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)\geq\tilde{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)

by (
3.4
). Taking the infimum over
V
yields
\lambda_{k}(\beta)\geq\tilde{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)
. The reverse inequality simply follows from the definition of
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
in (
3.2
) since
H_{\beta}\subseteq H^{s}(M)
and any subspace
V
of
H^{s}(M)
which belongs to
\mathcal{G}_{k}(H_{\beta})
is obviously in
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{\beta}(H^{s}(M))
. This proves (
3.4
).

We finally prove (
3.5
). Observe first that (ii) implies that the operator
P_{g}+\Lambda\beta:H^{s}(M)\to H^{-s}(M)
is invertible, where we endow
H^{-s}(M)
with the dual norm induced by
N_{\beta}
(which is equivalent to the dual norm induced by
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}
). We define, for
v\in L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
,

Lv:=\big(P_{g}+\Lambda\beta\big)^{-1}(\beta v).

Remark that
L
is well-defined since for any
v\in K_{\beta}
,
\beta v\equiv 0
by definition. Let
w\in H^{s}(M)
.
Then, by definition of
N_{\beta}
, we have

(3.7)

(Lv,w)_{N_{\beta}}=\int_{M}w(P_{g}^{s}+\Lambda\beta)Lvdv_{g}=\int_{M}\beta vwdv_{g},

where with a slight abuse of notations we have denoted by
(\cdot,\cdot)_{N_{\beta}}
the scalar product in
H^{s}(M)
associated to
N_{\beta}
. By choosing
w\in K_{s,\beta}
, which satisfies
\beta w\equiv 0
, we obtain with (
3.7
) that
\text{Im}L\subseteq H_{\beta}
. Cauchy-Schwarz inequality together with (
3.7
), Proposition
2.1
and point (iii) also shows that
\|Lv\|_{N_{\beta}}\leq C\|v\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}}
for some
C>0
, so that
L:(L^{2}_{\beta}(M),\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}})\to(H_{\beta},N_{\beta})
is continuous. Clearly
L
is self-adjoint because of (
3.7
). Since
(H_{\beta},N_{\beta})
embeds continuously and compactly in
(L^{2}_{\beta}(M),\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}})
by Proposition
2.1
, (2), we can think of
L
as a compact self-adjoint linear operator from
(L^{2}_{\beta}(M),\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{\beta}})
to itself. Equation (
3.5
) then follows from the spectral theorem applied to
L
and from the min-max characterisation of the eigenvalues of
L
in
L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

Notice that in the case where
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})\neq\{0\}
, assumption (
1.3
) is crucial in order to have Proposition
3.1
.

3.2.
Continuity of generalised eigenvalues and existence of eigenfunctions

In this subsection we generalise proposition
3.1
to the case where some generalised eigenvalues are allowed to be equal to
-\infty
. If
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
for some
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
is such that
\lambda_{m-1}(\beta)=-\infty
and
\lambda_{m}(\beta)>-\infty
for some
m\geq 2
we similarly prove the existence of eigenfunctions associated to
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
for
k\geq m
. We prove in addition that the only possible generalised eigenfunctions of
P_{g}^{s}
are associated to well-defined generalised eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
for
k\geq m
. We first prove the following upper semi-continuity result:

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and let
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of functions in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
that converges to some
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
in
L^{p}(M)
. Then

\limsup_{i\to+\infty}\lambda_{k}(\beta_{i})\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)

Proof.

We first assume that
\lambda_{k}(\beta)=-\infty
. Let
\epsilon>0
. By (
3.2
) there is a finite-dimensional subspace
V
of
H^{s}(M)
, satisfying
\dim_{\beta}V=k
, such that

\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)\leq-\frac{1}{\epsilon},

where
\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)
is as in (
3.1
). We claim that

(3.8)

\sup_{v\in V\backslash\{0\},Q(\beta,v)=1}\left|Q(\beta_{i},v)-1\right|=o(1)

as
i\to+\infty
. Let indeed
(v_{j})_{1\leq j\leq m}\subset H^{s}(M)
, for some
m\geq k
, be a generating family of
V
such that

B(\beta,v_{j},v_{\ell})=\delta_{j\ell}\quad\text{ for all }1\leq j,\ell\leq m.

First, since
\beta_{i}\to\beta
in
L^{p}(M)
and
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
we have by Sobolev’s inequality

B(\beta_{i},v_{j},v_{\ell})=\delta_{j\ell}+o(1)

as
i\to+\infty
for all fixed
1\leq j,\ell\leq m
, so that
\dim_{\beta_{i}}V=k
for
i
large enough. If
v\in V\backslash\{0\}
satisfies
Q(\beta,v)=1
we can write it as
v=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}v_{j}
with
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{2}=1
, and (
3.8
) follows. With (
3.8
) we now have, for
i
large enough,

\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{i},v)=\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\},Q(\beta,v)=1}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{i},v)\leq(1+o(1))\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v).

For
i
large enough we thus have

\lambda_{k}(\beta_{i})\leq\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{i},v)\leq-\frac{2}{\epsilon}.

Letting
i\to+\infty
and then
\epsilon\to 0
shows that
\limsup_{i\to+\infty}\lambda_{k}(\beta_{i})=-\infty
.

We now assume that
\lambda_{k}(\beta)>-\infty
. Let
\epsilon>0
. By (
3.2
) there is a finite-dimensional subspace
V
of
H^{s}(M)
, satisfying
\dim_{\beta}V=k
, such that

\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)+\epsilon

With (
3.8
) we again obtain that, for
i
large enough,

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(\beta_{i})\leq\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{i},v)\leq(1+o(1))\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)+\epsilon+o(1).

Letting
i\to+\infty
and then
\epsilon\to 0
completes the proof.
∎

A simple yet useful consequence of Proposition
3.2
is also that, if
\beta_{i}\to\beta
in
L^{p}(M)
for
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and
\liminf_{i\to+\infty}\lambda_{k}(\beta_{i})>-\infty
, then
\lambda_{k}(\beta)>-\infty
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
. For any
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
such that
\beta>_{a.e}0
, there is an open neighborhood
W_{\beta}
of
\beta
in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
such that
\lambda_{k}
is Lipschitz in
W_{\beta}
.

Proof.

Let
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
with
\beta>_{a.e}0
and let
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
be such that
\|\beta_{i}-\beta\|_{L^{p}}\leq\delta
for
i=1,2
and
\delta>0
to be chosen. By proposition
3.2
, up to reducing
\delta
, we have for
i=1,2
that

(3.9)

\lambda_{k}(\beta_{i})\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)+1

Let
v,w\in H^{s}(M)
. We have, since
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
,

(3.10)

\begin{split}\left|B(\beta_{1},v,w)-B(\beta_{2},v,w)\right|&=\int_{M}\left(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}\right)vwdv_{g}\leq\|\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}\|_{L^{p}}\|v\|_{L^{\frac{2p}{p-1}}}\|w\|_{L^{\frac{2p}{p-1}}}\\
&\leq C\|\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}\|_{L^{p}}\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|w\|_{H^{s}}\end{split}

by a Hölder inequality and classical Sobolev inequalities with
\frac{2p}{p-1}\leq\frac{2n}{n-2s}
. We also have by Proposition
2.1
, (1) that

(3.11)

\|v\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\leq C_{\beta}^{2}\|v\|_{H^{s}_{\beta_{1}}}^{2}=C_{\beta}^{2}\left(\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}v-\int_{M}vA_{g}^{s}v+\int_{M}\beta_{i}v^{2}\right)

for
i=1,2
, where
A_{g}^{s}
is as in (
2.8
), and by Proposition
2.1
, (3) that

\left|\int_{M}vA_{g}^{s}v\right|\leq\frac{1}{2C_{\beta}^{2}}\int_{M}|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v|^{2}dv_{g}+D_{\beta}\int_{M}\beta_{i}v^{2}dv_{g}

for some constant
D_{\beta}>0
which is independent of
\delta
, and provided
\delta
is chosen small enough to have
B_{L^{p}}(\beta,\delta)\subseteq V_{\beta}
, where
V_{\beta}
is given by Proposition
2.1
, (3). Then (
3.11
) becomes

\int_{M}|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v|^{2}dv_{g}+\int_{M}v^{2}dv_{g}\leq C_{\beta}^{2}\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}vdv_{g}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}v|^{2}dv_{g}+C_{\beta}^{2}D_{\beta}\int_{M}\beta_{i}v^{2}dv_{g},

so that there exist constants
A_{\beta}>0
and
B_{\beta}>0
such that

(3.12)

\|v\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\leq A_{\beta}\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}vdv_{g}+B_{\beta}\int_{M}\beta_{i}v^{2}.

Now, let
0<\epsilon\leq 1
and
V_{1}\in\mathcal{G}_{k}^{\beta_{1}}(H^{s})
be such that

\max_{v\in V_{1}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{1},v)\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta_{1})+\epsilon.

This implies that
\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}vdv_{g}\leq(\lambda_{k}(\beta)+\epsilon)Q(\beta_{1},v)
for all
v\in V_{1}
. Combining the latter with (
3.9
), (
3.10
) and (
3.12
), we obtain that for
v,w\in V_{1}

(3.13)

\left|B(\beta_{1},v,w)-B(\beta_{2},v,w)\right|\leq K_{\beta}\|\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}\|_{L^{p}}Q(\beta_{1},v)^{\frac{1}{2}}Q(\beta_{1},w)^{\frac{1}{2}}

for some constant
K_{\beta}>0
which does not depend on
\delta
.
Choosing
\delta=\delta_{0}(2K_{\beta})^{-1}
we thus have, for all
v,w\in V_{1}
,

\left|B(\beta_{1},v,w)-B(\beta_{2},v,w)\right|\leq\delta_{0}Q(\beta_{1},v)^{\frac{1}{2}}Q(\beta_{1},w)^{\frac{1}{2}}

and it is clear that
V_{1}\in\mathcal{G}_{k}^{\beta_{2}}(H^{s})
for
\delta_{0}
small enough. We now test
V_{1}
in the variational characterization of
\lambda_{k}(\beta_{2})
and we use (
3.13
) to get

\begin{split}\lambda_{k}(\beta_{2})&\leq\max_{v\in V_{1}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{2},v)\\
&\leq\big(\lambda_{k}(\beta_{1})+\epsilon\big)\left(1+2K_{\beta}\|\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}\|_{L^{p}}\right)\end{split}

provided
\delta_{0}
is small enough. Letting
\epsilon\to 0
gives

\lambda_{k}(\beta_{2})-\lambda_{k}(\beta_{1})\leq 2K_{\beta}\lambda_{k}(\beta_{1})\|\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}\|_{L^{p}}.

Repeating the same argument and exchanging
\lambda_{k}(\beta_{1})
and
\lambda_{k}(\beta_{2})
completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Let
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
for some
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
. We let in what follows

(3.14)

m(\beta)=\min\Big\{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*};\lambda_{k}(\beta)>-\infty\Big\}.

We recall the definition of the integers
k_{+}
and
k_{-}
defined in the introduction:

k_{-}=\max\big\{k\geq 1,{\lambda}_{k}(g)<0\big\}\;\hbox{ and }k_{+}=\min\big\{k\geq 1,{\lambda}_{k}(g)>0\big\}.

If
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
, using definition (
3.2
) it is easily seen that

(3.15)

\lambda_{k}(\beta)\geq 0\quad\text{ if }k\geq k_{+}\text{ and }\beta>0\text{ a.e.},\\

(3.16)

\lambda_{k}(\beta)\leq 0\quad\text{ if }k\leq k_{+}-1.

As a consequence,
m(\beta)\leq k_{+}
. In the next result we show that generalised eigenfunctions exist for
k\geq m(\beta)
and give general conditions ensuring the continuity of generalised eigenvalues.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*},2s<n
and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
(M,g)
. We assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). Let
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. We have the following properties

(1)

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and
b_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
such that
b_{i}\to 0
as
i\to+\infty
in
L^{p}(M)
. Then
\lambda_{k}(\beta+b_{i})\to\lambda_{k}(\beta)
in
\mathbb{R}\sqcup\{-\infty\}
as
i\to+\infty
.

(2)

There is a sequence of eigenfunctions
\{\varphi_{k}\}_{k\geq m(\beta)}
in
H^{s}(M)
such that for all
k,\ell\geq m(\beta)
we have
Q(\beta,\varphi_{k},\varphi_{\ell})=\delta_{k,\ell}
and

P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{k}=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\beta\varphi_{k}\quad\text{ in }M.

(3)

We have
\lambda_{k}(\beta)\to+\infty
as
k\to+\infty
. For any fixed
k\geq m(\beta)
, the space
E_{k}(\beta)
of
k
-th eigenfunctions is equal to

E_{k}(\beta)=\text{Span}\Big\{\varphi_{i};\lambda_{i}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\Big\}

and is a finite-dimensional subspace of
H^{s}(M)
. Furthermore, if there exists
\varphi\in H^{s}(M)\backslash\{0\}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that
P_{g}^{s}\varphi=\lambda\beta\varphi
then
\lambda=\lambda_{k}(\beta)
for some
k\geq m(\beta)
and
\varphi\in E_{k}(\beta)
.

(4)

Assume that
m(\beta)\geq 2
. Then for all
m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\epsilon>0
, there are functions
\varphi_{1}^{\epsilon,m},\cdots,\varphi_{m(\beta)-1}^{\epsilon,m}
that satisfy
the following: for any
1\leq i,j\leq m(\beta)-1
and any
m(\beta)\leq k\leq m
,

\mathcal{R}(\beta,\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon,m})\leq-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\text{ and }\left\{\begin{aligned} B(\beta,\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon,m},\varphi_{j}^{\epsilon,m})&=\delta_{i,j},\\
\Gamma(\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon,m},\varphi_{j}^{\epsilon,m})&=\mathcal{R}(\beta,\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon,m})\delta_{i,j}\\
B(\beta,\varphi_{k},\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon,m})&=0\\
\end{aligned}\right..

As a simple remark, note that it easily follows from (
3.2
) that the sequence
(\lambda_{k}(\beta))_{k\geq m(\beta)}
is nondecreasing.

Proof.

We prove Proposition
3.4
in several steps.

Step 1:
let
k\geq 1
. We first claim that
\lambda_{k}(\beta+\epsilon)\to\lambda_{k}(\beta)
as
\epsilon\to 0
. As a consequence, we prove that (2) and (4) hold.

Proof of Step 1:

Since
\beta+\epsilon>0
everywhere, we have by Proposition
3.1
that
\lambda_{1}(\beta+\epsilon)>-\infty
and that there exits generalised eigenfunctions
(\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon})_{i\geq 1}
associated to
\lambda_{i}(\beta+\epsilon)
, normalised as

B(\beta+\epsilon,\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon},\varphi_{j}^{\epsilon})=\delta_{i,j}

for
i,j\geq 1
. For simplicity we will let, in the rest of this proof,
\lambda_{i}^{\epsilon}=\lambda_{i}(\beta+\epsilon)
for any
i\geq 1
. Assume first that
k\leq m(\beta)-1
.
Then
\lambda_{k}(\beta)=-\infty
, and
\lambda_{k}(\beta+\epsilon)\to-\infty
as a consequence of Proposition
3.2
.
We now assume that
k\geq m(\beta)
. We will prove that there exists
\varphi_{k}\in H^{s}(M)
with
Q(\beta,\varphi_{k})=1
and
-\infty<\nu_{k}\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)
such that the following holds:

(3.17)

P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{k}=\nu_{k}\beta\varphi_{k}\quad\text{ in }M.

Assume first that
k\geq k_{+}
. Then by (
3.15
) we have
\lambda_{k}^{\epsilon}\geq 0
for all
\epsilon>0
, and hence by Proposition
3.2
we obtain
\lambda_{k}(\beta)\geq 0
. Since
\beta+\epsilon\to\beta
in
L^{p}(M)
as
\epsilon\to 0
for any
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
, with
\beta\neq 0
, lemma
2.2
applies and shows that the family
(\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. Up to the extraction of a subsequence,
\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon}
weakly converges in
H^{s}(M)
and strongly converges in
L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
and in
H^{s-1}(M)
to some function
\varphi_{k}
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Up to a subsequence, the previous analysis also shows that
\lambda_{k}^{\epsilon}\to\nu_{k}
as
\epsilon\to 0
for some real number
\nu_{k}
satisfying
0<\nu_{k}\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. For any
\epsilon>0
,
\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon}
satisfies

P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon}=\lambda_{k}^{\epsilon}(\beta+\epsilon)\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon}\quad\text{ in }M,

and passing the latter to the weak limit as
\epsilon\to 0
shows that (
3.17
) is satisfied when
k\geq k_{+}
. The relation
Q(\beta+\varepsilon,\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon})=1
also passes to the limit and shows that
Q(\beta,\varphi_{k})=1
, hence
\varphi_{k}\neq 0
. If
k,\ell\geq k_{+}
the relation
B(\beta+\epsilon,\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon},\varphi_{\ell}^{\epsilon})=\delta_{k,\ell}
similarly passes to the limit and shows that
B(\beta,\varphi_{k},\varphi_{\ell})=\delta_{k,\ell}
. In particular, for
\epsilon
small enough,

(3.18)

\dim_{\beta}\left(\text{Span}\left(\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon}\right)_{k_{+}\leq i\leq k}\right)=\dim\left(\text{Span}\left(\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon}\right)_{k_{+}\leq i\leq k}\right)=k-k_{+}+1.

We now assume that
m(\beta)\leq k\leq k_{+}-1
. Here the difficulty is to prove that

(3.19)

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}|\lambda_{k}^{\epsilon}|<+\infty.

We would like to test
V_{\epsilon}=\text{Span}\left(\varphi_{1}^{\epsilon},\cdots\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon}\right)
in the variational characterization of
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. However one could have
\dim_{\beta}\left(V_{\epsilon}\right)<k
. To avoid this problem we set a small perturbation for
t\in\mathbb{R}
:

V_{\epsilon}^{t}=\text{Span}\left(\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon}+t\varphi_{k_{+}-1+i}^{\epsilon};i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}\right).

We have that

P(t):=\det\left(\left(\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon}+t\varphi_{k_{+}-1+i}^{\epsilon}\right)\right)_{1\leq i\leq k}|\left(\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi_{k_{+}-1+i}^{\epsilon}\right)_{1\leq i\leq k}\right)

is a well defined polynomial of degree
k
since
\left(\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi_{k_{+}-1+i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq k}
is a free family by (
3.18
) and we have
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\frac{P(t)}{t^{k}}=1
. Since
P
has finitely many roots we can let
t_{\epsilon}\leq 1
be such that
P(t_{\epsilon})\neq 0
and we can test
V_{\epsilon}^{t_{\epsilon}}
in the definition (
3.2
) of
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. Let
(\alpha_{i}^{\epsilon})_{1\leq i\leq k}
be such that

v_{\epsilon}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}^{\epsilon}\left(\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon}+t_{\epsilon}\varphi_{k_{+}-1+i}^{\epsilon}\right)

attains
\max_{v\in V_{\epsilon}^{t_{\epsilon}}\backslash\{0\}}\frac{G(v)}{Q(\beta,v)}
. Then

\lambda_{k}(\beta)\leq\max_{v\in V_{\epsilon}^{t_{\epsilon}}}\frac{G(v)}{Q(\beta,v)}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\alpha_{i}^{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\left(\lambda_{i}^{\epsilon}+t_{\epsilon}^{2}\lambda_{k_{+}+i-1}^{\epsilon}\right)}{1+t_{\epsilon}^{2}-Q(\epsilon,v)}\leq\frac{\lambda_{k}^{\epsilon}+t_{\epsilon}^{2}\lambda_{k_{+}+k-1}^{\epsilon}}{1+t_{\epsilon}^{2}-Q(\epsilon,v)},

where we used that for any
i,j\geq 1
and
\epsilon>0
,
\Lambda(\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon},\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon})=\lambda_{i}^{\epsilon}\delta_{i,j}
. Then

\lambda_{k}^{\epsilon}\geq\lambda_{k}(\beta)\left(1-Q(\epsilon,v^{\epsilon})\right)-t_{\epsilon}^{2}\lambda_{k_{+}+k-1}^{\epsilon}\geq\min\left(\lambda_{k}(\beta),0\right)-\lambda_{k_{+}+k-1}^{\epsilon},

where to obtain the last inequality we used (
3.16
). The right-hand side of the previous expression is uniformly bounded from below as
\epsilon\to 0
since
\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\lambda_{k_{+}+k-1}^{\epsilon}\leq\lambda_{k_{+}+k-1}(\beta)
. This proves (
3.19
) for
m(\beta)\leq k\leq k_{+}-1
. With (
3.19
) we can proceed as in the case
k\geq k_{+}
: we apply lemma
2.2
to the sequence
(\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
and obtain, up to the extraction of a subsequence, that
\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon}\rightharpoonup\varphi_{k}
in
H^{s}(M)
and
\lambda_{k}^{\epsilon}\to\nu_{k}
for some
-\infty<\nu_{k}\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Passing the eigenvalue equation satisfied by
\varphi_{k}^{\epsilon}
to the weak limit as
\epsilon\to 0
similarly proves (
3.17
) for
k\leq k_{+}-1
.

We now prove (4). For
m\geq m(\beta)
we let

H_{m}=\Big\{v\in H^{s}(M);\forall m(\beta)\leq i\leq m,Q(\beta,\varphi_{i},v)=0\Big\}

where, for
i\geq m(\beta)
,
\varphi_{i}
is given by (
3.17
). We claim that

(3.20)

\inf_{V\in\mathcal{G}^{\beta}_{m(\beta)-1}(H_{m})}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)=-\infty.

Indeed, by definition of
m(\beta)
and by (
3.2
) we can let, for any
\epsilon>0
,
V\in\mathcal{G}_{m(\beta)-1}^{\beta}(H^{s}(M))
be such that

(3.21)

\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)\leq-\frac{1}{\epsilon}

Let

V_{m}=\left\{v-\pi_{m}(v);v\in V\right\}

where

\pi_{m}(v)=\sum_{i=m(\beta)}^{m}B(\beta,v,\varphi_{i})\frac{\varphi_{i}}{Q(\beta,\varphi_{i})}

is the orthogonal projection of
V
on
\text{Span}\big\{\varphi_{i},m(\beta)\leq i\leq m\big\}
with respect to
Q(\beta,\cdot)
. We observe that, up to assuming that
\epsilon
is small enough, we can assume that
\dim_{\beta}(V_{m})=\dim_{\beta}(V)=m(\beta)-1
. Indeed, if this were not the case, a linear combination of the family
(\varphi_{i})_{m(\beta)\leq i\leq m}
would belong to
V
, which would contradict (
3.21
) for
\epsilon
small enough. Straightforward computations using the definition of
\varphi_{i}
show that, for any
v\in V
,

B\big(\beta,v-\pi_{m}(v),\pi_{m}(v)\big)=\Gamma\big(\beta,v-\pi_{m}(v),\pi_{m}(v)\big)=0

holds, so that

\mathcal{R}(\beta,v-\pi_{m}(v))=\frac{G(v)-G(\pi_{m}(v))}{Q(\beta,v)-Q(\beta,\pi_{m}(v))}\leq\frac{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}Q(\beta,v)-\lambda_{m(\beta)}Q(\beta,\pi_{m}(v))}{Q(\beta,v)-Q(\beta,\pi_{m}(v))}\to-\infty

as
\epsilon\to 0
. This proves (
3.20
). Let now
V_{\epsilon,m}\in\mathcal{G}^{\beta}_{m(\beta)-1}(H_{m})
be such that

\max_{v\in V_{\epsilon,m}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)\leq-\frac{1}{\epsilon}

By an orthodiagonalization of
\Gamma
with respect to
Q(\beta,\cdot)
on
V_{\epsilon,m}
, we obtain a family
\varphi_{1}^{\epsilon,m},\cdots,\varphi_{m(\beta)-1}^{\epsilon,m}
which satisfies property (4).

We are now in position to conclude the proof of (1) when
b_{i}=\epsilon_{i}
and
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
is a sequence of positive constants, and conclude the proof of (2). Let
k\geq m(\beta)
and let

V_{\epsilon}=\text{Span}\Big\{\varphi_{1}^{\epsilon,k},\cdots,\varphi_{m(\beta)-1}^{\epsilon,k},\varphi_{m(\beta)},\cdots,\varphi_{k}\Big\}.

The variational characterization (
3.2
) of
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
together with (
3.17
) show that

(3.22)

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(\beta)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\alpha\in\mathbb{S}^{k-1}}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m(\beta)-1}\alpha_{i}^{2}\mathcal{R}(\beta,\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon,m})+\sum_{i=m(\beta)}^{k}\alpha_{i}^{2}\mathcal{R}(\beta,\varphi_{i})}{\sum_{i=1}^{m(\beta)-1}\alpha_{i}^{2}Q(\beta,\varphi_{i}^{\epsilon,m})+\sum_{i=m(\beta)}^{k}\alpha_{i}^{2}Q(\beta,\varphi_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\alpha\in\mathbb{S}^{k-1}}\sum_{i=m(\beta)}^{m}\alpha_{i}^{2}\mathcal{R}(\beta,\varphi_{i})\leq\nu_{k}.

Therefore,
\nu_{k}=\lambda_{k}(\beta)
and (1) holds for
b_{i}=\epsilon_{i}
. Coming back to (
3.17
), this also finishes the proof of point (2).

Step 2:
We prove property (3).

Proof of Step 2:
First, recall that by Proposition
3.1

E_{k}(\beta)
is orthogonal to
K_{\beta}
defined in (
2.1
) for the bilinear form
\Gamma(\beta,\cdot,\cdot)
. As a consequence,
B(\beta,\cdot,\cdot)
defines a scalar product on
E_{k}(\beta)
. We first prove that the unit ball of
E_{k}(\beta)
with respect to the norm
\sqrt{Q(\beta,\cdot)}
is compact. Indeed, let
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of generalised eigenfunctions associated to
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. By Lemma
2.2
, since
\beta\neq 0
is fixed,
(v_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. Up to the extraction of a subsequence,
v_{i}
converges to some
v
weakly in
H^{s}(M)
and strongly in
L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
. Passing to the weak limit, it is easily seen that
v
still satisfies
P_{g}^{s}v=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\beta v
in
M
. For all
i\geq 0
we thus have
P_{g}^{s}(v_{i}-v)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\beta(v_{i}-v)
, so that integrating against
v_{i}-v
and using (
2.8
) shows that
\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}(v_{i}-v)\big|^{2}dv_{g}=o(1)
as
i\to+\infty
. Thus the unit ball of
E_{k}(\beta)
for
\sqrt{Q(\beta,\cdot)}
is compact and
E_{k}(\beta)
is finite dimensional.

Assume now by contradiction that the sequence
(\lambda_{k}(\beta))_{k\geq m(\beta)}
is bounded. A similar argument to the one we just used, again relying on Lemma
2.2
, then shows that the unit ball of
E=\overline{\bigoplus_{k\geq m(\beta)}E_{k}(\beta)}
with respect to the norm
\sqrt{Q(\beta,\cdot)}
is compact. This shows that
E
is finite-dimensional, which is impossible since the family
(\varphi_{k})_{k\geq m(\beta)}
is free by point (2) of Proposition
3.4
. Hence
\lim_{k\to+\infty}\lambda_{k}(\beta)=+\infty
.

Assume now that there are
\varphi\in H^{s}(M)\backslash\{0\}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that
P_{g}^{s}\varphi=\lambda\beta\varphi
in
M
and assume that
\lambda\not\in\{\lambda_{k}(\beta)\}_{k\geq m(\beta)}
. We let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
be such that
\lambda_{k-1}(\beta)<\lambda<\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. We let

\tilde{H}_{k}=\Big\{v\in H^{s}(M);Q(\beta,\varphi,v)=0\text{ and},\forall m(\beta)\leq i\leq k-1,Q(\beta,\varphi_{i},v)=0\Big\}.

Arguing as in the proof of point (4) above it is easily seen that, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a family
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}^{\epsilon},\cdots,\tilde{\varphi}_{m(\beta)-1}^{\epsilon}
of functions in
H^{s}(M)
satisfying, for any
1\leq i,j\leq m(\beta)-1
and any
m(\beta)\leq\ell\leq k-1
,

\mathcal{R}(\beta,\tilde{\varphi}_{i}^{\epsilon})\leq-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\text{ and }\left\{\begin{aligned} B(\beta,\tilde{\varphi}_{i}^{\epsilon},\tilde{\varphi}_{j}^{\epsilon})&=\delta_{i,j},\\
\Gamma(\tilde{\varphi}_{i}^{\epsilon},\tilde{\varphi}_{j}^{\epsilon})&=\mathcal{R}(\beta,\tilde{\varphi}_{i}^{\epsilon})\delta_{i,j}\\
B(\beta,\varphi_{\ell},\tilde{\varphi}_{i}^{\epsilon})&=0\\
B(\beta,\varphi,\tilde{\varphi}_{i}^{\epsilon})&=0\end{aligned}\right..

We let

V_{\epsilon}=\text{Span}\Big\{\tilde{\varphi}_{1}^{\epsilon},\cdots,\tilde{\varphi}_{m(\beta)-1}^{\epsilon},\varphi_{m(\beta)},\cdots,\varphi\Big\}.

Then
\dim_{\beta}V_{\epsilon}=k
and mimicking the computations in (
3.22
), the variational characterisation (
3.2
) of
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
then shows that
\lambda_{k}(\beta)\leq\lambda
, which is a contradiction. Hence
\lambda=\lambda_{m}(\beta)
for some
m\geq m(\beta)
, and this also proves that for all
k\geq m(\beta)
we have

E_{k}(\beta)=\text{Span}\Big\{\varphi_{i};\lambda_{i}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\Big\},

and concludes the proof of (3).

Step 3:
we conclude the proof of (1).

Proof of Step 3:
we let
(b_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
such that
b_{i}\to 0
in
L^{p}(M)
as
i\to+\infty
. We claim that

(3.23)

m(\beta+b_{i})\leq m(\beta)

for all
i
large enough. Assume first that
\lambda_{m(\beta)}(\beta)\geq 0
. Then, by (
3.2
), any
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k}^{\beta}(H^{s}(M))
contains a nonzero element
v
such that
G(v)\geq 0
. Then, since
b_{i}\geq 0
,

\max_{v\in V\backslash\{0\}}\frac{G(v)}{Q(\beta+b_{i},v)}\geq 0

and hence
\lambda_{m(\beta)}(\beta+b_{i})\geq 0
. Assume now that
\lambda_{m(\beta)}<0
. Proposition
3.2
then shows that
\lambda_{m(\beta)}(\beta+b_{i})<0
for
i
large enough. In the definition (
3.2
) of
\lambda_{k}(\beta+b_{i})
the infimum may therefore only be taken over subsets
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k}^{\beta}(H^{s}(M))
such that
\max_{v\in V\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)<0
. For a such a
V
we have, since
b_{i}\geq 0
,

\max_{v\in V\backslash\{0\}}\frac{G(v)}{Q(\beta+b_{i},v)}\geq\max_{v\in V\backslash\{0\}}\frac{G(v)}{Q(\beta,v)},

and taking the infimum over
V
yields
\lambda_{m(\beta)}(\beta+b_{i})\geq\lambda_{m(\beta)}(\beta)
. This proves (
3.23
).
With (
3.23
), and by points (2) and (3) of Proposition
3.4
, for any
i\geq 0
there thus exists a sequence
(\varphi_{k}^{i})_{k\geq m(\beta)}
of generalised eigenfunctions associated to
\beta+b_{i}
and satisfying
P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{k}^{i}=\lambda_{k}(\beta+b_{i})(\beta+b_{i})\varphi_{k}^{i}
in
M
for every
k\geq m(\beta)
and
i\geq 0
. The arguments in the proof of (
3.23
) shows that for every
k\geq m(\beta)
the sequence
(\lambda_{k}(\beta+b_{i}))_{i\geq 0}
is uniformly bounded and, up to a subsequence, converges to some
\nu_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\nu_{k}\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. We can now mimic the arguments in the proof of Step
1
to show that
(\varphi_{k}^{i})_{i\geq 0}
converges, up to a subsequence, to
\varphi_{k}\in H^{s}(M)
satisfying
P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{k}=\nu_{k}\beta\varphi_{k}
in
M
. That
\nu_{k}=\lambda_{k}(\beta)
now follows from point (3) of Proposition
3.4
.
∎

We end this section with the following proposition. For
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
we let

\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*},2s<n
and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
(M,g)
. We assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). Let
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
.

Let
k\leq k_{-}
. Then
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
is upper semi-continuous and

(3.24)

\sup_{\beta\in C^{\infty}(M);\beta>0}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)=\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)=\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p},\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}\geq 1}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)<0.

Let
k\geq k_{+}
. Then
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
is lower semi-continuous and

(3.25)

\inf_{\beta\in C^{\infty}(M);\beta>0}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)=\inf_{\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)=\inf_{\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p},\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}\geq 1}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)>0.

This result will be used in the next section, in particular in the proof of Proposition
4.4
, when we will investigate the variational theory for eigenvalue functionals.

Remark 3.3
.

Notice that if
k\geq k_{+}
,
\lambda_{k}
is continuous in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
by Proposition
3.2
and Proposition
3.5
.

Proof.

We first assume that
k\leq k_{-}
. Upper semi-continuity of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
is given by Proposition
3.2
. We now prove (
3.24
). We first have by Proposition
3.4
(1) that

\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)=\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p},\beta>_{a.e}0}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta).

We then use that
\{\beta\in C^{\infty}(M),\beta>0\}
is dense in the set
\{\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p},\beta>_{a.e}0\}
with respect to the
L^{p}
distance and Proposition
3.3
to obtain the first equality in (
3.24
). The second equality comes from the observation that
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\nu\beta)=\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
for any
\nu>0
. The strict inequality in (
3.24
) is a consequence of lemma
2.2
(2) applied to a maximising sequence of eigenfunctions for
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
(which are given, for instance, by Proposition
3.4
, (2)).

We now assume that
k\geq k_{+}
. The proof of (
3.25
) follows the same lines than in the case
k\leq k_{-}
and we just have to prove that
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
is lower semi-continuous.
Let
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
such that
\beta_{i}\to\beta
in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
as
i\to+\infty
. For
k_{+}\leq j,\ell\leq k
, by Proposition
3.4
(2), there exist eigenfunctions
\varphi_{i}^{j}
associated to
\lambda_{j}(\beta_{i})\geq 0
such that
B(\beta_{i},\varphi_{i}^{j},\varphi_{i}^{\ell})=\delta_{j,\ell}
. By Proposition
3.2
, since
\beta_{i}
strongly converges to
\beta
in
L^{p}(M)
,
\left(\lambda_{j}(\beta_{i})\right)_{i\geq 0}
is bounded from above. Then Lemma
2.2
applies and since
\beta\neq 0
, shows that
(\varphi_{i}^{j})_{i\geq 0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
for
k\leq j\leq k_{+}
. Up to the extraction of a subsequence,
(\varphi_{i}^{j})_{i\geq 0}
weakly converges to
v_{j}
in
H^{s}(M)
. Passing to the weak limit in the equation satisfied by
\varphi_{i}^{j}
we get that

P_{g}^{s}v_{j}=\nu_{j}\beta v_{j}\quad\text{ in }M,

where up to the extraction of a subsequence,
\nu_{j}=\lim_{i\to+\infty}\lambda_{j}(\beta_{i})\leq\lambda_{j}(\beta)
.
By Proposition
2.1
, (2), we may pass the relations
B(\beta_{i},\varphi_{i}^{j},\varphi_{i}^{\ell})=\delta_{j,\ell}
to the limit as
i\to+\infty
and obtain
B(\beta,v_{j},v_{\ell})=\delta_{j,\ell}
.
We now claim that there exists a family
(\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1})
of functions in
H^{s}(M)
that satisfy

\displaystyle\int_{M}\varphi_{i}P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}dv_{g}\leq 0\quad\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq k_{+}-1\text{ and }

\displaystyle\int_{M}\varphi_{i}P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{j}dv_{g}=\Big(\int_{M}\varphi_{i}P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}dv_{g}\Big)\delta_{ij}\quad\text{ for all }1\leq i,j\leq k,

and such that
(\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1},v_{k_{+}},\cdots,v_{k})
is orthonormal with respect to
Q(\beta,\cdot)
. By Proposition
3.4
(2), we let
(\varphi_{m(\beta)},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1})
be generalised eigenfunctions associated to
\left(\lambda_{j}(\beta)\right)_{m(\beta)\leq j\leq k_{+}-1}
such that
(\varphi_{m(\beta)},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1},v_{k_{+}},\cdots,v_{k})
is orthonormal with respect to
Q(\beta,\cdot)
. By Proposition
3.4
(4), applied for
m=k
and
\epsilon=-1
, we obtain an orthonormal family
(\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1},v_{k_{+}},\cdots,v_{k})
with respect to
Q(\beta,\cdot)
so that the expected properties hold. We now let

V=\text{Span}\big\{\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1},v_{k_{+}},\cdots,v_{k}\big\},

which is an admissible subspace in the definition (
3.2
) of
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. Simple computations then show that
\lambda_{k}(\beta)\leq\nu_{k}
, which shows that
\lambda_{k}
is lower semi-continuous in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
when
k\geq k_{+}
. The lower semi-continuity of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
then easily follows.
∎

4.
Variational theory for generalised eigenvalues

In this section we derive the Euler-Lagrange equations associated to local extremals of renormalised eigenvalue functionals. We also prove the existence of almost-extremals for such functionals as well as the existence of true extremals for a subcritical approximation of (
1.2
).

4.1.
First variation of generalised eigenvalues

We aim at computing the directional derivatives of eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
at
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
for variations of the form
\beta+tb\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
, with
t\geq 0
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. If
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
for some
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
we introduce the following notations: for
k\geq m(\beta)
, where
m(\beta)
is as in (
3.14
),

(4.1)

\displaystyle i(k)

\displaystyle=\min\{i\in\mathbb{N}^{*};\lambda_{i}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\}

\displaystyle I(k)

\displaystyle=\max\{i\in\mathbb{N}^{*};\lambda_{i}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\}.

The integers
i(k)
and
I(k)
do depend on
\beta
but for simplicity, and since no confusion shall arise in the following, we will omit the dependence on
\beta
. If
\mathcal{R}
is as in (
3.1
) we denote by
\mathcal{R}_{\beta}(\beta,v)
the partial differential of
\mathcal{R}
with respect to the first variable at
(\beta,v)
, which is given by

(4.2)

\mathcal{R}_{\beta}(\beta,v)(h)=-\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)\frac{\int_{M}hv^{2}dv_{g}}{\int_{M}\beta v^{2}dv_{g}}.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
,
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
n>2s
and
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
. Let
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. We assume that
\beta>0
or that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). Let
k\geq m(\beta)
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. Then

(4.3)

\begin{split}\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{\lambda_{k}(\beta+tb)-\lambda_{k}(\beta)}{t}=&\min_{V\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}_{\beta}(\beta,v)(b)\\
=&\max_{V\in\mathcal{G}_{I(k)-k+1}(E_{k}(\beta))}\min_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}_{\beta}(\beta,v)(b)\end{split}

Remark 4.1
.

Proposition
4.1
allows us to compute right derivatives of eigenvalues at points
\beta
that can be at the boundary of the set
\mathcal{A}_{p}
. This generalises the previous results in
[
1
,
24
]
where, in the special case of
P_{g}^{1}=\Delta_{g}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}S_{g}
, left and right derivatives along variations of the form
(1+th)\beta
were computed. The right derivative along general variations
\beta+tb
that we cover here provides more information and was first observed and used in
[
48
,
52
,
51
]
for Laplace eigenvalues in dimension 2. This result will be crucial for the computation of the Euler-Lagrange equation of optimisers and also for almost optimisers.

Proof.

First, the second equality in (
4.3
) is a consequence of the explicit form (
4.2
) of
\mathcal{R}_{\beta}
and of min-max formulae for the quotients of a quadratic form by a positive definite quadratic form on finite-dimensional spaces. We thus only have to prove the first equality in (
4.3
). Notice that from Proposition
3.4
, we have that
\lambda_{k}(\beta+tb)\to\lambda_{k}(\beta)
as
t\searrow 0
, hence
\lambda_{k}(\beta+tb)>-\infty
for all
t
small enough when
k\geq m(\beta)
. For
t\geq 0
we denote by

\varphi_{i(k)}^{t},\cdots,\varphi_{I(k)}^{t}

a family of
Q(\beta+tb,\cdot)
-orthonormal generalised eigenfunctions associated to the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i(k)}(\beta+tb)\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{I(k)}(\beta+tb)
, that we denote for simplicity by

\lambda_{i(k)}^{t}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{I(k)}^{t}.

By Proposition
3.4
and by the definition (
4.1
) of
i(k)
and
I(k)
these families all converge to
\lambda_{k}=\lambda_{k}(\beta)
as
t\to 0
.
By Lemma
2.2
, since
\beta+tb\to\beta\neq 0
,
(\varphi_{i}^{t})_{t}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
as
t\to 0
for all
i(k)\leq i\leq I(k)
, so that up to the extraction of a subsequence as
t\to 0
,
\varphi_{i}^{t}
converges to
\varphi_{i}
weakly in
H^{s}(M)
, strongly in
H^{s-1}(M)
and in
L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
by Proposition
2.1
, (2). As a consequence,
Q(\beta+tb,\varphi_{i}^{t}-\varphi_{i})\to 0
as
t\to 0
, and passing to the weak limit the equation satisfied by
\varphi_{i}^{t}
and passing to the limit the relation
B(\beta+tb,\varphi_{i}^{t},\varphi_{j}^{t})=\delta_{i,j}
, we obtain

P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}=\lambda_{k}\beta\varphi_{i}\quad\text{ and }\quad B(\beta,\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j})=\delta_{i,j}

for
i(k)\leq i,j\leq I(k)
. Integrating the equation with respect to
\varphi_{i}
proves that

\begin{split}\int_{M}\varphi_{i}\Delta_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}dv_{g}&=-\int_{M}\varphi_{i}A_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}dv_{g}+\lambda_{k}Q(\beta,\varphi_{i})\\
&=-\int_{M}\varphi_{i}^{t}A_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}^{t}dv_{g}+\lambda_{i}^{t}Q(\beta+tb,\varphi_{i}^{t})+o(1)\\
&=\int_{M}\varphi_{i}^{t}\Delta_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}^{t}dv_{g}+o(1)\end{split}

as
t\to 0
, where
A_{g}^{s}
is as in (
2.8
), so that
\varphi_{i}^{t}
converges strongly to
\varphi_{i}
in
H^{s}(M)
as
t\to 0
.

For
i(k)\leq i\leq I(k)
we let
R_{i}^{t}=\varphi_{i}^{t}-\pi_{k}(\varphi_{i}^{t})
where for
v\in H^{s}(M)
we have let

\pi_{k}(v)=\sum_{i=i(k)}^{I(k)}B(\beta,v,\varphi_{i})\varphi_{i}

be the orthogonal projection of
v
on
E_{k}(\beta)
with respect to
Q(\beta,\cdot)
. We have, for every
t\geq 0
,

(4.4)

P_{g}^{s}R_{i}^{t}-\lambda_{k}\beta R_{i}^{t}=\lambda_{i}^{t}(\beta+tb)\varphi_{i}^{t}-\lambda_{k}\beta\varphi_{i}^{t}=(\lambda_{i}^{t}-\lambda_{k})\beta\varphi_{i}^{t}+\lambda_{i}^{t}tb\varphi_{i}^{t}.

For
i(k)\leq i\leq I(k)
we let, for
t>0
,

(4.5)

\alpha_{i}^{t}=\left|\lambda_{i}^{t}-\lambda_{k}\right|+t+\|R_{i}^{t}\|_{H^{s}}

and

(4.6)

\tilde{R}_{i}^{t}=\frac{R_{i}^{t}}{\alpha_{i}^{t}}\hskip 14.22636pt\tau_{i}^{t}=\frac{t}{\alpha_{i}^{t}}\hskip 14.22636pt\delta_{i}^{t}=\frac{\lambda_{i}^{t}-\lambda_{k}}{\alpha_{i}^{t}}.

Up to the extraction of a subsequence as
t\to 0
, we have

\tilde{R}_{i}^{t}\to\tilde{R}_{i}\text{ weakly in }H^{s}(M),\hskip 14.22636pt\tau_{i}^{t}\to\tau_{i},\hskip 14.22636pt\delta_{i}^{t}\to\delta_{i},

where
\tau_{i}
and
\delta_{i}
belong to
[0,1]
. Passing to the weak limit in (
4.4
) we obtain

(4.7)

P_{g}^{s}\tilde{R}_{i}-\lambda_{k}\beta\tilde{R}_{i}=\delta_{i}\beta\varphi_{i}+\tau_{i}\lambda_{k}b\varphi_{i}.

Dividing (
4.4
) by
\alpha_{i}^{t}
and substracting (
4.7
) shows that
\tilde{R}_{i}^{t}-\tilde{R}_{i}
satisfies

\big(P_{g}^{s}-\lambda_{k}\beta\big)\big(\tilde{R}_{i}^{t}-\tilde{R}_{i}\big)=o(1)\quad\text{ in }H^{-s}(M)\quad\text{ as }t\to 0.

Standard elliptic theory shows independently that for any
f\in E_{k}(\beta)^{\perp_{Q}(\beta,\cdot)}
we have
\|f\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\|\big(P_{g}^{s}-\lambda_{k}\beta\big)f\|_{H^{-s}}
for some
C>0
. Since by definition
\tilde{R}_{i}^{t}-\tilde{R}_{i}\in E_{k}(\beta)^{\perp_{Q}(\beta,\cdot)}
we get that, up to passing to a subsequence,
(\tilde{R}_{i}^{t})_{t}
converges strongly to
\tilde{R}_{i}
as
t\to 0
in
H^{s}(M)
and with (
4.5
), and (
4.6
) we have

(4.8)

\|\tilde{R}_{i}\|_{H^{s}}+|\delta_{i}|+\tau_{i}=1.

Now, we integrate (
4.7
) against
\varphi_{i}
and we obtain that

(4.9)

\delta_{i}Q(\beta,\varphi_{i})+\tau_{i}\lambda_{k}\int_{M}b\varphi_{i}^{2}dv_{g}=0.

Assume by contradiction that
\tau_{i}=0
, then by (
4.9
) and since
Q(\beta,\varphi_{i})=1
, we have
\delta_{i}=0
so that by (
4.7
),
\tilde{R}_{i}\in E_{k}(\beta)\cap E_{k}(\beta)^{\perp_{Q(\beta,\cdot)}}=\{0\}
. This contradicts (
4.8
). Therefore
\tau_{i}\neq 0
and, by (
4.9
),

\frac{\delta_{i}}{\tau_{i}}=\frac{-\lambda_{k}\int_{M}b\varphi_{i}^{2}dv_{g}}{Q(\beta,\varphi_{i})}

Integrating (
4.7
) against
\varphi_{j}
for
j\neq i
, we obtain that

\lambda_{k}\int_{M}b\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}=0.

so that
\varphi_{i(k)},\cdots,\varphi_{I(k)}
are both an orthonormal basis for
Q(\beta,\cdot)
and an orthogonal family for the bilinear form
(\varphi,\psi)\mapsto-\lambda_{k}\int_{M}b\varphi\psi dv_{g}
over
E_{k}(\beta)
. Since by construction we have
\frac{\delta_{i}^{t}}{\tau_{i}^{t}}=\frac{\lambda_{i}^{t}-\lambda_{i}}{t}
the family
\big(\frac{\delta_{i}^{t}}{\tau_{i}^{t}}\big)_{i(k)\leq i\leq I(k)}
is non-decreasing in
i
for every fixed
t>0
. Passing to the limit yields
\frac{\delta_{i(k)}}{\tau_{i(k)}}\leq\cdots\leq\frac{\delta_{I(k)}}{\tau_{I(k)}}
and classical min-max formulae for orthonormal diagonalization give

\frac{\delta_{i}}{\tau_{i}}=\min_{V\in\mathcal{G}_{i-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{-\lambda_{k}\int_{M}bv^{2}dv_{g}}{Q(\beta,v)}.

Since the right-hand side is independent of the choice of the subsequence as
t\to 0
, we obtain that the directional derivative exists and

\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{t}-\lambda_{i}}{t}=\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{\delta_{i}^{t}}{\tau_{i}^{t}}=\frac{\delta_{i}}{\tau_{i}}.

Since
\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)=\lambda_{k}
when
v\in V\in\mathcal{G}_{i-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))
this proves (
4.3
)
∎

4.2.
Euler-Lagrange equation for local extremals of renormalised eigenvalue functionals

Let
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
be fixed and let
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. Let
k\geq m(\beta)
, where
m(\beta)
is defined in (
3.14
). We define the following volume-renormalised version of
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
:

(4.10)

\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}.

We recall that the integers
k_{-}
and
k_{+}
in this paper are respectively defined as follows:

(4.11)

k_{-}=\max\{k\geq 1,{\lambda}_{k}(g)<0\}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad k_{+}=\min\{k\geq 1,{\lambda}_{k}(g)>0\},

where
{\lambda}_{k}(g)
denote the eigenvalues of the GJMS operator
P_{g}^{s}
over
H^{s}(M)
. If
\lambda_{1}(g)\geq 0
we use the convention
k_{-}=0
. We recall that
i(k)=i(k,\beta)
and
I(k)=I(k,\beta)
are defined in (
4.1
). We recall that we wish, in this paper, to maximise negative eigenvalues of
P_{g}^{s}
and minimise positive eigenvalues of
P_{g}^{s}
in
[g]
(the setting is that of Proposition
3.5
). The following result establishes the Euler-Lagrange equation satisfied by local extremals of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
over
\mathcal{A}_{p}
(if they exist):

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*},2s<n
, and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
(M,g)
. Let
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and let
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
be such that
\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}=1
. We assume that
\beta>0
or that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
).

•

Assume that
k\geq k_{+}
and that
\beta
is a local minimum of
\bar{\lambda}^{p}_{k}
. Then, for any subspace
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))
, there exists an integer
i(k)\leq r\leq k
, a
Q(\beta,\cdot)
-orthonormal family
(v_{r},\cdots,v_{k})
of
V
, and there exist positive numbers
d_{r},\cdots,d_{k}
satisfying
\sum_{i=r}^{k}d_{i}=1
such that

\beta^{p-1}=\sum_{i=r}^{k}d_{i}v_{i}^{2}\quad\text{ a. e. in }M.

•

Assume that
k\leq k_{-}
and that
\beta
is a local maximum of
\bar{\lambda}^{p}_{k}
. Then, for any subspace
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))
, there exists an integer
I(k)\geq s\geq k
, a
Q(\beta,\cdot)
-orthonormal family
(v_{k},\cdots,v_{s})
of
V
, and there exist positive numbers
d_{k},\cdots,d_{s}
satisfying
\sum_{i=k}^{s}d_{i}=1
such that

\beta^{p-1}=\sum_{i=k}^{s}d_{i}v_{i}^{2}\quad\text{ a. e. in }M.

Recall that if
i(k)\leq r\leq k
or
k\leq r\leq I(k)
we have
\lambda_{r}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)
by (
4.1
), and that if
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))
and
v\in V
we have
P_{g}^{s}v=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\beta v
in
M
. In the cases
s=1
and
s=2
weaker forms of Proposition
4.2
had previously been obtained in
[
24
,
46
]
.

Remark 4.2
.

A simple but important consequence of Proposition
4.2
is the following: if
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
is an extremal for
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
for some
k\geq 1
, then
\beta\in C^{0,\alpha}(M)
for some
0<\alpha<1
. We prove this in the case
k\geq k_{+}
but the proof in the case
k\leq k_{-}
is identical. Assume that
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
, with
\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}=1
, is a local minimum of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
. Then by Proposition
4.2
there are
i(k)\leq r\leq k
, a
Q(\beta,\cdot)
-orthonormal family
(v_{r},\cdots,v_{k})
of
E_{k}(\beta)
, and positive numbers
d_{r},\cdots,d_{k}
satisfying
\sum_{i=r}^{k}d_{i}=1
such that

(4.12)

\beta^{p-1}=\sum_{i=r}^{k}d_{i}v_{i}^{2}\quad\text{ a. e. in }M.

Since
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
,
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
, every
v_{i}
satisfies
P_{g}^{s}v_{i}=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\beta v_{i}
in
M
, and standard regularity theory (see for instance Mazumdar
[
41
, Theorem 5]
) shows that
v_{i}\in L^{q}(M)
for all
q>1
and
r\leq i\leq k
. This in turn implies that
\beta\in L^{q}(M)
for all
q>1
, and standard bootstrap arguments then show that
v_{i}\in C^{1}(M)
, hence
\beta\in C^{0,\alpha}(M)
for some
0<\alpha<1
.

Remark 4.3
.

Under the assumptions of Remark
4.2
, and in the special case where
s=1
, we also obtain that if the set
\{\beta=0\}
is non-empty it is of measure zero. Indeed, since every
v_{i}
,
r\leq i\leq k
, satisfies
P_{g}^{s}v_{i}=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\beta v_{i}
in
M
and
Q(\beta,v_{i})=1
, classical unique continuation results (see e.g.
[
26
, Theorem 1.7]
) show that
v_{i}
vanishes on a set of measure zero in
M
. It then follows from (
4.12
) that the set
\{\beta=0\}
, if it is non-empty, is of measure zero. Determining
a priori
whether an extremal
\beta
for
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
for some
k\geq 1
vanishes seems difficult. In
[
24
, Theorem 1.3]
, and for
s=1
, the authors construct examples of manifolds
(M,g)
where
k_{-}\geq 2
and where
\beta\equiv 1
achieves
\Lambda_{2}^{1}(M,[g])<0
– or, in other words, where
g
itself is the maximal metric for
\Lambda_{2}^{1}(M,[g])
. By contrast, in Proposition
7.2
below and for any
s\geq 1
, we describe special situations – corresponding to the particular case
r=k
in (
4.12
), and occurring both for positive and negative generalised eigenvalues – where
\{\beta=0\}\neq\emptyset
, and where
\beta g
does not define a smooth metric in
g
. This behavior was first observed in
[
1
]
when
s=1
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

We only prove the proposition for the local minimum, which corresponds to the case
k\geq k_{+}
. The proof for the case
k\leq k_{-}
is similar. Let
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
, so that
1<p^{\prime}\leq\frac{n}{n-2s}
. We set
\bar{\lambda}_{k}=\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
in this proof. We assume that
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
is a local minimum of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}
with
\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}=1
. Let
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))
. We first prove that

(4.13)

\begin{split}\{\zeta\in L^{p^{\prime}}(M),&\zeta\geq 0\text{ a.e }\}\cap co\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\left(\beta^{p-1}-v^{2}\right);v\in V,Q(\beta,v)=1\right\}\neq\emptyset,\end{split}

where
co
denotes the convex hull. Assume by contradiction that this intersection is empty. Since
V
is finite-dimensional it is easily seen that
\{\zeta\in L^{p^{\prime}}(M),\zeta\geq 0\text{ a.e }\}
and
co\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\left(\beta^{p-1}-v^{2}\right);v\in V,Q(\beta,v)=1\right\}
are, respectively, closed and compact subsets of
(L^{p^{\prime}}(M),\|\cdot\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}})
. Since they are assumed to be disjoint the geometric Hahn-Banach theorem shows that there is
b\in L^{p}(M)
such that

(4.14)

\forall\zeta\in L^{p^{\prime}}(M),\zeta\geq 0,\int_{M}\zeta bdv_{g}\geq 0

and

(4.15)

\forall\zeta\in co\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\left(\beta^{p-1}-v^{2}\right);v\in V,Q(\beta,v)=1\right\},\int_{M}\zeta bdv_{g}<0.

(
4.14
) implies that
b\geq 0
a.e and (
4.15
) that
b\neq 0
, and hence
b\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. By Proposition
4.1
and the Leibniz rule for products of derivatives we have

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta+tb)-\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)}{t}\leq\max_{v\in V}\left(\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\int_{M}b(\beta^{p-1}-v^{2})dv_{g}\right)

Then, there is
v\in V
such that
Q(\beta,v)=1
and

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta+tb)-\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)}{t}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\int_{M}b(\beta^{p-1}-v^{2})dv_{g}.

The latter is negative by (
4.15
) applied to the function
\zeta=\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)(\beta^{p-1}-v^{2})
, which is impossible since
\beta
is a local minimum of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}
. This proves (
4.13
). Therefore for any
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))
, there are
v_{1},\cdots,v_{\ell}\in V
such that

(4.16)

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}Q(\beta,v_{i})=1\quad\text{ and }\quad\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)(\beta^{p-1}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}v_{i}^{2})\geq 0.

Integrating (
4.16
) against
\beta
gives, since
\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}=1
,

\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\geq\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}Q(\beta,v_{i})=\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta).

Hence the latter inequality is an equality and by (
4.16
),
\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)(\beta^{p}-\beta\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}v_{i}^{2})\geq 0
a.e implies that

\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\left(\beta^{p}-\beta\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}v_{i}^{2}\right)=0\quad\text{ a.e. in }M.

Since
\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)>0
by Proposition
3.5
, (
4.16
) implies that
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}v_{i}^{2}=0
a.e. in
\{\beta=0\}
. We therefore obtain that

\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\left(\beta^{p-1}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}v_{i}^{2}\right)=0
a.e. in
M
, that is that
0\in co\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\left(\beta^{p-1}-v^{2}\right);v\in V,Q(\beta,v)=1\right\}
. This is true for any linear subspace
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))
, hence

0\in\bigcap_{V\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))}co\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\left(\beta^{p-1}-v^{2}\right);v\in V,Q(\beta,v)=1\right\}.

The proposition then follows from Lemma
4.1
below since for any linear subspace
V\subset E_{k}(\beta)
of dimension
\ell
we have

(4.17)

\begin{split}co&\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\left(\beta^{p-1}-v^{2}\right);v\in V,Q(\beta,v)=1\right\}\\
&=\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\left(\beta^{p-1}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}d_{i}v_{i}^{2}\right);\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}d_{i}=1\text{ and }(v_{i})\in O_{Q(\beta,\cdot)}(V)\right\}\end{split}

where
O_{Q(\beta,\cdot)}(V)
is the set of orthonormal bases of
V
with respect to the scalar product
Q(\beta,\cdot)
.
∎

For the sake of completeness, we give the proof of the following lemma of linear algebra that we used in the proof of Proposition
4.2
(see the mixing lemma in
[
47
]
, lemma 2.1 for a more general setting):

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
F\subseteq L^{2}_{\beta}(M)
be a linear subspace of dimension
\ell
and
O_{Q(\beta,\cdot)}(F)
be the set of orthonormal bases of
F
with respect to the scalar product
Q(\beta,\cdot)
. We have

(4.18)

\begin{split}co\left\{v^{2};v\in F,Q(\beta,v)=1\right\}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}d_{i}v_{i}^{2};\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}d_{i}=1\text{ and }(v_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\in O_{Q(\beta,\cdot)}(F)\right\}\end{split}

Proof.

The right inclusion is obvious and we prove the left one. Let
(v_{\alpha})_{1\leq\alpha\leq N}
,
v_{\alpha}\in F
, be such that
Q(\beta,v_{\alpha})=1
for all
\alpha
and let
(t_{\alpha})_{1\leq\alpha\leq N}
be nonnegative numbers such that
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}t_{\alpha}=1
. Let
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{\ell})\in O_{Q(\beta,\cdot)}(F)
. Then, for
1\leq\alpha\leq N
, we can write
v_{\alpha}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}c_{i}^{\alpha}e_{i}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(c_{i}^{\alpha}\right)^{2}=1
and we have

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}t_{\alpha}v_{\alpha}^{2}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq\ell}t_{\alpha}c_{i}^{\alpha}c_{j}^{\alpha}e_{i}e_{j}=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq\ell}A_{i,j}e_{i}e_{j}

where we have set

A_{i,j}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}t_{\alpha}c_{i}^{\alpha}c_{j}^{\alpha}.

A
is a non-negative symmetric matrix. Let
d_{1},\cdots,d_{\ell}
and
O\in\mathbb{O}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})
be such that

O^{T}AO=diag(d_{1},\cdots,d_{\ell}).

Then

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}t_{\alpha}v_{\alpha}^{2}=\sum_{1\leq i,j,k\leq\ell}O_{ik}d_{k}O_{jk}e_{i}e_{j}.

Let, for
1\leq k\leq\ell
,
v_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}O_{i,k}e_{i}
. Straightforward computations show that
(v_{1},\cdots,v_{\ell})\in O_{Q(\beta,\cdot)}(F)
and that

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}t_{\alpha}v_{\alpha}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}d_{k}v_{k}^{2}

Finally we have that
\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}d_{k}=tr(A)=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}t_{\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(c_{i}^{\alpha}\right)^{2}=1
and the proof of the lemma is complete.
∎

4.3.
Unboundedness of renormalised eigenvalue functionals

The following result, based
[
2
]
, shows that positive renormalised eigenvalue functionals are not bounded from above and that renormalised negative eigenvalues are not bounded from below:

Proposition 4.3
.

We have

(4.19)

\displaystyle\inf_{{\beta}\in C^{\infty}(M),{\beta}>0}\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=-\infty

\displaystyle\text{ if }k\leq k_{-}

\displaystyle\sup_{{\beta}\in C^{\infty}(M),{\beta}>0}\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=+\infty

\displaystyle\text{ if }k\geq k_{+}.

Proposition
4.3
justifies a posteriori the choice of the extremisation problem (
1.2
). This result was first proven in
[
2
]
for positive eigenvalues.

Proof.

Since, for any
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
, the sequence
(\lambda(\beta))_{k\geq m(\beta)}
is nondecreasing we only need to prove (
4.19
) for
k=k_{-}
and
k=k_{+}
, respectively. A general result of this type has been proven in
[
2
, Theorem 1.3, Theorem 4.1]
. We check here that the assumptions of the results in
[
2
]
are satisfied. First, relation (
2.7
) shows that
P_{g}^{s}
is a conformally covariant elliptic operator of order
2s
and of bidegree
((n-2s)/2,(n+2s)/2)
in the sense of
[
2
, Definition 2.2]
.
Let now
g_{*}
denote the product metric in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
: if
g_{0}
is the round metric of
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
it is given by

g_{*}=dt^{2}\oplus g_{0}

where
t
is the variable in
\mathbb{R}
. This metric defines a metric laplacian
\Delta_{g_{*}}=-\partial_{t}^{2}+\Delta_{g_{0}}
. A recent result of Case-Malchiodi
[
10
, Theorem 1.1]
shows that since
(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1},g_{*})
is a product of Einstein manifolds,
P_{g_{*}}
explicitly writes as

P_{g_{*}}^{s}=\begin{cases}\prod_{i=0}^{\frac{s-2}{2}}D_{s-1-2i}\text{ if }s\text{ is even}\\
\left(\Delta_{g_{*}}+\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}\right)\prod_{i=0}^{\frac{s-3}{2}}D_{s-1-2i}\text{ if }s\text{ is odd},\end{cases}

where
D_{j}
is defined as

\begin{split}D_{j}&=\Delta_{g_{*}}^{2}+2\left(\left(\frac{n-2}{2}\right)^{2}\Delta_{g_{*}}+j^{2}\left(\Delta_{g}+\partial_{t}^{2}\right)\right)+\left(\left(\frac{n-2}{2}\right)^{2}-j^{2}\right)^{2}\\
&=\left(\Delta_{g_{*}}+\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}-j^{2}\right)^{2}+4j^{2}\Delta_{g}.\end{split}

Since
n>2s
we have, for
0\leq j\leq\frac{s-2}{2}
if
s
is even or
0\leq j\leq\frac{s-3}{2}
is
s
is odd,
\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}-j^{2}>0
. Straightforward computations, since all the coefficients of the operators that appear in the product are positive and since all the operators
\Delta_{g}
,
\partial_{t}
,
\Delta_{g_{*}}
commute, show that for
u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})
we have

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}}uP_{g_{*}}^{s}udv_{g_{*}}\geq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}}|\Delta_{g_{*}}^{\frac{s}{2}}u|^{2}dv_{g_{*}}+c\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}}u^{2}dv_{g_{*}}

where

c=\begin{cases}\prod_{i=0}^{\frac{s-2}{2}}\left(\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}-(s-1-2i)^{2}\right)^{2}\\
\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}\prod_{i=0}^{\frac{s-3}{2}}\left(\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}-(s-1-2i)^{2}\right)^{2}\end{cases}.

In particular
P_{g_{*}}^{s}:H^{k}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})
is coercive and thus invertible and hence
P_{g}^{s}
is invertible in
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
in the sense of
[
2
, Definition 2.4]
and its essential spectrum is contained in
[\sigma,+\infty)
for some
\sigma>0
(here
\sigma
denotes the bottom of the essential spectrum).

We may thus apply the analysis of
[
2
]
that we briefly recall. Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,2))
be such that
0\leq\chi\leq 2
and
\chi=1
in
[0,1]
. Let
2\delta<i_{g}(M)
and
\xi\in M
be fixed. We define, for
x\in M
,

\beta(x)^{\frac{1}{2s}}=1-\chi\big(d_{g}(\xi,x)\big)+\chi\big(d_{g}(\xi,x)\big)\frac{1}{d_{g}(\xi,x)}

and, for
\epsilon>0
small enough,

\beta_{\epsilon}(x)^{\frac{1}{2s}}=\left(1-\chi\left(\frac{d_{g}(x,\xi)}{\epsilon}\right)\right)\beta(x)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\chi\left(\frac{d_{g}(x,\xi)}{\epsilon}\right).

Clearly,
\beta_{\epsilon}\to\beta
in
C^{0}_{loc}(M\backslash\{\xi\})
. It is proven in
[
2
, Section 3]
that
\beta^{\frac{1}{s}}g
defines a complete asymptotically cylindrical metric in
M\backslash\{\xi\}
and that the essential spectrum of
P_{\beta^{\frac{1}{s}}g}^{s}
in
L^{2}(M\backslash\{\xi\},\beta^{\frac{1}{s}}g)
is the same as the essential spectrum of
P_{g_{*}}^{s}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R},g_{*})
, and is as such contained in
[\sigma,+\infty)\subset(0,+\infty)
. Since
P_{\beta^{\frac{1}{s}}g}^{s}
is self-adjoint its spectrum in the interval
(-\sigma,\sigma)
is discrete and, when it exists, we will denote by
\lambda_{\beta,+}
the smallest positive isolated eigenvalue of
P_{\beta^{\frac{1}{s}}g}^{s}
(resp. by
\lambda_{\beta,-}
the largest negative isolated eigenvalue of
P_{\beta^{\frac{1}{s}}g}^{s}
). We now claim that

(4.20)

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\lambda_{k_{-}}(\beta_{\epsilon})<0\quad\text{ and }\quad\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\lambda_{k_{+}}(\beta_{\epsilon})>0.

It is easily seen that
\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}\to+\infty
as
\epsilon\to 0
by definition of
\beta_{\epsilon}
, so that (
4.19
) follows from (
4.20
). We now prove (
4.20
) for
k=k_{-}
but the proof for
k=k_{+}
is identical. If first
\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\lambda_{k_{-}}(\beta_{\epsilon})\leq-\sigma
then (
4.20
) is obvious. We may thus assume that
-\sigma<\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\lambda_{k_{-}}(\beta_{\epsilon})\leq 0
. Then
[
2
, Theorem 4.1]
applies and shows that, as
\epsilon\to 0
, we have
\lambda_{k_{-}}(\beta_{\epsilon})\to\lambda_{\beta,-}<0
, and (
4.20
) follows.
∎

4.4.
Existence of almost optimisers of renormalised eigenvalue functionals

Let
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
be fixed and
k\geq 1
be an integer. In this subsection and in the next one we investigate the following variational problem:

(4.21)

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sup_{{\beta}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)&\text{ if }k\leq k_{-}\\
&\inf_{{\beta}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)&\text{ if }k\geq k_{+},\end{aligned}\right.

where
k_{-}
and
k_{+}
are defined by (
4.11
) and where
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
is as in (
4.10
). In the special case where
p=\frac{n}{2s}
we let

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=\Lambda_{k}^{s,\frac{n}{2s}}(M,[g]),

where
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is the variational problem introduced in (
1.2
). We prove in this subsection that almost-optimisers for
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])
always exist, in a sense that we describe. We first state our results for almost-minimisers of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
when
k\geq k_{+}
:

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
2s<n
, and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
(M,g)
. Assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). Let
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and
k\geq k_{+}
be fixed. Define
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
. For any
\epsilon>0
there is
\beta_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
with
1\leq\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{p}}\leq 1+\epsilon
such that

\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta_{\epsilon})\leq\Lambda_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])+\epsilon^{2}

and there is an integer
k_{+}\leq\ell_{\epsilon}\leq k
, an orthonormal family
(v_{\ell_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon},\cdots,v_{k}^{\epsilon})
with respect to
Q(\beta_{\epsilon},\cdot)
of eigenfunctions in
E_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})
, positive numbers
d_{\ell_{\epsilon}},\cdots,d_{k}
such that
\sum_{i=\ell_{\epsilon}}^{k}d_{i}^{\epsilon}=1
and a sequence
(f_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon}
of functions in
L^{p^{\prime}}(M)
such that
\|f_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\leq\epsilon
and

\bar{\lambda}^{p}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\sum_{i=\ell_{\epsilon}}^{k}d_{i}^{\epsilon}\left(v_{i}^{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\leq\bar{\lambda}^{p}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\beta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}+f_{\epsilon}\quad\text{ a.e. in }M.

The corresponding version for almost-maximisers when
k\leq k_{-}
is as follows:

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
2s<n
, and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
(M,g)
. Assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). Let
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and
k\leq k_{-}
be fixed. Define
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
. For any
\epsilon>0
there is
\beta_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
with
1\leq\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{p}}\leq 1+\epsilon
such that

\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta_{\epsilon})\geq\Lambda_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])-\epsilon^{2}

and there is an integer
k\leq\ell_{\epsilon}\leq k_{-}
, an orthonormal family
(v_{k}^{\epsilon},\cdots,v_{\ell_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})
with respect to
Q(\beta_{\epsilon},\cdot)
of eigenfunctions in
E_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})
, positive numbers
d_{k},\cdots,d_{\ell_{\epsilon}}
such that
\sum_{i=k}^{\ell_{\epsilon}}d_{i}^{\epsilon}=1
and a sequence
(f_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon}
of functions in
L^{p^{\prime}}(M)
such that
\|f_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\leq\epsilon
and

\bar{\lambda}^{p}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\sum_{i=k}^{\ell_{\epsilon}}d_{i}^{\epsilon}\left(v_{i}^{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\geq\bar{\lambda}^{p}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\beta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}-f_{\epsilon}\quad\text{ a.e. in }M.

Proof.

We only prove Proposition
4.4
for
k\geq k_{+}
, since the proof of Proposition
4.5
in the case
k\leq k_{-}
is similar. We also set, for simplicity,
\bar{\lambda}_{k}=\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
during the proof. Let
\tilde{\beta}_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
be such that

\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\tilde{\beta}_{\epsilon})\leq\Lambda_{k}(M,[g])+\epsilon^{2}\text{ and }\|\tilde{\beta}_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{p}}=1.

By Remark
3.3
,
\bar{\lambda}_{k}
is a continuous map on the complete metric space

\mathcal{A}_{p}^{\prime}=\{\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p};\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}\geq 1\}

endowed with the distance associated to
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}}
. By Ekeland’s variational principle, for any
\epsilon>0
, there is
\beta_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}^{\prime}
such that
\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\leq\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\tilde{\beta}_{\epsilon})
,
\|\beta_{\epsilon}-\tilde{\beta}_{\epsilon}\|\leq\epsilon
and

(4.22)

\forall\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}^{\prime},\quad\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)-\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\geq-\epsilon\|\beta-\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{p}}.

We immediately deduce that
1\leq\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{p}}\leq 1+\epsilon
and that
\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\leq\Lambda_{k}(M,[g])+\epsilon^{2}
.
For
b\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
and
t\geq 0
we choose
\beta=\beta_{\epsilon}+tb
in (
4.22
).Letting
t\to 0
we obtain

(4.23)

\forall b\in\mathcal{A}_{p},\quad\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon}+tb)-\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\varepsilon})}{t}\geq-\epsilon\|b\|_{L^{p}}.

We now let
V\in F\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})
be a linear subspace. We claim that

(4.24)

\begin{split}\{\zeta\in L^{p^{\prime}}(M),\zeta\geq 0\text{ a.e }\}\cap K\neq\emptyset\end{split}

where we have let

K=co\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\left(\beta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}-v^{2}\right)+f;v\in F,Q(\beta_{\epsilon},v)=1,\|f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\leq\epsilon\right\}.

Assume by contradiction that the intersection in (
4.24
) is empty. Since
\{\zeta\in L^{p^{\prime}}(M),\zeta\geq 0\text{ a.e }\}
is closed and
K
is weakly compact in
L^{p^{\prime}}(M)
, the geometric Hahn-Banach theorem shows that there is
b\in L^{p}(M)
such that

(4.25)

\forall\zeta\in L^{p^{\prime}}(M),\zeta\geq 0,\int_{M}\zeta bdv_{g}\geq 0

and there is
\delta>0
such that

(4.26)

\forall\zeta\in K,\int_{M}\zeta bdv_{g}\leq-\delta.

First, (
4.25
) implies that
b\geq 0
a.e, and then (
4.26
) shows that
b\neq 0
, so that
b\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. By Proposition
4.1
we have

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon}+tb)-\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})}{t}\leq\max_{v\in V}\left(\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\int_{M}b(\beta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}-v^{2})dv_{g}\right),

so that there is
v\in V
such that
Q(\beta_{\epsilon},v)=1
and

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon}+tb)-\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})}{t}\leq\int_{M}\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})(\beta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}-v^{2})bdv_{g}.

Using (
4.23
), we obtain that

\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\int_{M}(\beta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}-v^{2})bdv_{g}\geq-\epsilon\|b\|_{L^{p}}.

We let
f=\epsilon b^{p-1}\|b\|_{L^{p}}^{1-p}
. Then
f\in L^{p^{\prime}}(M)
,
\|f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}=\epsilon
and we get

\int_{M}fbdv_{g}=\epsilon\int_{M}b^{p}\|b\|_{L^{p}}^{1-p}=\epsilon\|b\|_{L^{p}},

so that

\int_{M}\left(\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})(\beta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}-v^{2})+f\right)bdv_{g}\geq 0.

This contradicts (
4.26
) for the function
\zeta=\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})(\beta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}-v^{2})+f
. Therefore (
4.24
) holds and
Proposition
4.4
follows from lemma
4.1
since

\begin{split}&co\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\left(\beta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}-v^{2}\right)+f;v\in V,Q(\beta_{\epsilon},v)=1,\|f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\leq\epsilon\right\}\\
&=\left\{\bar{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\left(\beta^{p-1}-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}d_{i}v_{i}^{2}\right)+f;\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}d_{i}=1\text{ and }(v_{i})\in O_{Q(\beta,\cdot)}(V),\|f\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\leq\epsilon\right\}\end{split}

where
O_{Q(\beta,\cdot)}(V)
is the set of orthonormal bases of
V
with respect to the scalar product
Q(\beta,\cdot)
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Notice that Proposition
4.4
and Proposition
4.5
rely on Ekeland’s variational principle. One can refer to
[
52
,
51
]
for a similar use of this principle in dimension 2.

4.5.
Existence of optimisers of the subcritical approximations

In this subsection we consider problem (
4.21
) when
p>\frac{n}{2s}
. Under this assumption the problem becomes subcritical, since the higher integrability of
\beta
allows us to recover the compactness of almost-optimising sequences of generalised eigenfunctions. We prove the following result:

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
2s<n
, and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
in
(M,g)
. Assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). Let
p>\frac{n}{2s}
. Then, for any
k\leq k_{-}
or
k\geq k_{+}
,
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])
defined by (
4.21
) is attained. In other words, there exists
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
such that

\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)={\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sup_{{\beta}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)&\text{ if }k\leq k_{-}\\
&\inf_{{\beta}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}}\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)&\text{ if }k\geq k_{+},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
is given by (
4.10
).

Proof.

We prove Proposition
4.6
in the case
k\geq k_{+}
since the proof when
k\leq k_{-}
is identical. By proposition
4.4
there exists, for any
\epsilon>0
, some
\beta_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
, with
\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{p}}=1+O(\epsilon)
, such that

(4.27)

\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta_{\epsilon})\leq\Lambda_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])+\epsilon^{2}

holds, and there is an integer
k_{+}\leq\ell_{\epsilon}\leq k
, there is a family
(v_{\ell_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon},\cdots,v_{k}^{\epsilon})
in
E_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})
, orthonormal with respect to
Q(\beta_{\epsilon},\cdot)
, there are positive numbers
d_{\ell_{\epsilon}},\cdots,d_{k}
with
\sum_{i=k}^{\ell_{\epsilon}}d_{i}^{\epsilon}=1
and there exists a family of functions
f_{\epsilon}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(M)
, with
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
, such that
\|f_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\leq\epsilon
and

(4.28)

\bar{\lambda}^{p}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\sum_{i=\ell_{\epsilon}}^{k}d_{i}^{\epsilon}\left(v_{i}^{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\leq\bar{\lambda}^{p}_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\beta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}+f_{\epsilon}

a.e. in
M
. Up to passing to a subsequence we may assume that
\ell_{\epsilon}=\ell
is constant. We let
i\in\{\ell,\dots,k\}
. The function
v_{i}^{\epsilon}
satisfies
Q(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{i}^{\epsilon})=1
and

P_{g}^{s}v_{i}^{\epsilon}=\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\beta_{\epsilon}v_{i}^{\epsilon}\quad\text{ in }M.

Lemma
2.2
then applies and shows, since
p>\frac{n}{2s}
, that
(v_{i}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. Up to a subsequence as
\epsilon\to 0
we may thus assume that there is
v_{i}\in H^{s}(M)
such that
v_{i}^{\epsilon}
converges to
v_{i}
weakly in
H^{s}(M)
and strongly in
H^{s-1}(M)
. Let
\beta
be the weak limit of
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in
L^{p}(M)
. Then
\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}\leq 1
and it is easily seen that
v_{i}
satisfies

P_{g}^{s}v_{i}=\nu_{k}\beta v_{i}\quad\text{ in }M,

where we have let
\nu_{k}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})
. Lemma
2.2
also shows that
\nu_{k}>0
and by standard elliptic theory and since
p>\frac{n}{2s}
(see again
[
41
, Theorem 5]
), we have
v_{i}\in L^{\infty}(M)
. We now claim that
v_{i}^{\epsilon}
strongly converges to
v_{i}
in
H^{s}(M)
, as
\epsilon\to 0
, up to taking a subsequence. Indeed,
v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i}
satisfies

(4.29)

\displaystyle P_{g}^{s}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})

\displaystyle=\big(\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})-\nu_{k}\big)\beta_{\epsilon}v_{i}^{\epsilon}+\nu_{k}\beta_{\epsilon}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})

\displaystyle+\nu_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta)v_{i}

in
M
. Since
\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta
weakly converges to
0
in
L^{p}(M)
and
v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i}
strongly converges to
0
in
L^{p^{\prime}}(M)
by Sobolev’s embedding, we have

\left|\int_{M}(\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta)v_{i}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})dv_{g}\right|\leq\|v_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{M}|\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta||v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i}|dv_{g}=o(1)

as
\epsilon\to 0
. Independently, and since
p>\frac{n}{2s}
, we have
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}<\frac{n}{n-2s}
. Sobolev’s embeddings then show that the sequence
\big((v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{0})^{2}\big)_{\epsilon>0}
strongly converges to
0
in
L^{p^{\prime}}(M)
. Since
(\beta_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon}
is bounded in
L^{p}(M)
we then have

\int_{M}\nu_{k}\beta_{\epsilon}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})^{2}dv_{g}=o(1)

as
\epsilon\to 0
. Integrating (
4.29
) against
v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i}
thus gives, by (
2.8
) and since
\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\to\nu_{k}
,

\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})\big|^{2}dv_{g}=o(1)

as
\epsilon\to 0
, which shows that
v_{i}^{\epsilon}\to v_{i}
in
H^{s}(M)
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Since
v_{i}^{\epsilon}\to v_{i}
in
H^{s}(M)
, and since
(v_{\ell}^{\epsilon},\cdots,v_{k}^{\epsilon})
is
Q(\beta_{\epsilon},\cdot)
-orthonormal, we obtain at the limit that
(v_{\ell},\cdots,v_{k})
is
Q(\beta,\cdot)
-orthonormal. We may now pass equation (
4.28
) to the weak limit as
\epsilon\to 0
. Up to taking a subsequence we may assume that
d_{i}^{\epsilon}\to d_{i}
for all
\ell\leq i\leq k
with
\sum_{i=k}^{\ell}d_{i}=1
.
Since
(v_{i}^{\epsilon})^{2}
strongly converges to
v_{i}^{2}
in
L^{p^{\prime}}(M)
as

\epsilon\to 0
we have that
\left(\sum_{i=\ell}^{k}d_{i}^{\epsilon}\left(v_{i}^{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}

strongly converges to
\left(\sum_{i=\ell}^{k}d_{i}v_{i}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}
in
L^{p}(M)
.
Since pointwise inequalities are preserved under weak convergence, and since
\nu_{k}>0
we obtain that

(4.30)

\sum_{i=\ell}^{k}d_{i}v_{i}^{2}\leq\beta^{p-1}\quad\text{ a.e. in }M.

Multiplying the latter inequality by
\beta
and integrating over
M
then shows that

1=\int_{M}\sum_{i=\ell}^{k}d_{i}\beta v_{i}^{2}dv_{g}\leq\int_{M}\beta^{p}dv_{g}\leq 1.

All these inequalities are thus equalities, so that
\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}=1=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{p}}
and therefore, since
p>1
and
L^{p}(M)
is uniformly convex,
\beta_{\epsilon}
strongly converges to
\beta
in
L^{p}(M)
as
\epsilon\to 0
.
By lower semi-continuity of positive eigenvalues in
\mathcal{A}_{p}
(Proposition
3.5
), we obtain
\nu_{k}\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. Passing (
4.27
) to the limit as
\epsilon\to 0
finally shows that
\Lambda_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])\leq\nu_{k}
, and since
\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}=1
we thus obtain that

\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)\leq\Lambda_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])

which concludes the proof of Proposition
4.6
.
∎

We conclude this subsection by a simple result that will be used in the proof of Theorem
1.2
in Section
6
:

Proposition 4.7
.

Assume that
k\leq k_{-}
or
k\geq k_{+}
and let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
2s<n
. As
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
from above we have

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])\to{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g]).

Proof.

Let
p>\frac{n}{2s}
and
\beta\in L^{p}(M)
. By Hölder’s inequality we have
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}\leq\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}\text{Vol}_{g}(M)^{\frac{2sp-n}{np}}
. Since
\text{Vol}_{g}(M)^{\frac{2sp-n}{np}}=1+O\big(\big|p-\frac{n}{2s}\big|\big)
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
this implies

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\leq{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])\Big[1+O\big(\big|p-\frac{n}{2s}\big|\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\text{ if }k\geq k_{+}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\geq{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])\Big[1+O\big(\big|p-\frac{n}{2s}\big|\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\text{ if }k\leq k_{-},

from which we deduce that

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\leq\liminf_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])

\displaystyle\text{ if }k\geq k_{+}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\geq\limsup_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])

\displaystyle\text{ if }k\leq k_{-}.

For the other inequality we only treat the
k\geq k_{+}
case, since the case
k\leq k_{-}
is similar. Let
\epsilon>0
and let
\beta
be a positive and continuous function in
M
such that
\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}\leq{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])+\epsilon
. By definition,
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}
. Since
\beta>0
in
M
we have
\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}\to\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, and thus

\limsup_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}\leq{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])+\epsilon.

Letting
\epsilon\to 0
proves that
\limsup_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])\leq{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
. ∎

4.6.
The first generalised eigenvalue of a coercive operator

We conclude this section by investigating the first generalised eigenvalue when the operator
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive in
H^{s}(M)
. We recall that
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive if
v\in H^{s}(M)\mapsto\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}v\,dv_{g}
is equivalent to the
H^{s}(M)
norm or, equivalently, if there exists
C>0
such that

(4.31)

\|u\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\leq C\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}v\,dv_{g}\quad\text{ for any }u\in H^{s}(M).

This is the case if
(M,g)
is the round sphere
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
and, more generally, if
(M,g)
is an Einstein manifold
(M,g)
of positive scalar curvature: this follows for instance from (
2.9
). We prove that in this case the invariant
\Lambda_{1}^{s}(M,[g])
defined in (
1.2
) coincides with the Rayleigh quotient of
P_{g}^{s}
:

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
and
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
, and we assume that
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive. Then

(4.32)

\Lambda_{1}^{s}(M,[g])=\inf_{u\in C^{\infty}(M)\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\int_{M}uP_{g}^{s}udv_{g}}{\left(\int_{M}|u|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}}.

Remark that if
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive we have
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
, so that (
1.3
) is automatically satisfied. The coercivity assumption is equivalent to requiring that
\lambda_{1}(P_{g}^{s})>0
.

Proof.

We let
I(M,[g])
denote the right-hand side in (
4.32
). Let
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
, with
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
. By (
3.2
) it is easy to see that

(4.33)

\lambda_{1}(\beta)=\inf_{v\in H^{s}(M),\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}v\not\equiv 0}\frac{\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}vdv_{g}}{\int_{M}\beta v^{2}dv_{g}}.

If
v
satisfies
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}v\not\equiv 0
, Hölder’s inequality gives
\int_{M}\beta v^{2}dv_{g}\leq\Big(\int_{M}|v|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\Big)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}
, so that
\lambda_{1}(\beta)\geq I(M,[g])
. Taking the infimum over
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
with
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
yields
\Lambda_{1}^{s}(M,[g])\geq I(M,[g])
. For the reverse inequality, we let
v\in C^{\infty}(M)
with
v>0
, and let
