\beta=v^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}
. By (
4.33
) we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}\leq\frac{\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}vdv_{g}}{\int_{M}v^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}}\left(\int_{M}v^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n}}=\frac{\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}vdv_{g}}{\left(\int_{M}v^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}}.

Taking the infimum over all
v>0
and then over all
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
yields
\Lambda_{1}^{s}(M,[g])\leq I(M,[g])
.
∎

We recall the definition of the best constant for Sobolev’s inequality in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
given by (
2.18
):

\frac{1}{K_{n,s}^{2}}=\inf_{u\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\Delta_{\xi}^{\frac{s}{2}}u\right|^{2}dv_{\xi}}{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{\xi}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}}.

We recall (see
[
38
]
) that
K_{n,s}^{-2}
is attained by a
n+2
-dimensional family of smooth functions which, up to translation, dilation and multiplication by a nonzero constant, coincide with

U_{0}(x)=\left(1+|x|^{2}\right)^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}\quad\text{ for }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Remark that for any function
u\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\Delta_{\xi}^{\frac{s}{2}}u\right|^{2}dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u(\Delta_{\xi})^{s}udv_{\xi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}uP_{\xi}^{s}udv_{\xi}
. If
g_{0}
denotes the round metric on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, using the stereographic projection and the conformal invariance property of
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
shows that we also have

\frac{1}{K_{n,s}^{2}}=\inf_{u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\backslash\{0\}}\frac{\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}uP_{g_{0}}^{s}udv_{g_{0}}}{\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}|u|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g_{0}}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}}.

Proposition
4.8
then shows that

(4.34)

\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n},[g_{0}])=K_{n,s}^{-2}

and we again obtain with the result of
[
38
]
that
\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n},[g_{0}])
is attained for every
s\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
. Together with (
4.34
), assumption (
2.19
) in the statement of Lemma
2.3
can be reinterpreted in the following way: if
({\beta}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is a sequence of nonnegative functions that is uniformly bounded in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
its set of
set of concentration points
is defined as

(4.35)

A=\Biggl\{x\in M\,\Big|\,\forall{\delta}>0,\;\limsup_{i\to+\infty}\int_{B_{g}(x,{\delta})}{\beta}_{i}^{\frac{n}{2s}}\,dv_{g}>\frac{1}{2}\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}\Biggr\}.

As we will see in the proof of Theorem
1.2
in Section
6
, condition (
4.35
) ensures that the invariants
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
, when they are not attained, satisfy rigid quantification properties.

5.
Maximisation of negative eigenvalues: proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section we show that negative eigenvalues can be always be maximised in a fixed conformal class. Let
s\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
and
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
, and we assume that it satisfies (
1.3
). Let
k\leq k_{-}
be an integer. The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.1
i.e. to prove that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<0
and
\Lambda_{k}(M,[g])
is attained at a generalised metric
\tilde{g}
. We recall that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is defined in (
1.2
).

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The proof follows the same lines than the proof of Proposition
4.6
, with a few complications due to the criticality of the exponent
p=\frac{n}{2s}
. We will prove that the compactness of sequences of generalised eigenfunctions in
H^{s}(M)
follows this time from the negativity of the eigenvalues. By Proposition
3.5
we have

\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=\sup_{\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}}\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}.

Since
k\leq k_{-}
we have
\lambda_{k}(\beta)<0
for any
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
. We first prove that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<0
. Let for this
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
,
\beta_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
,
\|\beta_{i}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
be a sequence satisfying
\lambda_{k}(\beta_{i})\to\Lambda_{k}(M,[g])
. By Proposition
3.4
we can let
(\varphi_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be an associated sequence of generalised eigenvectors satisfying
Q(\beta_{i},\varphi_{i})=1
and

P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}=\lambda_{k}(\beta_{i})\beta_{i}\varphi_{i}\quad\text{ in }M.

Since
\lambda_{k}(\beta_{i})<0
, Lemma
2.2
applies and shows that

\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=\limsup_{i\to+\infty}\lambda_{k}(\beta_{i})<0.

We now prove that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained. We apply for this Proposition
4.4
: for any
\epsilon>0
, it shows that there exists
\beta_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
,
\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1+O(\epsilon)
, such that

(5.1)

\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\geq\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])-\epsilon^{2}

holds, and there is an integer
k\leq\ell_{\epsilon}\leq k_{-}
, there is a family
(v_{k}^{\epsilon},\cdots,v_{\ell_{\epsilon}}^{\epsilon})
in
E_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})
, orthonormal with respect to
Q(\beta_{\epsilon},\cdot)
, there exist positive numbers
d_{\ell_{\epsilon}},\cdots,d_{k}
with
\sum_{i=k}^{\ell_{\epsilon}}d_{i}^{\epsilon}=1
and there exists a family of functions
f_{\epsilon}\in L^{\frac{n}{n-2s}}(M)
such that
\|f_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{n-2s}}}\leq\epsilon
and

(5.2)

\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\sum_{i=k}^{\ell_{\epsilon}}d_{i}^{\epsilon}\left(v_{i}^{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\geq\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\beta_{\epsilon}^{\frac{n-2s}{2s}}-f_{\epsilon}

a.e. in
M
. Up to passing to a subsequence we may assume that
\ell_{\epsilon}=\ell
is constant. We let
i\in\{k,\dots,\ell\}
. The function
v_{i}^{\epsilon}
satisfies
Q(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{i}^{\epsilon})=1
and

P_{g}^{s}v_{i}^{\epsilon}=\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\beta_{\epsilon}v_{i}^{\epsilon}\quad\text{ in }M.

Lemma
2.2
then applies and shows, since
\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})<0
, that
(v_{i}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. Up to a subsequence we may thus assume that there is
v_{i}\in H^{s}(M)
such that
v_{i}^{\epsilon}
converges to
v_{i}
weakly in
H^{s}(M)
and strongly in
H^{s-1}(M)
. Let
\beta
be the weak limit of
(\beta_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
. Then
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}\leq 1
and it is easily seen that
v_{i}
satisfies

(5.3)

P_{g}^{s}v_{i}=\nu_{k}\beta v_{i}\quad\text{ in }M,

where we have let
\nu_{k}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})
. By Lemma
2.2
we again have
\nu_{k}<0
. By (
5.3
), classical regularity results for higher-order elliptic equations (see again Mazumdar
[
41
, Theorem 5]
) show that
v_{i}\in L^{q}(M)
for any
1\leq q<+\infty
.

We first prove that
v_{i}^{\epsilon}
strongly converges to
v_{i}
in
H^{s}(M)
as
\epsilon\to 0
. The function
v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i}
satisfies

(5.4)

\displaystyle P_{g}^{s}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})

\displaystyle=\big(\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})-\nu_{k}\big)\beta_{\epsilon}v_{i}^{\epsilon}+\nu_{k}\beta_{\epsilon}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})

\displaystyle+\nu_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta)v_{i}

in
M
. First, since
\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})\to\nu_{k}
as
\epsilon\to 0
, Hölder’s inequality shows that

\int_{M}\big(\lambda_{k}(\beta_{\epsilon})-\nu_{k}\big)\beta_{\epsilon}v_{i}^{\epsilon}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})dv_{g}=o(1)

as
\epsilon\to 0
. Let now
R>0
be fixed. We write

\displaystyle\int_{M}(\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta)v_{i}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{\{|v_{i}|\leq R\}}(\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta)v_{i}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})dv_{g}

\displaystyle+\int_{\{|v_{i}|\geq R\}}(\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta)v_{i}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})dv_{g}.

On the one hand, since
\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta
converges weakly to
0
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
and
v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i}
converges strongly to
0
in
L^{\frac{n}{n-2s}}(M)
by Sobolev’s embedding, we have

\left|\int_{\{|v_{i}|\leq R\}}(\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta)v_{i}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})dv_{g}\right|\leq R\int_{M}|\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta||v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i}|dv_{g}=o(1)

as
\epsilon\to 0
. On the other hand, since
(v_{i}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
and
(\beta_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
is bounded in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
, Hölder’s inequality shows that

\left|\int_{\{|v_{i}|\geq R\}}(\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta)v_{i}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})dv_{g}\right|\leq C\left(\int_{\{|v_{i}|\geq R\}}|v_{i}|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{2n}}.

Combining these two estimates and letting
R\to+\infty
thus gives

\limsup_{\epsilon\to+\infty}\left|\int_{M}(\beta_{\epsilon}-\beta)v_{i}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})dv_{g}\right|=0.

With the previous arguments, integrating (
5.4
) against
v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i}
we thus obtain, by (
2.8
),

\int_{M}\big|\Delta_{g}^{\frac{s}{2}}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})\big|^{2}dv_{g}-\nu_{k}\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}(v_{i}^{\epsilon}-v_{i})^{2}dv_{g}=o(1)

as
\epsilon\to 0
. Since
\nu_{k}<0
and
\beta_{\epsilon}\geq 0
this shows that
v_{i}^{\epsilon}
strongly converges to
v_{i}
in
H^{s}(M)
. The relations
B(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{i}^{\epsilon},v_{j}^{\epsilon})=\delta_{i,j}
, for any
k\leq i,j\leq\ell
, now pass to the limit as
\epsilon\to 0
and show that
B(\beta,v_{i},v_{j})=\delta_{i,j}
, which shows in particular that
\beta\neq 0
.

We may now pass equation (
5.2
) to the weak limit as
\epsilon\to 0
. Up to taking a subsequence, we let
d_{i}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}d_{i}^{\epsilon}
, so that we again have
\sum_{i=k}^{\ell}d_{i}=1
. Since
(v_{i}^{\epsilon})^{2}
strongly converges to
v_{i}^{2}
in
L^{\frac{n}{n-2s}}(M)
,
then
\left(\sum_{i=k}^{\ell}d_{i}^{\epsilon}\left(v_{i}^{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n-2s}}
converges strongly to
\left(\sum_{i=k}^{\ell}d_{i}v_{i}^{2}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n-2s}}
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
. Then, since pointwise inequalities are preserved under weak convergence, and since
\nu_{k}<0
, we obtain that

\beta^{\frac{n-2s}{2s}}\geq\sum_{i=k}^{\ell}d_{i}v_{i}^{2}\quad\text{ a.e. in }M.

Multiplying the latter inequality by
\beta
and integrating over
M
then shows that

1=\int_{M}\sum_{i=k}^{\ell}d_{i}\beta v_{i}^{2}dv_{g}\leq\int_{M}\beta^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}\leq 1.

All these inequalities are thus equalities, so that
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}
and therefore, since
n>2s
and
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
is uniformly convex,
\beta_{\epsilon}
strongly converges to
\beta
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
as
\epsilon\to 0
.
We can thus apply Proposition
3.2
, and we get that
\nu_{k}\leq\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. Using (
5.1
) this finally gives

\lambda_{k}(\beta)\geq\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g]).

By definition of
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
and since
\|\beta\|_{\frac{n}{2s}}=1
we thus have
\lambda_{k}(\beta)=\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
, which shows that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained at
\beta
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
∎

6.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

We prove in this section the main result of this paper, Theorem
1.2
. Unlike in the case of negative eigenvalues, the situation for positive ones is much more complicated since bubbling phenomena may occur. We prove Theorem
1.2
in several steps. As a first result, we prove that the following large inequality always holds:

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
and
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
. Let
k\geq k_{+}
be an integer, where
k_{+}
is defined in (
4.11
). Then

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\leq X_{k}^{s}(M,[g]),

where
X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is as in (
1.5
).

We then prove that equality holds if
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is
not
attained. Following Proposition
3.5
we will say that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is not attained if for every
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
we have
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}
. We recall that if
g_{0}
is the round metric in
\mathbb{S}^{n}
we write
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n},[g_{0}])=\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
. The main result that we prove in this section is the following:

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
and
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
and we assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). Let
k\geq k_{+}
be an integer, where
k_{+}
is defined in (
4.11
). Assume that
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is not attained. Then:

•

Either there exist
\ell_{0}\in\{k_{+},\cdots,k-1\}
and
\ell_{1}\in\{1,\cdots,k-1\}
with
\ell_{0}+\ell_{1}=k
such that
\Lambda_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained and

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}\geq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}

•

or

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\geq{\Lambda}_{k-{k_{+}}+1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n}).

Furthermore, in the case where
(M,g)
is the round sphere
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
, only the first alternative occurs.

Note that the first case can only occur if
k\geq k_{+}+1
. Theorem
6.2
implies Theorem
1.2
as we now show:

Proof of Theorem
1.2
assuming Theorem
6.2
.

First, the inequality
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\leq X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
follows from Proposition
6.1
. We now assume that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])<X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
. We proceed by contradiction and assume that
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is
not
attained at a generalised metric. In this case Theorem
6.2
applies.

Assume first that
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}\geq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}
for some indexes
\ell_{0}\in\{k_{+},\cdots,k-1\}
and
\ell_{1}\in\{1,\cdots,k-1\}
such that
\ell_{0}+\ell_{1}=k
and such that
\Lambda_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained. If
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is also attained then, by definition of
X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
in (
1.5
), we have
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\geq X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
, which is a contradiction. Thus
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is not attained and we may then apply again Theorem
6.2
to the
\ell_{1}
-th generalised eigenvalue for the round sphere
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
. In this case only the first alternative occurs, and since
k_{+}=1
for
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
, we obtain that

{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}\geq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{2}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{3}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}

for some
\ell_{2}\geq 1
and
1\leq\ell_{3}\leq\ell_{1}-1
, where
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{2}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is attained. If
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{3}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is attained then we have again shown that
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\geq X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
which leads to a contradiction. Hence
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{3}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is not attained. We may thus repeat this argument by induction: after a finite number of iterations, we reach a contradiction since
\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
is attained (this follows from (
4.34
) and the result in
[
38
]
).

Assume then that
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\geq{\Lambda}_{k-{k_{+}}+1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
. If
{\Lambda}_{k-{k_{+}}+1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is attained then we again have
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\geq X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
by definition of
X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
, which is a contradiction. Otherwise the proof follows from an inductive application of Theorem
6.2
to the conformal eigenvalues of
(\mathbb{S}^{n},[g_{0}])
as in the previous case. This concludes the proof of
1.2
.
∎

We prove Proposition
6.1
and Theorem
6.2
in the next subsections.

6.1.
Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

We prove a slightly more general result. We define

(6.1)

Y_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=\inf\left\{\left({\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\cdots+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{r}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n}}\right\}

where the infimum is taken over the set of all
r,\ell_{0},\cdots,\ell_{r}\in\doba{N}
such that

(1)

\ell_{0}\in\{0\}\cup\{k_{+},\cdots,k-1\}
where by convention
\Lambda_{0}^{s}(M,[g])=0
,

(2)

\ell_{0}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=k
if
\ell_{0}\geq k_{+}
and
\ell_{0}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=k-k_{+}+1
if
\ell_{0}=0
.

In other words,
Y_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is a rougher version than
X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
defined in (
1.5
): it is defined similarly to
X_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
but without assuming that
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])
or the
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{i}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
are attained. As a consequence, we easily have

(6.2)

Y_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\leq X_{k}^{s}(M,[g]).

In this subsection we prove the following result:

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
and
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
. We let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
. Let
k\geq k_{+}
be an integer, where
k_{+}
is defined in (
4.11
). Then

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\leq Y_{k}^{s}(M,[g]).

Proposition
6.1
then follows from (
6.2
) and from Proposition
6.3
.

Proof.

Let
r,\ell_{0},\cdots,\ell_{r}\in\doba{N}
be such that

(6.3)

\ell_{0}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=k

if
\ell_{0}\geq k_{+}
and

(6.4)

\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=k-k_{+}+1

if
\ell_{0}=0
. We fix
\epsilon>0
and we let
{\beta}_{0}\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)\backslash\{0\},\beta_{0}\geq 0
a.e., and
{\beta}_{1},\cdots,{\beta}_{r}\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\backslash\{0\},\beta_{i}\geq 0
a.e. for all
1\leq i\leq r
satisfy the following:

•

For all
1\leq i\leq r
,

(6.5)

\inf_{V\in\mathcal{G}^{{\beta}_{i}}_{\ell_{i}}(H^{s}(\doba{S}^{n}))}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{i},v)\leq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{i}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})+\epsilon.

•

if (
6.3
) holds, that is if
\ell_{0}\geq k_{+}
, then

(6.6)

\inf_{V\in\mathcal{G}^{{\beta}_{0}}_{\ell_{0}}(H^{s}(M))}\max_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{0},v)\leq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])+\epsilon

In the case where
\ell_{0}=0
, we let
\beta_{0}\equiv\text{Vol}(M,g)^{-\frac{2s}{n}}
. Without loss of generality, and by Proposition
3.5
, we can assume that
{\beta}_{i}>0
a.e. for any
1\leq i\leq r
and that
\|{\beta}_{i}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
.
By Proposition
3.4
, for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,r\}
and
j\in\{1,\cdots,\ell_{i}\}
, there exists
v_{j}^{i}\in H^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
and

{\lambda}_{j}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, with
\lambda_{1}^{i}\leq\cdots\leq{\lambda}_{\ell_{i}}^{i}
such that for all
1\leq p,q\leq\ell_{i}
we have
\int_{\doba{S}^{n}}{\beta}_{i}v^{i}_{p}v^{i}_{q}\,dv_{g_{0}}=\delta_{pq}
and
P_{g_{0}}^{s}v_{p}^{i}={\lambda}_{p}^{i}{\beta}_{i}v_{p}^{i}
in
\doba{S}^{n}
. Furthermore, for any
1\leq i\leq r
, if we let
V_{i}=\hbox{span}\{v^{i}_{1}\cdots,v^{i}_{\ell_{i}}\}
, then
V_{i}\in\mathcal{G}^{{\beta}_{i}}_{\ell_{i}}(H^{s}(\doba{S}^{n}))
and we have

\max_{v\in V_{i}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{i},v)=\inf_{V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{{\beta}_{i}}_{\ell_{i}}(H^{s}(\doba{S}^{n}))}\max_{v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{i},v),

as well as
\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{i},v_{1}^{i})=\min_{v\in V_{i}}\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{i},v)
and
\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{i},v^{i}_{\ell_{i}})=\max_{v\in V_{i}}\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{i},v)
. Similarly, if (
6.3
) holds (which implies
\ell_{0}\geq k_{+}
), for any
j\in\{1,\cdots,\ell_{0}\}
, there exists
v_{j}^{0}\in H^{s}(M)
and
{\lambda}_{j}^{0}=\lambda_{j}(\beta_{0})\in\mathbb{R}
, with
\lambda_{1}^{0}\leq\cdots\leq{\lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{0}
such that for all
1\leq p,q\leq\ell_{0}
we have,
\int_{M}{\beta}_{0}v^{0}_{p}v^{0}_{q}\,dv_{g}=\delta_{pq}
,
P_{g}^{s}v_{p}^{0}={\lambda}_{p}^{0}{\beta}_{0}v_{p}^{0}
in
M
and

\max_{v\in V_{0}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{0},v)=\inf_{V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}^{{\beta}_{0}}_{\ell_{0}}(H^{s}(M))}\max_{v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{0},v)

where we have let
V_{0}=\hbox{span}\{v^{0}_{1}\cdots,v^{0}_{\ell_{0}}\}
. In particular by (
6.5
) and (
6.6
) we have

(6.7)

{\lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{0}\leq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])+\epsilon\;\hbox{ and }{\lambda}_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\leq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{i}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})+\epsilon\quad\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq r.

Let
p\in\doba{S}^{n}
be fixed. The manifolds
(\doba{S}^{n}\setminus\{p\},g_{0})
and
(\doba{R}^{n},\xi)
are conformally equivalent. Precisely, if
\pi
denote the stereographic projection that sends
-p
to
0
we have
(\pi^{-1})^{*}g_{0}(x)=U(x)^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}\xi
, where
\xi
is the Euclidean metric in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and where we have let
U(x)=\left(\frac{2}{1+|x|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{2s}}
. For
1\leq i\leq r
and
1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}
we define

y_{j}^{i}(x)=\frac{v_{j}^{i}}{U}\big(\pi^{-1}(x)\big)\quad\text{ and }\quad\tilde{\beta}_{i}(x)=U(x)^{-\frac{4s}{n-2s}}\beta_{i}(\pi^{-1}(x))\quad\text{ for any }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

By the conformal invariance property (
2.7
) of
P_{g_{0}}
, each function
y_{j}^{i}
belongs to
D^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(see (
A.1
) below
for the definition of
D^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
) so that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\Delta_{\xi}^{\frac{s}{2s}}y_{j}^{i}|^{2}\,dv_{\xi}<+\infty
, and they satisfy

(6.8)

P^{s}_{\xi}y_{j}^{i}={\lambda}_{j}^{i}\tilde{{\beta}}_{i}y_{j}^{i}

in
\doba{R}^{n}
and

(6.9)

\int_{\doba{R}^{n}}\tilde{{\beta}}_{i}y_{j_{1}}^{i}y_{j_{2}}^{i}\,dv_{\xi}=\delta_{j_{1}j_{2}}\quad\hbox{ and }\quad\int_{\doba{R}^{n}}\tilde{{\beta}}_{i}^{\frac{n}{2s}}\,dv_{\xi}=1.

For
a>0
and for any
1\leq i\leq r
we now let

z_{j}^{i,a}=a^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}y_{j}^{i}(ax)\quad\text{ and }\quad{\beta}_{i,a}=a^{2s}\tilde{{\beta}}_{i}(ax)

in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As one easily checks,
z_{j}^{i,a}
and
{\beta}_{i,a}
still satisfy (
6.8
) and (
6.9
). We now let
x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}
be distinct points in
M
. Let
{\delta}>0
be small and let
\eta_{i}\in C^{\infty}(M)
a cut-off function equal to
1
in
B_{g}(x_{i},{\delta})
and
0
outside
B_{g}(x_{i},2{\delta})
. If
{\delta}
is small enough the supports of the
\eta_{i}
are disjoint. We now define

(6.10)

\mu_{i}={\lambda}_{\ell_{i}}^{i}\quad\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq r\quad\text{ and }\quad\mu_{0}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &{\lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{0}&\text{ if }\ell_{0}\geq k_{+}\\
&0&\text{ if }\ell_{0}=0\end{aligned}\right..

and we let, for
a>0
,

{\beta}_{a}=\mu_{0}\beta_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}\eta_{i}\beta_{i,a}\circ{\mathop{\rm exp}}_{x_{i}}^{-1}

where
{\mathop{\rm exp}}_{x_{i}}
denotes the exponential chart at
x_{i}
for the metric
g
. We also let

V^{i,a}_{j}=\eta_{i}z^{i,a}_{j}\circ{\mathop{\rm exp}}_{x_{i}}^{-1}\quad\text{ for }1\leq i\leq r,1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}.

In case
\ell_{0}\geq k_{+}
we let
V_{j}^{0,a}=v^{0}_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq\ell_{0}
. If
\ell_{0}=0
we let
\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1}
be the
k_{+}-1
first eigenfunctions of
P_{g}^{s}
in
M
and we let
V_{j}^{0,a}=\varphi_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq k_{+}-1
. With these definitions and since the support of the
\eta_{i}
are disjoint, it is easy to check that for all
0\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq r
and
1\leq j\leq\ell_{i},1\leq j^{\prime}\leq\ell_{i^{\prime}}
(
1\leq j\leq k_{+}-1
if
\ell_{0}=0
) we have

(6.11)

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to+\infty}\int_{M}{\beta}_{a}^{\frac{n}{2s}}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{r}\left(\mu_{i}\right)^{\frac{n}{2s}}

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to+\infty}\int_{M}{\beta}_{a}V_{j}^{i,a}V_{j^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime},a}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\mu_{i}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to+\infty}\int_{M}P_{g}^{s}V_{j}^{i,a}V_{j^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime},a}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle={\lambda}_{j}^{i}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}.

For
a
large enough we define

V_{a}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &{\mathop{\rm Span}}\big\{V_{i,a}^{j}|i\in\{0,\cdots,r\},j\in\{0,\cdots,\ell_{i}\}\big\}&\text{ if }\ell_{0}\geq k_{+}\\
&{\mathop{\rm Span}}\big\{V_{i,a}^{j},V_{0,a}^{p}|i\in\{1,\cdots,r\},j\in\{0,\cdots,\ell_{i}\},p\in\{1,\cdots,k_{+}-1\}\big\}&\text{ if }\ell_{0}=0\\
\end{aligned}\right..

By (
6.11
) and by the definition of
\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{r}
we have
\dim_{\beta_{a}}V_{a}=k
so that
V_{a}\in\mathcal{G}_{k}^{\beta_{a}}(H^{s}(M))
. In particular,
we get that

(6.12)

{\Lambda}_{k}(M,[g])\leq\max_{v\in V_{a}}\mathcal{R}({\beta}_{a},v)\|\beta_{a}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}.

First, using (
6.7
), (
6.10
) and (
6.11
) we have

\lim_{a\to+\infty}\int_{M}\beta_{a}^{\frac{n}{2s}}\,dv_{g}\leq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\cdots+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{r}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+(r+1)\epsilon,

where we used the convention that
\Lambda_{0}^{s}(M,[g])=0
. Let now
{\alpha}_{a,ij}\in\doba{R}
, where the indexes
i
and
j
are as in the definition of
V_{a}
, be chosen so such that
\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{a,ij}^{2}=1
and

v_{a}=\sum_{i,j}{\alpha}_{a,ij}V_{j}^{i,a}

attains the maximum in the right-hand side of (
6.12
). Up to a subsequence as
a\to+\infty
we can assume that
\alpha_{a,ij}\to\alpha_{ij}
, with again
\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i,j}^{2}=1
. Using (
6.10
) and (
6.11
) we have

\lim_{a\to+\infty}\int_{M}\beta_{a}v_{a}^{2}\,dv_{g}=\sum_{i,j}{\alpha}_{ij}^{2}\mu_{i},

while using (
6.10
) and since
{\lambda}_{j}^{i}\leq\mu_{i}
we get

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to+\infty}\int_{M}v_{a}P_{g}^{s}v_{a}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}{\alpha}_{ij}^{2}{\lambda}_{j}^{i}\leq\sum_{i,j}{\alpha}_{ij}^{2}\mu_{i}.

Combining these estimates into (
6.12
) finally gives

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}\leq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\cdots+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{r}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+(r+1)\epsilon.

Since
r,\ell_{0},\cdots,\ell_{r}
and
\epsilon
are arbitrary, we obtain that

(6.13)

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\leq Y_{k}^{s}(M,[g])

which concludes the proof of Proposition
6.1
.
∎

6.2.
Proof of Theorem
6.2
.

In this subsection we let
k\geq k_{+}
be fixed and we assume that
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is not attained. We prove Theorem
6.2
by proving an energy-quantification identity for a suitable minimising sequence for
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
that we construct.

For any
p>\frac{n}{2s}
, we consider the subcritical approximation
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])
of
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
given by (
4.21
). Proposition
4.6
shows that, for each
p>\frac{n}{2s}
,
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])
is attained: that is, there exists
\beta_{p}\in L^{p}(M)
, with
\|\beta_{p}\|_{L^{p}}=1
, such that
\lambda_{k}(\beta_{p})={\Lambda}_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])
. Proposition
4.2
then shows there exists an integer
k_{+}\leq\ell_{p}\leq k
with
\lambda_{\ell_{p}}(\beta_{p})=\lambda_{k}(\beta_{p})
, a
Q(\beta_{p},\cdot)
-orthonormal family
(v_{\ell_{p},p},\cdots,v_{k,p})
in
E_{k}(\beta_{p})
and there exist positive numbers
d_{\ell_{p},p},\cdots,d_{k,p}
satisfying
\sum_{i=\ell_{p}}^{k}d_{i,p}=1
such that

(6.14)

\beta_{p}^{p-1}=\sum_{i=\ell_{p}}^{k}d_{i,p}v_{i,p}^{2}\quad\text{ a. e. in }M.

Up to passing to a subsequence as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
from above we may assume that
\ell_{p}
is constant and equal to
\ell
. If
k=k_{+}
, we have
\ell=k
. By Proposition
3.4
we can let
(v_{k_{+},p},\cdots,v_{\ell-1,p})
be a family of eigenfunctions associated to
\lambda_{k_{+}}(\beta_{p}),\cdots,\lambda_{\ell-1}(\beta_{p})
such that the family
(v_{k_{+},p},\cdots,v_{k,p})
is
Q(\beta_{p},\cdot)
-orthonormal. We thus have

(6.15)

P_{g}^{s}v_{i,p}=\lambda_{i,p}\beta_{p}v_{i,p}\quad\text{ in }M

and

(6.16)

\int_{M}\beta_{p}v_{i,p}v_{j,p}\,dv_{g}=\delta_{ij}.

for all
k_{+}\leq i,j\leq k
, where we have let
\lambda_{i,p}=\lambda_{i}(\beta_{p})
for simplicity. We perform in what follows an asymptotic analysis of the sequences
(\beta_{p})_{p}
and
(v_{k_{+},p},\cdots,v_{k,p})_{p}
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
from above.

First, up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that the
(d_{i,p})_{\ell\leq i\leq k}
and the
({\lambda}_{i,p})_{k_{+}\leq i\leq k}
converge, respectively to
d_{i}\in[0,1]
and
{\lambda}_{i}\in[0,{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])]
, as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. By the assumption
\sum_{i=\ell}^{k}d_{i}=1
there exists at least one
i\in\{\ell,\cdots,k\}
such that
d_{i}>0
. Proposition
4.7
also implies that

(6.17)

{\lambda}_{k}=\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}{\lambda}_{k,p}={\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g]).

An important consequence of Lemma
2.2
is that for all
k_{+}\leq i\leq k

(6.18)

{\lambda}_{i}>0,\quad\text{ and in particular }\quad{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])>0.

Since, for each
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
, the sequence
(\lambda_{i,p})_{p}
is bounded, Lemma
2.2
applies and shows that for any
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
the following dichotomy occurs along a subsequence as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
:

•

either
(v_{i,p})_{p}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
,

•

or
(v_{i,p})_{p}
is unbounded in
H^{s}(M)
and we have

(6.19)

v_{i,p}=\gamma_{i,p}\big(K+o(1)\big)\quad\text{ as }p\to\frac{n}{2s},

where
\gamma_{i,p}=\|v_{i,p}\|_{H^{s}}\to+\infty
,
K
is a nonzero element of
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
and
o(1)
denotes a sequence that strongly converges to
0
in
H^{s}(M)
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. In this case
v_{i,p}
also decomposes as

(6.20)

v_{i,p}=k_{i,p}+\tilde{w}_{i,p},

where
\tilde{w}_{i,p}\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})^{\perp}
is the orthogonal projection of
v_{i,p}
onto
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
for the
H^{s}(M)
-scalar product. Lemma
2.2
also states that the sequence
(\tilde{w}_{i,p})_{p}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
and that
\tilde{w}_{i,p}\rightharpoonup 0
in
H^{s}(M)
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
.

Remark that, again by Lemma
2.2
, the second alternative can only occur if
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})\neq\{0\}
. We prove Theorem
6.2
in a series of lemmas. Unbounded families of eigenfunctions are the main obstacle in passing to the limit the Euler-Lagrange relation (
6.14
) as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. We separate the indexes in
\{\ell,\cdots,k\}
appearing in (
6.14
) in two, according to the limit of
d_{i}
: we write
\{\ell,\cdots,k\}=I\sqcup J
, where

(6.21)

I=\big\{i\in\{\ell,\cdots,k\},d_{i}>0\big\}\quad\text{ and }\quad J=\big\{i\in\{\ell,\cdots,k\},d_{i}=0\big\}.

The first result that we prove establishes that the eigenfunctions
v_{i,p}
in (
6.14
) with
i\in I
are uniformly bounded in
H^{s}(M)
:

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
i\in\{\ell,\cdots,k\}
, where
\ell
is as in (
6.14
). If
i\in I
, that is if
d_{i}>0
, there exists
C>0
independent of
p
such that
\|v_{i,p}\|_{H^{s}}\leq C
for all
p>\frac{n}{2s}
.

Recall that the relations
d_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i=\ell}^{k}d_{i}=1
ensures that
I\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let
i\in I
. Equality (
6.14
) shows that, almost everywhere in
M
, we have

(6.22)

\frac{d_{i}}{2}(v_{i,p})^{2}\leq\beta_{p}^{p-1}

which shows, since
\|\beta_{p}\|_{L^{p}}=1
, that
(v_{i,p})_{p}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(M)
. Assume by contradiction that
(v_{i,p})_{p}
is not bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. Then (
6.19
) shows that

\|v_{i,p}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\gamma_{i,p}^{2}\big(\|Z\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+o(1)\big),

which is a contradiction since
Z\neq 0
.
∎

We next observe that only the sequences
(v_{i,p})_{p}
for
i\in I
contribute to (
6.14
):

Lemma 6.2
.

For
p>\frac{n}{2s}
, we define

(6.23)

\tilde{\beta}_{p}=\Big(\beta_{p}^{p-1}-\sum_{i\in J}d_{i,p}v_{i,p}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}.

Then

(6.24)

\|\tilde{\beta}_{p}-\beta_{p}\|_{L^{p}}\to 0

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
from above.

Proof.

First,
\tilde{\beta}_{p}
is well-defined since
\beta_{p}^{p-1}-\sum_{i\in J}d_{i,p}v_{i,p}^{2}\geq 0
a.e. by (
6.14
). Similarly, (
6.14
) also shows that
0\leq\tilde{\beta}_{p}\leq\beta_{p}
a.e. in
M
. As a consequence, we have

(6.25)

\int_{M}|\beta_{p}-\tilde{\beta}_{p}|^{p}dv_{g}\leq 2^{p-1}\int_{M}\beta_{p}^{p-1}|\beta_{p}-\tilde{\beta}_{p}|dv_{g}.

We now estimate
|\beta_{p}-\tilde{\beta}_{p}|
pointwise. Since
p>1
, we recall that the following inequality holds for all real numbers
a\geq 0
and
0\leq b\leq a
:

\Big|\big(a^{p-1}-b^{p-1}\big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}-a\Big|\leq Ca^{2-p}b^{p-1}

for some positive constant
C
that is independent of
p
. Applying the latter to
a=\beta_{p}
and
b=\big(\sum_{i\in J}d_{i,p}v_{i,p}^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}
shows that we have, a.e. in
M
,

\beta_{p}^{p-2}|\beta_{p}-\tilde{\beta}_{p}|\leq C\sum_{i\in J}d_{i,p}v_{i,p}^{2}.

Using (
6.25
) finally gives, since
Q(\beta_{p},v_{i,p})=1
for all
p
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}|\beta_{p}-\tilde{\beta}_{p}|^{p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{M}\sum_{i\in J}d_{i,p}\beta_{p}v_{i,p}^{2}dv_{g}=C\sum_{i\in J}d_{i,p}\to 0

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
by definition of
J
in (
6.21
). This proves the lemma.
∎

Using Lemma
6.2
, the Euler-Lagrange relation (
6.14
) now rewrites as

(6.26)

\tilde{\beta}_{p}^{p-1}=\sum_{i\in I}d_{i,p}v_{i,p}^{2}\quad\text{ a. e. in }M,

where
\tilde{\beta}_{p}
is given by (
6.23
). Relation (
6.26
) is of course reminiscent of (
6.14
), but in (
6.26
) every
v_{i,p}
in the r.h.s is now bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. Since
\|\beta_{p}\|_{L^{p}}=1
, there exists
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
such that
\beta_{p}\rightharpoonup\beta
weakly in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, and by (
6.24
) we also have
\tilde{\beta}_{p}\rightharpoonup\beta
weakly in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
. For any
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
we define a new family of functions as follows:

(6.27)

w_{i,p}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{i,p}&\text{ if }(v_{i,p})_{p}\text{ is bounded in }H^{s}(M)\\
&\tilde{w}_{i,p}&\text{ otherwise },\end{aligned}\right.

where
\tilde{w}_{i,p}
is defined in (
6.20
). For any
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
, the sequence
(w_{i,p})_{p}
is in particular bounded in
H^{s}(M)
, and we denote by
w_{i}
its weak limit in
H^{s}(M)
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. If
i
is such that
(v_{i,p})_{p}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
, we denote by
v_{i}
its weak limit. Again by Lemma
2.2
we thus have

w_{i}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{i}&\text{ if }(v_{i,p})_{p}\text{ is bounded in }H^{s}(M)\\
&0&\text{ otherwise}\end{aligned}\right..

We define the
set of concentration points of
({\beta}_{p})_{p}
by

A=\Biggl\{x\in M\,\Big|\,\forall{\delta}>0,\;\limsup_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\int_{B_{g}(x,{\delta})}\beta_{p}^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}\geq\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})}{\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])}\right)^{\frac{n}{2s}}\Biggl\}.

It is a finite set. As a first result we prove that each sequence
(w_{i,p})_{p}
strongly converges to
w_{i}
outside of
A
:

Lemma 6.3
.

As
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, we have

(6.28)

w_{i,p}\to w_{i}\quad\text{ strongly in }H^{s}_{loc}(M\backslash A)

and

(6.29)

\beta_{p}\to\beta\quad\text{ strongly in }L^{\frac{n}{2s}}_{loc}(M\backslash A).

Also, if there exists
i\in\{k_{+},\cdots,k\}
such that
(v_{i,p})_{p}
is not bounded in
H^{s}(M)
, then
\beta\equiv 0
.

Proof.

Let
i
be such that
(v_{i,p})_{p}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. Then
w_{i,p}=v_{i,p}
and
v_{i,p}\to v_{i}
in
H^{s}_{loc}(M\backslash A)
by Lemma
2.3
, which proves (
6.28
) in this case. The set of such indexes
i
is not empty, since it contains indexes
i\in I
by Lemma
6.1
, where
I
is given by (
6.21
). Using (
6.28
) together with (
6.24
) and (
6.26
) then proves (
6.29
).

We now assume that there exists
i\in\{\ell,\cdots,k\}
such that
(v_{i,p})_{p}
is not bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. Then Lemma
2.2
implies that
\beta\equiv 0
and (
6.29
) shows that
\beta_{p}\to 0
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}_{loc}(M\backslash A)
. Let
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\backslash A)
with
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
. By (
6.15
) and (
6.27
), and since
w_{i,p}\rightharpoonup 0
in
H^{s}(M)
,
\eta w_{i,p}
satisfies

P_{g}^{s}(\eta w_{i,p})=\lambda_{i,p}\beta_{p}\eta v_{i,p}+o(1)\quad\text{ in }M,

where
\|o(1)\|_{H^{-s}}\to 0
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. By (
2.11
) and since
L^{\frac{2n}{n+2s}}(M)
continuously embeds into
H^{-s}(M)
by Hölder and Sobolev inequalities we have

\|w_{i,p}\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\|\beta_{p}\eta v_{i,p}\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n+2s}}}+o(1)

for some
C>0
. By Hölder’s inequality and (
6.16
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\big(\beta_{p}\eta v_{i,p}\big)^{\frac{2n}{n+2s}}dv_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{M}(\eta\beta_{p})^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n+2s}}\to 0

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, since
\beta_{p}\to 0
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}_{loc}(M\backslash A)
. Combining these inequalities proves that
\|w_{i,p}\|_{H^{s}}\to 0
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
from above and concludes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

A consequence of our assumption that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is not attained is that there exist concentration points:

Lemma 6.4
.

We have
A\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Assume that
A=\emptyset
. Then, by (
6.29
),
\beta_{p}\to\beta
strongly in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
, so that
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
. A consequence of Lemma
2.2
is then that every sequence
(v_{i,p})_{p}
, for
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
, is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. If we denote by
(v_{i})_{k_{+}\leq i\leq k}
the respective weak limits in
H^{s}(M)
, relations (
6.15
) and (
6.16
) strongly pass to the limit as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
and show that
(v_{i})_{k_{+}\leq i\leq k}
is an orthonormal family, for
Q(\beta,\cdot)
, of eigenfunctions associated to eigenvalues
(\lambda_{i})_{k_{+}\leq i\leq k}
of the generalised metric
\beta g
. Here we used that
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}\mapsto\lambda_{k}(\beta)
is continuous since
k\geq k_{+}
(see Remark
3.3
), which implies that
\lambda_{k}=\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\lambda_{k}(\beta_{p})=\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. By (
6.17
) this shows that
\lambda_{k}(\beta)=\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
and hence that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained which contradicts the assumptions of Theorem
6.2
.
∎

In the following we distinguish two cases depending on the weak limit
\beta
.

6.3.
Proof of Theorem
6.2
: the case
\beta\not\equiv 0
.

If
\beta\not\equiv 0
, for any
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
the sequence
(v_{i,p})_{p}
is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
by Lemma
2.2
. The weak limits
v_{i}
then satisfy

(6.30)

\displaystyle P_{g}^{s}v_{i}={\lambda}_{i}\beta v_{i}\quad\text{ in }M.

We now introduce a new family of eigenfunctions that will simplify the analysis. We let
{\lambda}_{k_{+}}^{\prime},\cdots,{\lambda}_{m}^{\prime}
be the distinct values of
{\lambda}_{k_{+}},\cdots,{\lambda}_{k}
i.e.

\{{\lambda}_{k_{+}}^{\prime},\cdots,{\lambda}_{m}^{\prime}\}=\{{\lambda}_{k_{+}},\cdots,{\lambda}_{k}\}

and
{\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}\not={\lambda}^{\prime}_{j}
if
i\not=j
.
For
i\in\{k_{+},\cdots,m\}
, denote by
S_{i}
set of indexes
j\in\{k_{+},\cdots,k\}
such that
{\lambda}_{j}={\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}
.
We define
V_{i,p}={\mathop{\rm Span}}_{j\in S_{i}}\{v_{j,p}\}
and
V_{p}=\oplus_{i=k_{+}}^{m}V_{i,p}
. In particular,
V_{p}=\text{Span}\big\{v_{k_{+},p},\cdots,v_{k,p}\big\}
.
The bilinear form

\displaystyle B(\beta_{p},h,k)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\beta_{p}hkdv_{g}

defines a scalar products in
V_{p}
(and thus on each
V_{i,p}
), and
(v_{j,p})_{j\in S_{i}}
is an orthonormal basis of
V_{i,p}
for
B(\beta_{p},\cdot,\cdot)
. By standard results of basic algebra, for any
i\in\{k_{+},\cdots,m\}
, there exists an orthonormal basis
(u_{j,p})_{j\in S_{i}}
of
V_{i,p}
for
B({\beta}_{p},\cdot,\cdot)
which is orthogonal for
B(\beta,\cdot,\cdot)
. By doing this in each
V_{i,p}
we get an orthonormal basis
(u_{k_{+},p},\cdots,u_{k,p})
of
V_{p}
for the scalar product
B({\beta}_{p},\cdot,\cdot)
which is orthogonal for
B(\beta,\cdot,\cdot)
i.e.

(6.31)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{p}u_{i,p}u_{j,p}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\text{ for all }k_{+}\leq i,j\leq k\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta u_{i,p}u_{j,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{ for all }k_{+}\leq i,j\leq k,i\neq j.

Assume that
j\in S_{i}
. Then there exist some real numbers
({\alpha}_{r,p})_{r\in S_{i}}
satisfying
\sum_{r\in S_{i}}{\alpha}_{r,p}^{2}=1
such that
u_{j,p}=\sum_{r\in S_{i}}{\alpha}_{r,p}v_{r,p}
.
Therefore, using (
6.15
),

P_{g}^{s}u_{j,p}=\beta_{p}\sum_{r\in S_{i}}{\lambda}_{r,p}{\alpha}_{r,p}v_{r,p}.

Since by construction
{\lambda}_{j,p}
tends to
{\lambda}_{j}={\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
we have

(6.32)

P_{g}^{s}u_{j,p}={\lambda}_{j}{\beta}_{p}u_{j,p}+{\beta}_{p}f_{j,p}

where
f_{j,p}=\sum_{r\in S_{i}}({\lambda}_{r,p}-{\lambda}_{j}){\alpha}_{r,p}v_{r,p}
satisfies

(6.33)

\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\|f_{j,p}\|_{H^{s}}=0\quad\text{ and hence }\quad\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\int_{M}{\beta}_{p}(f_{j,p})^{2}dv_{g}=0.

Up to passing to a subsequence we can assume that for each
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
,
(u_{i,p})_{p}
weakly converges to
u_{i}\in H^{s}(M)
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Passing (
6.31
) and (
6.32
) to the limit yields

(6.34)

\int_{M}\beta u_{i}u_{j}dv_{g}=0\quad\text{ for all }k_{+}\leq i\neq j\leq k

and

(6.35)

P_{g}^{s}u_{j}={\lambda}_{j}\beta u_{j}.

We also have, thanks to (
6.28
) and since
v_{i,p}=w_{i,p}
for all
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
,

(6.36)

u_{i,p}\to u_{i}\quad\text{ in }H^{s}(M\backslash A)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. We will work from now on with the sequences
(u_{j,p})_{p}
instead of
(v_{j,p})_{p}
. We define in what follows the
weight
of
M\setminus A
by

(6.37)

{\omega}_{0}=\sharp\{i\in\{k_{+},\cdots,k\}|\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{i}\not\equiv 0\}.

We prove energy-quantification of
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
in two steps. We first estimate the energy contained in
M
after passing to the weak limit:

Lemma 6.5
.

Assume that
\omega_{0}>0
. Then

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\left(\int_{M}\beta^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n}}\geq{\Lambda}_{\omega_{0}+k_{+}-1}^{s}(M,[g]).

Remark 6.1
.

We will prove later that, if
\omega_{0}>0
, equality holds in the previous inequality and that
{\Lambda}_{\omega_{0}+k_{+}-1}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained.

Proof.

Let
k_{+}\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\omega_{0}}\leq k
be such that
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{i_{r}}\not\equiv 0
for
r=1\dots\omega_{0}
. From (
6.34
) we know that
\big(\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{i_{1}},\cdots,\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{i_{\omega_{0}}}\big)
is free, and by (
6.35
) each
u_{j}
is a eigenfunction associated to a positive eigenvalue for the generalised metric
\beta g
. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
3.5
there is a family
(\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1})
of functions in
H^{s}(M)
that satisfy

\displaystyle\int_{M}\varphi_{i}P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}dv_{g}\leq 0\quad\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq k_{+}-1\text{ and }

\displaystyle\int_{M}\varphi_{i}P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{j}dv_{g}=\Big(\int_{M}\varphi_{i}P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}dv_{g}\Big)\delta_{ij}\quad\text{ for all }1\leq i,j\leq k_{+}

and such that
(\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1},u_{i_{1}},\cdots,u_{\omega_{0}})
is orthonormal with respect to
Q(\beta,\cdot)
. We may thus let

V=\text{Span}\big\{\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1},u_{i_{1}},\cdots,u_{\omega_{0}}\big\}.

Then
\dim_{\beta}V=\omega_{0}+k_{+}-1
and
V
is an admissible subspace in the definition (
3.2
) of
\lambda_{\omega_{0}+k_{+}-1}(\beta)
. Using again (
6.35
) we then obtain that

(6.38)

\lambda_{\omega_{0}+k_{+}-1}(\beta)\leq\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)\leq\lambda_{k}={\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g]),

where
\mathcal{R}(\beta,v)
is as in (
3.1
). Going back to the definition (
4.21
) of
\Lambda_{\omega_{0}+k_{+}-1}(M,[g])
we thus obtain

{\Lambda}_{\omega_{0}+k_{+}-1}^{s}(M,[g])\leq\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\left(\int_{M}\beta^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n}}

which proves the lemma.
∎

We now estimate the energy which is lost
via
bubbling:

Lemma 6.6
.

We have

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\left(1-\int_{M}\beta^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n}}\geq\Lambda_{k-\omega_{0}-k_{+}+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n}).

Remark 6.2
.

We have

(6.39)

0<\int_{M}\beta^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}<1.

Indeed,
\beta\not\equiv 0
, and since
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
is uniformly convex, the assumption
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
would imply that
\beta_{p}
strongly converges to
\beta
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
, which would contradict Lemma
6.4
. Note also that lemma
6.6
does not require the assumption
\omega_{0}>0
.

Proof.

Let
\delta>0
small enough so that all the balls
B_{g}(x,\delta)
, for
x\in A
, are disjoint. Let
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,+\infty))
be such that
\eta\equiv 1
in
[0,\delta]
,
\eta\equiv 0
in
[2\delta,+\infty)
and
|\eta^{(r)}|\leq\frac{C}{{\delta}^{r}}
for any
r\geq 1
, where
C
does not depend on
{\delta}
. Let
\Gamma\subseteq\{k_{+},\cdots,k\}
be the set of indexes
i
such that
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{i}\equiv 0
. By definition of
\omega_{0}
in (
6.37
) we have

(6.40)

\sharp\Gamma=k-\omega_{0}-k_{+}+1.

Define, for
i\in\Gamma
,

(6.41)

z_{i,p}=\chi u_{i,p},

where
u_{i,p}
is as in (
6.31
) and where we have let
\chi=\sum_{x\in A}\eta\big(d_{g}(x,\cdot)\big)
. As a first observation, we claim that the following holds:
if
i,j\in\Gamma
and
\delta>0
is fixed we have

(6.42)

\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\int_{M}\beta_{p}z_{i,p}z_{j,p}dv_{g}=\delta_{ij}

and

(6.43)

\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\int_{M}z_{j,p}P_{g}^{s}z_{i,p}dv_{g}=\lambda_{i}\delta_{ij}+\varepsilon(\delta)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, where
\varepsilon(\delta)
denotes a real function such that
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\varepsilon(\delta)=0
.

Proof of (
6.42
) and (
6.43
).

Let
i\in\Gamma
. By (
6.31
), (
6.36
) and since
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{i}\equiv 0
we have

(6.44)

\displaystyle\int_{\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,\delta)}\beta_{p}u_{i,p}u_{j,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}-\int_{M\backslash\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,\delta)}\beta_{p}u_{i,p}u_{j,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}+o(1)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Independently, since
z_{i,p}=u_{i,p}
in
\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,\delta)
, and again by (
6.36
) and since
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{i}\equiv 0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{p}z_{i,p}z_{j,p}dv_{g}=\int_{\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,\delta)}\beta_{p}u_{i,p}u_{j,p}dv_{g}+o(1)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, so that (
6.42
) follows from (
6.44
). To prove (
6.43
) we now observe that

P_{g}^{s}z_{i,p}=P_{g}^{s}\big(\chi u_{i,p}\big)=\chi P_{g}^{s}u_{i,p}+O\big(\sum_{r+t\leq 2s,r\geq 1,t\geq 0}|\nabla^{r}\chi||\nabla^{t}u_{i,p}|\big),

so that by definition of
\chi
we have

(6.45)

\displaystyle\int_{M}z_{j,p}P_{g}^{s}

\displaystyle z_{i,p}dv_{g}=\int_{M}\chi^{2}u_{j,p}P_{g}^{s}u_{i,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle+O\Big(\int_{\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,2\delta)\backslash B_{g}(x,\delta)}\sum_{r+t\leq 2s,r\geq 1,t\geq 0}\delta^{-r}|\nabla^{t}u_{i,p}||u_{j,p}|dv_{g}\Big).

If
r,t
are fixed integers with
r\geq 1,t\geq 0
and
r+t\leq 2s
we have, using the definition of
\chi
, (
6.36
) and Hölder’s inequality, that

\displaystyle\delta^{-r}

\displaystyle\int_{\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,2\delta)\backslash B_{g}(x,\delta)}|\nabla^{t}u_{i,p}||u_{j,p}|dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\delta^{-r}\int_{\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,2\delta)\backslash B_{g}(x,\delta)}|\nabla^{t}u_{i}||u_{j}|dv_{g}+o(1)

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{-r}\sum_{x\in A}{\mathop{\rm Vol}}_{g}\big(B_{g}(x,2\delta)\big)^{\frac{4s-2t}{2n}}\left(\int_{B_{g}(x,2\delta)}|\nabla^{t}u_{i}|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s+2t}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2s+2t}{2n}}

\displaystyle\times\left(\int_{B_{g}(x,2\delta)\backslash B_{g}(x,\delta)}|u_{j}|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}+o(1)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{x\in A}\left(\int_{B_{g}(x,2\delta)\backslash B_{g}(x,\delta)}|u_{j}|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}+o(1)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon(\delta)+o(1)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, where we used that
2s-t\geq r
. Independently, using (
6.31
), (
6.32
) and (
6.33
), we get that

\int_{M}\chi^{2}P_{g}^{s}u_{i,p}u_{j,p}dv_{g}=\int_{M}\chi^{2}\lambda_{j}\beta_{p}u_{i,p}u_{j,p}dv_{g}+o(1).

Again by (
6.36
) and since
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{i}\equiv 0
we then get that

\displaystyle\int_{M}\chi^{2}\lambda_{j}\beta_{p}u_{i,p}u_{j,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,\delta)}\lambda_{j}\beta_{p}u_{i,p}u_{j,p}dv_{g}+o(1)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{j}\delta_{ji}+o(1)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, where the last line follows from (
6.44
). Combining the previous equations in (
6.45
) proves (
6.43
).
∎

We now denote the points in
A
by
x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}
. We let
y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\in\doba{R}^{n}
be
r
distinct points of
\doba{R}^{n}
and let
\delta>0
be small enough so that all the balls
B_{\xi}(y_{t},3\delta)
,
1\leq t\leq r
, are disjoint, where we have denoted by
\xi
the Euclidean metric. For any
1\leq t\leq r
we let
{\varphi}_{t}:B_{g}(x_{t},2\delta)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a local chart that defines normal coordinates in
M
and which sends
z_{t}
to
y_{t}
. For
\delta
small enough we may assume that
{\varphi}_{t}\big(B_{g}(x_{t},2{\delta})\big)\subset B_{\xi}(y_{t},3{\delta})
. We consider the map

(6.46)

{\varphi}:\cup_{t=1}^{r}B_{g}(x_{t},2{\delta})\to\cup_{t=1}^{r}{\varphi}_{t}\big(B_{g}(x_{t},2{\delta})\big)

defined by
{\varphi}(y)={\varphi}_{t}(y)
if
y\in B_{g}(y_{t},2\delta)
. We define, for any
i\in\Gamma
,

Z_{i,p}=z_{i,p}\circ{\varphi}^{-1}\;\hbox{ and }\Theta_{p}=\beta_{p}\circ{\varphi}^{-1},

where
z_{i,p}
is defined in (
6.41
).
Z_{i,p}
is well-defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
since
z_{i,p}
is compactly supported in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}B_{g}(x_{i},2\delta)
, and our convention here is that we extend
\Theta_{p}
by zero outside of the image of
{\varphi}
. We now claim that the following holds:

(6.47)

\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\int_{\doba{R}^{n}}\Theta_{p}Z_{i,p}Z_{j,p}dv_{\xi}=\delta_{ij}+\varepsilon(\delta)

and

(6.48)

\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\int_{\doba{R}^{n}}P_{\xi}^{s}(Z_{i,p})Z_{i,p}dv_{\xi}=\lambda_{i}\delta_{ij}+\varepsilon(\delta),

where as before
\varepsilon(\delta)
denotes a real function such that
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\varepsilon(\delta)=0
.

Proof of (
6.47
) and (
6.48
).

Since
\varphi_{t}
defines normal coordinates in each ball
B_{g}(x_{t},2\delta)
,
1\leq t\leq r
, we have

(6.49)

\big[(\varphi^{-1})^{*}g\big]_{ij}=\xi_{ij}+O(\delta)\quad\text{ and }\quad dv_{(\varphi^{-1})^{*}g}=\big(1+O(\delta)\big)dv_{\xi}

in
\cup_{t=1}^{r}{\varphi}_{t}\big(B_{g}(x_{t},2{\delta})\big)
, so that (
6.47
) follows from the
L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}(M)
-boundedness of
z_{i,p}
, (
6.42
), (
6.49
) and a simple change of variables. To prove (
6.48
) we first recall that
P_{\xi}^{s}=(\Delta_{\xi})^{s}
. We express
P_{g}^{s}
in
B_{g}(x_{y},2\delta)
in the normal coordinates defined by
\varphi_{t}
: for any smooth function
u
compactly supported in
{\varphi}_{t}\big(B_{g}(x_{t},2{\delta})\big)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have

(6.50)

P_{(\varphi^{-1})^{*}g}u=(\Delta_{(\varphi^{-1})^{*}g})^{s}u+Tu,

where
T
is a differential operator of order at most
2s-1
whose coefficients converge in
C^{0}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
\delta\to 0
(see for instance
[
29
]
or
[
40
, Proposition 2.1]
). We let in the following

U_{\delta}=\cup_{t=1}^{r}{\varphi}_{t}\big(B_{g}(x_{t},2{\delta})\big).

Since the sequence
(z_{i,p})_{p}
is uniformly bounded in
H^{s}(M)
, and since
Z_{i,p}
is supported in
U_{\delta}
, a change of variables with (
6.49
) shows that there exists
C>0
independent of
p
and
\delta
such that
\|\Delta_{\xi}^{\frac{s}{2}}Z_{i,p}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C

for all
p
. Sobolev’s embedding then shows that

(6.51)

\|Z_{i,p}\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C

for some
C>0
independent of
p
and
\delta
. If
Z_{i}
denotes the weak limit of
(Z_{i,p})_{p}
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which is supported in
U_{\delta}
, compact embeddings show that

Z_{i,p}\to Z_{i}
strongly in
H^{s-1}_{0}(U_{\delta})
as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, so that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}TZ_{i,p}Z_{j,p}dv_{\xi}=\int_{U_{\delta}}TZ_{i}Z_{j}dv_{\xi}+o(1)=\varepsilon(\delta)+o(1)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Independently, by (
6.49
), (
6.51
) and
[
41
, Lemma 9.1]
, we have

\int_{U_{\delta}}Z_{j,p}(\Delta_{(\varphi^{-1})^{*}g})^{s}Z_{i,p}dv_{(\varphi^{-1})^{*}g}=\int_{U_{\delta}}Z_{j,p}\Delta_{\xi}^{s}Z_{i,p}dv_{\xi}+\varepsilon(\delta)

as
\delta\to 0
. Using again (
6.49
) and (
6.50
) then gives, since
P_{\xi}^{s}=\Delta_{\xi}^{s}
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}P_{g}^{s}z_{i,p}z_{j,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{{\varphi}_{t}\big(B_{g}(x_{t},2{\delta})\big)}P_{(\varphi^{-1})^{*}g}Z_{i,p}Z_{j,p}dv_{\xi}+\varepsilon(\delta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}Z_{j,p}P_{\xi}^{s}Z_{i,p}dv_{\xi}+\varepsilon(\delta)+o(1)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Equation (
6.48
) then follows from (
6.43
).
∎

We can now conclude the proof of Lemma
6.6
. We let
W_{p}={\mathop{\rm Span}}_{i\in\Gamma}\{Z_{i,p}\}
. For fixed
\delta>0
small enough, and as
p
tends to
\frac{n}{2s}
, (
6.47
) shows that
\dim_{\Theta_{p}}W_{p}=\sharp\Gamma=k-\omega_{0}-k_{+}+1
by (
6.40
). Hence
W_{p}
is an admissible subspace to compute
\Lambda_{k-\omega_{0}-k_{+}+1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
and we have

(6.52)

{\Lambda}_{k-\omega_{0}-k_{+}+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})\leq\max_{v\in W_{p}\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\Theta_{p},v)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Theta_{p}^{\frac{n}{2s}}v_{\xi}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n}},

where
\mathcal{R}
is as in (
3.1
). We implicitly used here that
(\mathbb{S}^{n}\backslash\{p\},g_{0})
is conformally diffeomorphic to
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\xi)
for any
p\in\doba{S}^{n}
via stereographic projection. Recall that the functions
\Theta_{p}
and
Z_{i,p}
are compactly supported in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
so there are no integrability conditions at infinity to verify. Let
({\alpha}_{i,p})_{i\in\Gamma}
be such that
\sum_{i\in\Gamma}{\alpha}_{i,p}^{2}=1
and such that
Z_{p}:=\sum_{i\in\Gamma}{\alpha}_{i,p}Z_{i,p}
achieves
\max_{v\in W_{p}\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\Theta_{p},v)
. Then (
6.47
) and (
6.48
) show that

(6.53)

\max_{v\in W_{p}\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\Theta_{p},v)=\frac{\int_{\doba{R}^{n}}Z_{p}P_{\xi}^{s}Z_{p}dv_{\xi}}{\int_{\doba{R}^{n}}\Theta_{p}Z_{p}^{2}dv_{\xi}}\leq{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])+\varepsilon(\delta).

By definition of
\Theta_{p}
and (
6.48
) we have

\int_{\doba{R}^{n}}\Theta_{p}^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{\xi}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{B^{g}(x_{i},2{\delta})}{\beta}_{p}^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}+\varepsilon(\delta)=1-\int_{M\backslash\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,2\delta)}{\beta}_{p}^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}+\varepsilon(\delta).

From (
6.29
) we thus obtain, since
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
,

(6.54)

\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\int_{\doba{R}^{n}}\Theta_{p}^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{\xi}=1-\int_{M}{\beta}^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{g}+\varepsilon(\delta).

Combining (
6.53
) and (
6.54
) in (
6.52
) and letting
\delta\to 0
concludes the proof of Lemma
6.6
.
∎

We are now in position to prove Theorem
6.2
when
\beta\not\equiv 0
. Assume first that
\omega_{0}=0
, where
\omega_{0}
is as in (
6.37
). Lemma
6.6
then shows that
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M)\geq\Lambda_{k-k_{+}+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
and concludes the proof. If now
\omega_{0}>0
, summing the conclusions of Lemmas
6.5
and
6.6
shows that

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\geq({\Lambda}_{\omega_{0}+k_{+}-1}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{k-\omega_{0}-k_{+}+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}})^{\frac{2s}{n}}.

The right-hand side is by definition larger than the invariant
Y_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
(given by (
6.1
)) hence we have
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\geq Y_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
. Proposition
6.3
shows that the reverse inequality also holds. We thus have

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])=({\Lambda}_{\omega_{0}+k_{+}-1}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{k-\omega_{0}-k_{+}+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}})^{\frac{2s}{n}}.

As a consequence, the inequality of Lemma
6.5
is also an equality. Since
\beta\not\equiv 0
we obtain by using (
6.38
) that
{\Lambda}_{\omega_{0}+k_{+}-1}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained by
{\beta}
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
6.2
in the case where
\beta\not\equiv 0
and when
(M,g)
is not conformally diffeomorphic to the round sphere
(\doba{S}^{n},g_{0})
.

We conclude this subsection by proving that in the case of the standard sphere we can always assume that
\beta\not\equiv 0
. In the following lemma we use the notations introduced in the previous paragraphs

Lemma 6.7
.

Assume that
(M,g)
is conformally diffeomorphic to the round sphere
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
. Without loss of generality we can always assume that
\beta\not\equiv 0
and that
{\omega}_{0}>0
, where
\omega_{0}
is as in (
6.37
).

Proof.

We keep the same notations than before. We recall that
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
is coercive (see e.g. (
2.9
)) and hence
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g_{0}}^{s})=\{0\}
and
k_{+}=1
. As the discussion before (
6.28
) shows, this implies that all the sequences
(v_{i,p})_{p}
, for
1=k_{+}\leq i\leq k
, are bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. We also have
\beta_{p}\in C^{0,\alpha}(M)
for some
0<\alpha<1
, and this follows from (
6.14
) and Remark
4.2
. We let
V_{p}=\text{Span}\big\{v_{1,p},\cdots,v_{k,p}\big\}
, so that

(6.55)

{\lambda}_{k,p}=\max_{v\in V_{p}\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{p},v),

where
\mathcal{R}(\beta_{p},v)
is as in (
3.1
). Up to composing
\beta_{p}
and
(v_{i,p})_{1\leq i\leq k}
with an isometry of
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
we can assume that, for any
p
,
\beta_{p}
attains its maximum at the north pole
N
. Let
\pi_{N}:\doba{S}^{n}\backslash\{-N\}\to\doba{R}^{n}

be the stereographic projection of pole
-N
, that sends
N
to
0
. Let
\mu_{p}>0
be a positive number to be fixed and let
h_{p}:\doba{R}^{n}\to\doba{R}^{n}
be the homothecy defined by
h_{p}(x)=\mu_{p}x
. We define
\varphi_{p}=\pi_{N}^{-1}\circ h_{p}\circ\pi_{N}
and, if
u\in L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is any function,

\theta_{p}(u)=\mu_{p}^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}u\circ{\varphi}_{p}.

It is well-known that
{\varphi}_{p}
is a conformal diffeomorphism of
\doba{S}^{n}
, and therefore it preserves the quadratic form
G
defined in (
3.1
). A simple change of variables with (
6.55
) thus gives

(6.56)

\lambda_{k,p}=\max_{v\in V_{p}\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{p},v)=\max_{v\in\theta_{p}(V_{p})\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}\big(\mu_{p}^{2s}\beta_{p}\circ\varphi_{p},v\big).

We let in what follows, for
1\leq i\leq k
,
\bar{v}_{i,p}=\theta_{p}(v_{i,p})
and
\bar{\beta}_{p}=\mu_{p}^{2s}\beta_{p}\circ\varphi_{p}
. Using (
6.15
) it is straightforward to check that
\bar{v}_{i,p}
satisfies

(6.57)

P_{g_{0}}^{s}\bar{v}_{i,p}=\lambda_{i,p}\bar{\beta}_{p}\bar{v}_{i,p}\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{S}^{n}.

We choose

\mu_{p}=\|\beta_{p}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-\frac{1}{2s}}.

With this choice of
\mu_{p}
the function
\bar{\beta}_{p}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
and attains its maximum, of value
1
, at the north pole
N
. We let
\bar{\beta}
be the weak limit of
(\bar{\beta}_{p})_{p}
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)
, up to a subsequence as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Since
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{S})\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}vP_{g_{0}}^{s}vdv_{g_{0}}
is equivalent to the standard
H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
norm and is invariant under conformal transformations, the sequences
(\bar{v}_{i,p})_{p}
, for
1\leq i\leq k
, remain bounded in
H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
. If we denote their weak limits by
(\bar{v}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}
, (
6.57
) and standard elliptic theory show that
\bar{v}_{i,p}\to v_{i}
in
C^{2s-1}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
.
We claim that there exists
i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
such that

\bar{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{v}_{i}\not\equiv 0.

By (
6.37
) this will conclude the proof of the Lemma. To see this, we remark that relation (
6.14
) rewrites as

\mu_{p}^{n-2s-2s(p-1)}\bar{\beta}_{p}^{p-1}=\sum_{i=\ell}^{k}d_{i,p}\bar{v}_{i,p}^{2}\quad\text{ everywhere in }\mathbb{S}^{n}.

This is now a pointwise relation since both
\bar{\beta}_{p}
and
\bar{v}_{i,p}
are continuous in
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. By evaluating this relation at the north pole
N
we obtain

(6.58)

\sum_{i=\ell}^{k}d_{i,p}\bar{v}_{i,p}(N)^{2}=\mu_{p}^{n-2s-2s(p-1)}.

The condition
\|\beta_{p}\|_{L^{p}}=1
implies that
\|\beta_{p}\|_{L^{\infty}}\not\to 0
, and hence there exists
C>0
independent of
p
such that
\mu_{p}\leq C
for all
p
. Since
p>\frac{n}{2s}
we have
n-2s-2s(p-1)<0
, so that

\mu_{p}^{n-2s-2s(p-1)}\geq C^{n-2s-2s(p-1)}=1+o(1)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Passing (
6.58
) to the limit as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
then gives

\sum_{i\in I}^{k}d_{i}\bar{v}_{i}(N)^{2}\geq 1

where
I
is as in (
6.21
), so that there exists
i_{0}\in I
such that
\bar{v}_{i_{0}}(N)\neq 0
, and hence
\bar{v}_{i_{0}}\not\equiv 0
. Now, equation (
6.57
) also passes to the limit and shows that

P_{g_{0}}^{s}\bar{v}_{i_{0}}=\lambda_{i_{0}}\bar{\beta}\bar{v}_{i_{0}}\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{S}^{n},

where
\lambda_{i_{0}}>0
by (
6.18
). Since
\bar{v}_{i_{0}}\not\equiv 0
and since
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
is coercive, integrating by parts shows that
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\bar{\beta}\bar{v}_{i_{0}}^{2}dv_{g_{0}}>0
, and hence that
\bar{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{v}_{i_{0}}\not\equiv 0
.
∎

Lemma
6.7
concludes the proof of Theorem
6.2
in the case where
\beta\not\equiv 0.

6.4.
Proof of Theorem
6.2
: the case
\beta\equiv 0
.

We now assume that
\beta\equiv 0
. In this case we cannot guarantee anymore that every sequence
(v_{i,p})_{p}
,
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
, is bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. To overcome this issue we work with the sequences
(w_{i,p})_{p}
introduced in (
6.27
), that are bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. We define, for
i\in\{k_{+},\cdots,k\}
,

(6.59)

z_{i,p}=\chi w_{i,p},

where
w_{i,p}
is as in (
6.27
) and where we have let
\chi=\sum_{x\in A}\eta\big(d_{g}(x,\cdot)\big)
. As a first result we show that the family
\{z_{i,p},k_{+}\leq i\leq k\}
is free in
L^{2}_{\beta_{p}}(M)
:

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
V^{\prime}_{p}={\mathop{\rm Span}}\{z_{i,p},k_{+}\leq i\leq k\}
. Then, for
p
close enough to
\frac{n}{2s}
, we have
\dim_{\beta_{p}}V_{p}^{\prime}=k-k_{+}+1
.

Proof.

Assume by contradiction that there is a nontrivial family
(a_{i,p})_{k_{+}\leq i\leq k}
such that

\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\beta}_{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}a_{i,p}z_{i,p}=0\quad\text{ in }M.

Since
z_{i,p}=\chi w_{i,p}
the latter rewrites as

\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\beta}_{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}a_{i,p}w_{i,p}=\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}a_{i,p}(1-\chi){\beta}_{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}w_{i,p}.

Going back to (
6.20
) we write
w_{i,p}=v_{i,p}-k_{i,p}
, with
k_{i,p}\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
and with the convention that
k_{i,p}\equiv 0
if the sequence
(v_{i,p})_{p}
was bounded in
H^{s}(M)
. We thus get

\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\beta}_{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}a_{i,p}v_{i,p}={\beta}_{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{p}+\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}a_{i,p}(1-\chi){\beta}_{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}w_{i,p},

where we have let
K_{p}=\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}a_{i,p}k_{i,p}\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
.
Let
A_{p}=\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}|a_{i,p}|
. We integrate the latter against
{\beta}_{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}v_{i_{0},p}
for some fixed
i_{0}\in\{k_{+},\cdots,k\}
. First, integrating (
6.15
) against
K_{p}
shows that
\int_{M}\beta_{p}K_{p}v_{i_{0},p}dv_{g}=0
. Second, Since
\beta_{p}\to 0
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}_{loc}(M\backslash A)
we have
(1-\chi)\beta_{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}w_{i,p}\to 0
in
L^{2}(M)
for all
i\in\{k_{+},\cdots,k\}
. We thus obtain, with (
6.16
),

a_{i_{0},p}=o(A_{p})

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Summing over all
i_{0}
gives
A_{p}=o(A_{p})
which proves that all the
a_{i,p}
are zero for
p
close enough to
\frac{n}{2s}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

We now claim that the following relations hold: for any
k_{+}\leq i,j\leq k
,

(6.60)

\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\left|\int_{M}\beta_{p}z_{i,p}z_{j,p}dv_{g}-\int_{M}\beta_{p}w_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}\right|=0

and

(6.61)

\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\left|\int_{M}P_{g}^{s}z_{i,p}z_{j,p}dv_{g}-\int_{M}P_{g}^{s}w_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}\right|=\varepsilon(\delta)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, where
\varepsilon(\delta)
denotes a real function such that
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\varepsilon(\delta)=0
.

Proof of (
6.60
) and (
6.61
).

First, by (
6.29
) we have
\beta_{p}\to 0
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}_{loc}(M\backslash A)
. As a consequence, and since all the sequences
(z_{i,p})_{p}
and
(w_{i,p})_{p}
, for
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
, are bounded in
H^{s}(M)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{p}z_{i,p}z_{j,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,\delta)}\beta_{p}w_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}+o(1)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\beta_{p}w_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}+o(1),

which proves (
6.60
). Arguing as in the proof of (
6.43
) we similarly obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{M}P_{g}^{s}z_{i,p}z_{j,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\chi^{2}P_{g}^{s}w_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}+o(1)+\varepsilon(\delta)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\lambda_{i,p}\chi^{2}\beta_{p}v_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}+o(1)+\varepsilon(\delta)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, where we used (
6.27
) and (
6.15
) to write that
P_{g}^{s}w_{i,p}=\lambda_{i,p}\beta_{p}v_{i,p}
. Observe now that

\displaystyle\int_{M}\lambda_{i,p}\chi^{2}\beta_{p}v_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\lambda_{i,p}\beta_{p}v_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{i,p}\int_{M}(1-\chi^{2})\beta_{p}v_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}P_{g}^{s}w_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}+o(1),

where in the last line we used (
6.16
) and the definition of
\chi
to write that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{M}(1-\chi^{2})\beta_{p}v_{i,p}w_{j,p}dv_{g}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M\backslash\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,\delta)}\beta_{p}|v_{i,p}||w_{j,p}|dv_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{M\backslash\bigcup_{x\in A}B_{g}(x,\delta)}\beta_{p}w_{j,p}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=o(1)

since
\beta_{p}\to 0
in
L^{\frac{n}{2s}}_{loc}(M\backslash A)
. This proves (
6.61
).
∎

As in the case
\beta\not\equiv 0
, we denote the points in
A
by
x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}
, we let
y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\in\doba{R}^{n}
be
r
distinct points of
\doba{R}^{n}
, we let
\delta>0
be small enough so that all the balls
B_{\xi}(y_{t},3\delta)
are disjoint and we let
\varphi
be as in (
6.46
). For any
k_{+}\leq i\leq k
we define

Z_{i,p}=z_{i,p}\circ{\varphi}^{-1}\;\hbox{ and }\Theta_{p}=\beta_{p}\circ{\varphi}^{-1},

where
z_{i,p}
is defined this time as in (
6.59
).
Z_{i,p}
is well-defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
since
z_{i,p}
is compactly supported in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}B_{g}(x_{i},2\delta)
, and we again extend
\Theta_{p}
by zero outside of the image of
{\varphi}
. We now let

W_{p}^{\prime}={\mathop{\rm Span}}_{k_{+}\leq i\leq k}\big\{Z_{i,p}\big\}.

By Lemma
6.8
and by the definition of
Z_{i,p}
and
\Theta_{p}
we have
\dim_{\Theta_{p}}W_{p}^{\prime}=k-k_{+}+1
, so that
W_{p}^{\prime}
is an admissible subspace to compute
\Lambda_{k-\omega_{0}-k_{+}+1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
. By Hölder’s inequality we have

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Theta_{p}^{\frac{n}{2s}}dv_{\xi}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n}}\leq\text{Vol}_{g}(M)^{1-\frac{n}{2sp}}\|\beta_{p}\|_{L^{p}}=1+o(1)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, and thus

(6.62)

{\Lambda}_{k-k_{+}+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})\leq\big(1+o(1)\big)\max_{v\in W_{p}^{\prime}\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\Theta_{p},v),

where
\mathcal{R}
is as in (
3.1
). For any
p>\frac{n}{2s}
let
({\alpha}_{i,p})_{k_{+}\leq i\leq k}
be such that
\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}^{2}=1
and such that

Z_{p}=\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}Z_{i,p}

achieves the maximum of the right-hand side of (
6.62
). By (
6.49
), (
6.60
) and since all the sequences
(Z_{i,p})_{p}
are bounded in
L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}(M)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Theta_{p}Z_{p}^{2}dv_{\xi}

\displaystyle\geq\big(1+\varepsilon(\delta)\big)\int_{M}\beta_{p}\big(\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}z_{i,p}\big)^{2}dv_{g}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{M}\beta_{p}\big(\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}w_{i,p}\big)^{2}dv_{g}+o(1)+\varepsilon(\delta)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
, for some positive constant
C
independent of
p
. Using (
6.27
) we can write

\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}w_{i,p}=\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}v_{i,p}+K_{p},

for some
K_{p}\in{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})
. Integrating (
6.15
) against
K_{p}
gives that for all
i
,
\int_{M}{\beta}_{p}v_{i,p}K_{p}dv_{g}=0
. Using (
6.16
) we then obtain

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{p}\big(\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}w_{i,p}\big)^{2}dv_{g}=1+\int_{M}{\beta}_{p}K_{v}^{2}dv_{g}\geq 1,

so that in the end

(6.63)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Theta_{p}Z_{p}^{2}dv_{\xi}\geq 1+o(1)+\varepsilon(\delta)

holds true as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Independently, arguing as in the proof of (
6.48
), we have

(6.64)

\lim_{p\to\frac{n}{2s}}\left|\int_{\doba{R}^{n}}P_{\xi}^{s}(Z_{i,p})Z_{i,p}dv_{\xi}-\int_{M}P_{g}^{s}(z_{i,p})z_{i,p}dv_{g}\right|=\varepsilon(\delta).

Using (
6.61
) and since
P_{g}^{s}w_{i,p}=P_{g}^{s}v_{i,p}
and
\int_{M}{\beta}_{p}v_{i,p}K_{p}dv_{g}=0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}z_{i,p}\right)P_{g}^{s}\left(\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}z_{i,p}\right)dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\left(\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}v_{i,p}\right)P_{g}^{s}\left(\sum_{i=k_{+}}^{k}{\alpha}_{i,p}v_{i,p}\right)dv_{g}+o(1)+\varepsilon(\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])+o(1)+\varepsilon(\delta),

where the last inequality follows from (
6.15
), (
6.16
) and (
6.17
). Combining with (
6.64
) then gives

(6.65)

\displaystyle\int_{\doba{R}^{n}}Z_{p}P_{\xi}^{s}Z_{p}dv_{\xi}\leq\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])+o(1)+\varepsilon(\delta)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Plugging (
6.63
) and (
6.65
) in (
6.62
) finally proves that

{\Lambda}_{k-k_{+}+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})\leq\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])+o(1)+\varepsilon(\delta)

as
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
. Letting
p\to\frac{n}{2s}
and then
\delta\to 0
concludes the proof of Theorem
6.2
in the case
\beta\equiv 0
.
\square

Remark 6.3
.

The asymptotic analysis that we perform in the proof of Theorem
6.2
heavily relies on the choice of the minimising sequence
(\beta)_{p}
obtained as a minimum of the subcritical invariants
\Lambda_{k}^{s,p}(M,[g])
. It is unlikely to hold true for a general minimising sequence.

7.
Coercive operators and the case of the standard sphere
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})

In this Section we apply Theorem
1.2
to the case of the standard sphere
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
, and we prove Theorems
1.6
and
1.7
. We first recall some generalities concerning positivity properties of the operators
P_{g}^{s}
. We let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold,
n>2s
and we let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
that we assume to be coercive as in (
4.31
). We will say in what follows that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies
the pointwise comparison principle
if, for any
u\in C^{2s}(M)
such that
P_{g}^{s}u\geq 0
, then either
u\equiv 0
or
u>0
in
M
. If
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive we have
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
and we can thus let
G_{g}^{s}
be the Green’s function of
P_{g}^{s}
. It is the unique smooth function in
M\times M\backslash\{(x,x),x\in M\}
that, for any
x\in M
, satisfies

P_{g}^{s}G_{g}^{s}(x,\cdot)=\delta_{x}

in
M
. For
u\in C^{2s}(M)
we then have, for any
x\in M
,

u(x)=\int_{M}G_{g}^{s}(s,\cdot)P_{g}^{s}u\,dv_{g}.

It is easily seen that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies the pointwise comparison principle if and only if, for any
x\in M
,
G_{g}^{s}(x,\cdot)
is positive in
M\backslash\{x\}
. An easy example of manifolds
(M,g)
for which
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies the pointwise comparison principle are Einstein manifolds with positive scalar curvature: by (
2.9
),
P_{g}^{s}
indeed decomposes as a product of second-order operators that each satisfy the point-wise comparison principle, and the result follows by induction. The round metric on the sphere falls in this category. When
s=1
the coercivity of
P_{g}^{s}=\Delta_{g}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}S_{g}
is equivalent to the pointwise comparison principle by the maximum principle. When
s\geq 2
, however, it is well-known that coercivity alone does not entail the pointwise comparison property in general. Determining whether
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies the pointwise comparison principle is a difficult question in general.

For operators satisfying the positive comparison principle we prove the following result that complements Proposition
3.4
:

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold,
n>2s
and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
. We assume that
s=1
or that
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive and satisfies the pointwise comparison principle. Let
0<\alpha<1
be fixed. Then, for any
\beta\in C^{0,\alpha}(M)\backslash\{0\}
with
\beta\geq 0
in
M
, we have

\lambda_{1}(\beta)<\lambda_{2}(\beta)\quad\text{ and }\quad\dim E_{1}(\beta)=1,

where
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
and
E_{1}(\beta)
are as in (
3.2
) and Proposition
3.4
. In addition, if
\varphi_{1}
is a generator of
E_{1}(\beta)
, then either
\varphi_{1}>0
in
M
or
\varphi_{1}<0
in
M
.

Remark that if
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive it satisfies in particular
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
. Under the assumptions of Proposition
7.1
, property (
1.3
) is therefore satisfied.

Proof.

Let
\beta\in C^{0,\alpha}(M)\backslash\{0\}
with
\beta\geq 0
, for some
0<\alpha<1
. The definition of
\lambda_{1}(\beta)
in (
3.2
) shows that

\lambda_{1}(\beta)=\inf_{v\in H^{s}(M),\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}v\neq 0}\frac{\int_{M}vP_{g}^{s}v\,dv_{g}}{\int_{M}\beta v^{2}\,dv_{g}}.

Elements in
E_{1}(\beta)
are exactly the functions
\varphi\in H^{s}(M)\cap L^{2}_{\beta}(M)\backslash\{0\}
attaining the infimum in the right-hand side. We first claim that if
\varphi
is nonzero and attains
\lambda_{1}(\beta)
, then

(7.1)

\text{ either }\varphi>0\text{ in }M\text{ or }\varphi<0\text{ in }M.

We prove (
7.1
): let
\varphi\in H^{s}(M)
with
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\neq 0
that attains
\lambda_{1}(\beta)
. It satisfies in particular

P_{g}^{s}\varphi=\lambda_{1}(\beta)\beta\varphi\quad\text{ in }M,

and standard elliptic theory thus shows that
\varphi\in C^{2s,\alpha}(M)
. Assume first that
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive and satisfies the pointwise comparison principle. We let
\psi\in C^{2s}(M)
be the unique solution of

P_{g}^{s}\psi=|P_{g}^{s}\varphi|\quad\text{ in }M.

The existence of such a
\psi
follows from the coercivity of
P_{g}^{s}
, and
\psi\in C^{2s,\alpha}(M)
by standard elliptic theory. By definition we have
P_{g}^{s}(\psi\pm\varphi)\geq 0
, so that the pointwise comparison property of
P_{g}^{s}
shows that
\psi\geq|\varphi|
everywhere in
M
. Note that
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi\neq 0
, otherwise we would have
\beta^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\equiv 0
. Hence
\psi\neq 0
, and the pointwise comparison principle again shows that
\psi>0
everywhere in
M
. We now prove that
\psi
again achieves
\lambda_{1}(\beta)
: we indeed have

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\beta)\leq\frac{\int_{M}\psi P_{g}^{s}\psi\,dv_{g}}{\int_{M}\beta\psi^{2}\,dv_{g}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}(\beta)\frac{\int_{M}\psi\beta|\varphi|\,dv_{g}}{\int_{M}\beta\psi^{2}\,dv_{g}}\leq\lambda_{1}(\beta)\left(\frac{\int_{M}\beta\varphi^{2}\,dv_{g}}{\int_{M}\beta\psi^{2}\,dv_{g}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\lambda_{1}(\beta),

where we used Hölder’s inequality and since
\psi\geq|\varphi|
everywhere. This shows that
\psi
attains
\lambda_{1}(\beta)
. In addition, all these inequalities are equalities: the equality case in Hölder’s inequality then shows that
\psi=|\varphi|
everywhere. Since
\psi>0
in
M
this proves (
7.1
) in this case. Assume now that
s=1
. Then up to replacing
\varphi
by
|\varphi
, which still attains
\lambda_{1}(\beta)
, we may assume that
\varphi\geq 0
a.e. in
M
. Then (
7.1
) simply follows from the maximum principle.

We now prove that
E_{1}(\beta)
is one-dimensional. Let
\varphi\in E_{1}(\beta)\backslash\{0\}
. Then
\varphi\in C^{2s}(M)
and up to changing
\varphi
in
-\varphi
, we may assume that
\varphi>0
in
M
by (
7.1
). Let
\theta\in E_{1}(\beta)
, let
x_{0}\in M
and let
t=\frac{\theta(x_{0})}{\varphi(x_{0})}
. The function
\theta-t\varphi
is still in
E_{1}
and, since it vanishes at
x
, it is zero everywhere in
M
by (
7.1
). Thus
\theta=t\varphi
and
E_{1}(\beta)
is one-dimensional, which also proves that
\lambda_{1}(\beta)<\lambda_{2}(\beta)
thanks to Proposition
3.4
.
∎

If
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
we recall the definition of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
introduced in (
4.10
):
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}
. The following result complements Proposition
4.2
and establishes situations in which optimisers of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}(\beta)
are simple:

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
. Let
k\geq 1
,
p\geq\frac{n}{2s}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. Assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
) and that one of the following assumptions is satisfied:

(1)

k=k_{-}
and
\beta
is a local maximum of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}

(2)

k=k_{+}
and
\beta
is a local minimum of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}

(3)

k=2
,
\beta
is a local minimum of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
and we assume that either [
s=1
] or [
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive and satisfies the pointwise comparison principle],

Then
E_{k}(\beta)
has dimension
1
and if
\varphi\in E_{k}(\beta)
is such that
\int_{M}\beta\varphi^{2}\,dv_{g}=1
, we have

\beta^{p-1}=\varphi^{2}.

As a consequence,
\varphi
solves

P_{g}^{s}\varphi=\lambda_{k}(\beta)|\varphi|^{\frac{2}{p-1}}\varphi\quad\text{ in }M.

Furthermore, this
\varphi
is a truly sign-changing function, in the sense that
\varphi_{+}\neq 0
and
\varphi_{-}\neq 0
, in the following cases:

•

If assumption (3) is satisfied

•

If
s=1
and assumptions (1) or (2) are satisfied with, respectively,
k_{-}\geq 2
or
k_{+}\geq 2
.

Recall that if
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive we have
k_{+}=1
. In the particular case
p=\frac{n}{2s}
, and under the assumptions of Proposition
7.2
, the unique
\varphi\in E_{k}(\beta)
with
Q(\beta,\varphi)=1
satisfies

P_{g}^{s}\varphi=\lambda_{k}(\beta)|\varphi|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}\varphi\quad\text{ in }M.

In the case
s=1
, and for negative eigenvalues, this was already observed in
[
24
, Remark 6.1]
.

Proof.

We only prove Proposition
7.2
when
k\geq k_{+}
, since the case
k=k_{-}
follows from the same arguments. Assume thus that
k\geq k_{+}
and, without loss of generality, that
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
is a local minimum of
\bar{\lambda}_{k}^{p}
with
\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}=1
. Under the assumptions of Proposition
7.2
, Proposition
4.2
applies: it shows that for any linear subspace
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))
there exists
k_{+}\leq r\leq k
with
\lambda_{r}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)
, a
Q(\beta,\cdot)
-orthonormal family
(v_{r},\cdots,v_{k})
of
V
and there exist positive numbers
d_{r},\cdots,d_{k}
satisfying
\sum_{i=r}^{k}d_{i}=1
such that

(7.2)

\beta^{p-1}=\sum_{i=r}^{k}d_{i}v_{i}^{2}\quad\text{ a. e. in }M.

Assume first that
k=k_{+}
. Then
i(k)=k=k_{+}
and any linear subspace
V\in\mathcal{G}_{k-i(k)+1}(E_{k}(\beta))=\mathcal{G}_{1}(E_{k}(\beta))
is spanned by a nonzero function
v\in H^{s}(M)
with
Q(\beta,v)=1
. Equation (
7.2
) then applies to
V=\mathbb{R}v
and shows that
r=k_{+}
,
d_{r}=1
and
v_{r}=v
, so that

\beta=|v|^{\frac{2}{p-1}}
a.e. in
M
. Since
P_{g}^{s}v=\lambda_{k_{+}}(\beta)\beta v
we obtain that
P_{g}^{s}v=\lambda_{k}(\beta)|v|^{\frac{2}{p-1}}v
in
M
.
We have thus shown that

\beta^{p-1}=v^{2}
for all
v\in E_{k}(\beta)
with
\int_{M}\beta v^{2}dv_{g}=1
. Assume by contradiction that
E_{k}(\beta)
has dimension greater or equal than
2
, then we can choose
v_{i}
(
i=1,2
) in
E_{k}({\beta})
such that

\int_{M}\beta v_{i}v_{j}dv_{g}={\delta}_{ij}.

We obtain that for all
t
close to
0

\frac{(v_{1}+tv_{2})^{2}}{\int_{M}\beta(v_{1}+tv_{2})^{2}dv_{g}}={\beta}^{p-1}.

Expanding in
t
shows that
v_{1}v_{2}=0
in
M
which contradicts
v_{i}^{2}=\beta^{p-1}
.

Assume now that
[s=1]
or
[P_{g}^{s}
is coercive, satisfies the pointwise comparison principle] and
k=2
. Let
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{p},\|\beta\|_{L^{p}}=1
be a local minimum of
\bar{\lambda}_{2}^{p}
. By the coercivity of
P_{g}^{s}
we have
k_{+}=1
, and Proposition
7.1
applies and shows that
\lambda_{1}(\beta)<\lambda_{2}(\beta)
. Thus
i(2)=2
and any linear subspace
V\in\mathcal{G}_{2-i(2)+1}(E_{2}(\beta))=\mathcal{G}_{1}(E_{2}(\beta))
is spanned by a nonzero function
v\in H^{s}(M)
with
Q(\beta,v)=1
. Equation (
7.2
) then applies to
V=\mathbb{R}v
and again shows that
\beta=|v|^{\frac{2}{p-1}}
a.e. in
M
. We conclude that
\dim E_{2}(\beta)=1
as in the previous case.

It remains to prove that, under the assumptions of the last part of Proposition
7.2
, the unique generator
\varphi\in E_{k}(\beta)
with
Q(\beta,v)=1
is a truly sign-changing function. Assume by contradiction that
v\geq 0
or
v\leq 0
a.e. in
M
. Let
\varphi_{1}>0
be a nonzero generator of
E_{1}(\beta)
which is given by Proposition
7.1
. Again by Proposition
7.1
we have
\lambda_{1}(\beta)<\lambda_{2}(\beta)
, so that
\varphi_{1}
and
v
are
B(\beta,\cdot,\cdot)
-orthogonal, that is
0=\int_{M}\beta\varphi_{1}v\,dv_{g}=\int_{M}\varphi_{1}v^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}\,dv_{g}
. Since
\varphi_{1}>0
in
M
this implies that
v\equiv 0
, which is a contradiction since
\int_{M}|v|^{\frac{2p}{p-1}}\,dv_{g}=\int_{M}\beta^{p}\,dv_{g}=1
.
∎

With Proposition
7.2
we can now prove Corollaries
1.4
and
1.5
:

Proof of Corollaries
1.4
and
1.5
.

We only prove the case
k=k_{+}
since the case
k=k_{-}
is similar. Under the assumptions of Corollary
1.5
,
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained. If
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)\backslash\{0\}
, with
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
, attains
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
. Proposition
7.2
then applies and shows that
\dim E_{k+}(\beta)=1
and that, if we denote by
\varphi
the unique function in
E_{k}(\beta)
with
Q(\beta,\varphi)=1
, we have
\beta=|\varphi|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}
. Also,
\varphi
satisfies

P_{g}^{s}\varphi=\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])|\varphi|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}\varphi\quad\text{ in }M.

That
\varphi
changes sign when
s=1
and
k_{+}\geq 2
also follows from Proposition
7.2
.
∎

Similarly, we are now in position to prove Theorem
1.6
:

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

First, Theorem
1.2
shows that

(7.3)

\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}\leq 2\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

We proceed by contradiction and assume that

(7.4)
