\text{ either }\quad\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}<2\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}\quad\text{ or }\quad\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\quad\text{ is attained. }

Again by Theorem
1.2
, assumption (
7.4
) implies that
\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is attained at some function
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
. Since
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
is coercive and satisfies the pointwise comparison principle by (
2.9
), case
(3)
of Proposition
7.2
applies and shows that there exists a sign-changing
\varphi\in H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
, with
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}|\varphi|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}\,dv_{g_{0}}=1
, such that

(7.5)

P_{g_{0}}^{s}\varphi=\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})|\varphi|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}\varphi\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{S}^{n}.

Integrating against
\varphi
then shows, together with (
7.3
), that

(7.6)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi P_{g_{0}}^{s}\varphi\,dv_{g_{0}}=\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\leq 2^{\frac{2s}{n}}\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

We now use the conformal invariance of
P_{g_{0}}
. We let
N\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
and let
\pi_{N}
denote the stereographic projection that sends
-N
to
0
. It is well-known that
(\pi_{N}^{-1})^{*}g_{0}(x)=U(x)^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}\xi
, where
\xi
is the Euclidean metric in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and where we have let
U(x)=\left(\frac{2}{1+|x|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{2s}}
. We define, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\tilde{\varphi}(x)=\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n-2s}{4s}}\frac{\varphi}{U}\big(\pi_{N}^{-1}(x)\big).

Using (
7.5
) and (
7.6
), the conformal invariance property (
2.7
) of
P_{g_{0}}
shows that
\tilde{\varphi}
satisfies

(7.7)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\xi}^{s}\tilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle=|\tilde{\varphi}|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}\tilde{\varphi}\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big|\Delta_{\xi}^{\frac{s}{2}}\tilde{\varphi}\big|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\varphi P_{g_{0}}^{s}\varphi dv_{g_{0}}=\Lambda_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}\leq 2K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n}{s}},

where
K_{n,s}
is the optimal Sobolev constant defined by (
2.18
) and where we used that
K_{n,s}^{-2}=\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
, which follows from (
4.34
). Equation (
7.7
) then contradicts the result of Lemma
A.1
in the appendix. Hence (
7.4
) cannot occur, which together with (
7.3
) concludes the proof of Theorem
1.6
.
∎

We now prove Theorem
1.7
:

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Let
s\geq 1
and assume that
n\geq 2s+5
. Recall that by (
2.9
)
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
writes as

(7.8)

P_{g_{0}}^{s}=\prod_{j=1}^{s}\Big(\Delta_{g_{0}}+\frac{(n+2j-2)(n-2j)}{4}\Big).

We proceed by contradiction and assume that, for any
3\leq k\leq n+1
,
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is not attained. It then follows from Theorem
1.2
for
k=n+1
that

{\Lambda}_{n+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}={\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\cdots+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{r}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}

for some indexes
\ell_{0},\cdots,\ell_{r}\geq 1
such that
\ell_{0}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=n+1
and such that
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{i}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
is attained for all
0\leq i\leq r
. By Theorem
1.6
and by our contradiction assumption we then have
r=n
and
\ell_{i}=1
for all
i
which implies
that

(7.9)

{\Lambda}_{n+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})=(n+1)^{\frac{2s}{n}}{\Lambda}_{1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})=(n+1)^{\frac{2s}{n}}K_{n,s}^{-2},

where
K_{n,s}
is as in (
2.18
) and where we again used (
4.34
). It is shown in
[
38
]
that the explicit value of
K_{n,s}
is given by

K_{n,s}^{-2}=2^{2s}\pi^{s}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n+2s}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-2s}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(n)}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n}},

where
\Gamma
denotes the Gamma function. It is shown in
[
4
,
60
]
that
K_{n,1}^{-2}=\frac{n(n-2)}{4}\omega_{n}^{\frac{2}{n}}
where
\omega_{n}
is the volume of
(\mathbb{S}^{n},g_{0})
.
We deduce from the latter and standard properties of the Gamma function that

\omega_{n}^{\frac{2s}{n}}=2^{2s}\pi^{s}\left(\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(n)}\right)^{\frac{2s}{n}},

so that
K_{n,s}^{-2}=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n+2s}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-2s}{2}\right)}\omega_{n}^{\frac{2s}{n}}
. Standard properties of the Gamma function finally show that

(7.10)

K_{n,s}^{-2}=\frac{\omega_{n}^{\frac{2s}{n}}}{2^{2s}}\prod_{j=-s}^{s-1}(n+2j).

We now estimate the left-hand side of (
7.9
) from above. First, the constant function
1
is an eigenfunction of
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
associated to the eigenvalue
\frac{1}{2^{2s}}\prod_{j=1}^{s}(n+2j-2)(n-2j)
by (
7.8
). The coordinate functions
x_{i}
of
\doba{R}^{n+1}
, for
1\leq i\leq n+1
, when restricted to
\doba{S}^{n}
are eigenfunction of
\Delta_{g_{0}}
associated to the eigenvalue
n
. As a consequence, and again by (
7.8
), they are also eigenfunctions of
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
associated to the eigenvalue

(7.11)

\lambda_{0}=\frac{1}{2^{2s}}\prod_{j=1}^{s}\Big(4n+(n+2j-2)(n-2j)\Big)=\frac{1}{2^{2s}}\prod_{j=1}^{s}(n-2j+2)(n+2j).

By definition of
{\Lambda}_{n+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
we thus have
\lambda_{0}\omega_{n}^{\frac{2s}{n}}\geq{\Lambda}_{n+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
. Using (
7.9
), (
7.10
) and the expression of
\lambda_{0}
, the latter inequality becomes

(7.12)

\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}=\frac{1+\frac{2s}{n}}{1-\frac{2s}{n}}\geq(n+1)^{\frac{2s}{n}}.

For
\alpha\in[0,1)
we define

F(\alpha)=\frac{1+\alpha}{1-\alpha}-(n+1)^{\alpha}.

Simple considerations show that
F
is continuous and that
F>0
for
\alpha
close to
1
. As
\alpha\to 0
we have
F(\alpha)=\big(2-\ln(n+1)\big)\alpha+o(\alpha)
, so that
F(0)=0
and
F(\alpha)<0
for
\alpha>0
small, since the assumption
n\geq 2s+5
implies in particular
n\geq 7
and thus
\ln(n+1)>2
. As a consequence,
F
vanishes in
(0,1)
. We denote by
\alpha_{0}
the smallest positive zero of
F
in
(0,1)
. Inequality (
7.12
) shows that
\frac{2s}{n}\geq\alpha_{0}
. Straightforward computations show that, for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

(7.13)

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{2-\ln(n+1)(1-\alpha^{2})}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}+\ln(n+1)F(\alpha)\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{4-\ln(n+1)^{2}(1-\alpha)(1-\alpha^{2})}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}+\ln(n+1)^{2}F(\alpha).

Since
F<0
in
[0,\alpha_{0})
we have
F^{\prime}(\alpha_{0})\geq 0
, which implies
1-\alpha_{0}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{\ln(n+1)}
. The latter implies, since
\alpha_{0}>0
, that
1-\alpha_{0}<\frac{2}{\ln(n+1)}
and thus that
4-\ln(n+1)^{2}(1-\alpha_{0})(1-\alpha_{0}^{2})>0
, which in turn implies that
F^{\prime\prime}(\alpha_{0})>0
. Thus
F
changes sign at
\alpha_{0}
and becomes positive in a right-neighbourhood of
\alpha_{0}
. Any
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
now satisfies
\alpha^{2}>\alpha_{0}^{2}\geq 1-\frac{2}{\ln(n+1)}
, so that (
7.13
) shows that
F
is increasing in
(\alpha_{0},1]
and hence that
\alpha_{0}
is the only zero of
F
in
(0,1)
.

Assume first that
n=7
, in which case
s=1
. Since
F(\frac{1}{3})=0
we have
\alpha_{0}=\frac{1}{3}
, and inequality (
7.12
) shows that
\frac{2}{n}\geq\frac{1}{3}
which is a contradiction. We may now assume that
n\geq 8
(and
n\geq 2s+5
). We have

F\big(1-\frac{4}{n}\big)=\frac{n-2}{2}-(n+1)^{1-\frac{4}{n}}\leq 0,

which in turn implies that
\alpha_{0}\geq 1-\frac{4}{n}
. Inequality (
7.12
) then shows that

\frac{2s}{n}\geq\alpha_{0}\geq 1-\frac{4}{n},

which contradicts the assumption
n\geq 2s+5
. This proves that there exists
k\in\{3,\cdots,n+1\}
such that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is attained.

We let
k\in\{3,\cdots,n+1\}
be such that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
is attained. We proceed by contradiction and assume that it is attained by the round metric. By this we mean that
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})={\lambda}_{k}(P_{g_{0}}^{s})\omega_{n}^{\frac{2s}{n}}
, or in other words that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
is equal to
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
, where
\beta_{0}
is the constant function equal to
\omega_{n}^{-\frac{2s}{n}}
that satisfies
\|\beta_{0}\|_{\frac{n}{2s}}=1
. We first observe that the eigenfunctions of
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
are still given by the restriction to
\mathbb{S}^{n}
of homogeneous harmonic polynomials in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Indeed, these polynomials are still eigenfunctions of
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
by (
7.8
), and they form a Hilbert basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
since they are the eigenfunction of
\Delta_{g_{0}}
. They therefore exhaust all the eigenfunctions of
P_{g_{0}}^{s}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
by the spectral theorem. The coordinate functions
x_{i}
of
\doba{R}^{n+1}
, for
1\leq i\leq n+1
, when restricted to
\doba{S}^{n}
are the eigenfunction of
\Delta_{g_{0}}
associated to the least positive eigenvalue
n
. Since
k\leq n+1
they thus also span the eigenspace associated to
\lambda_{k}(\beta_{0})
and we have

\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})=\prod_{j=1}^{s}\left(n+\frac{(n+2j-2)(n-2j)}{4}\right)\omega_{n}^{\frac{2s}{n}}.

Since
\beta_{0}
attains
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
, Proposition
4.2
applies and shows that there exist nonnegative numbers
d_{1}\cdots,d_{n+1}
, satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}d_{i}=1
and at most
k-1
of which are non-zero, such that

\beta_{0}={\omega}_{n}^{-\frac{n-2s}{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}d_{i}x_{i}^{2}\quad\text{ a. e. in }\doba{S}^{n}.

Since the left-hand side is constant over
\doba{S}^{n}
and
k-1<n+1
we obtain a contradiction.
∎

Let
k
be the integer in the statement of Theorem
1.7
. The inequality
3\leq k\leq n+1
is valid for any
n\geq 2s+5
, and it can be easily verified that it is sharp for low values of
n
and
s
. As
n
gets large, however, we obtain a much better bound on
k
that does not depend on
s
:

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
s\geq 1
be fixed and assume that
n\geq 2s+5
. Let

k_{n}=\min\Big\{k\in\{3,\cdots,n+1\},\quad\Lambda_{k}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})\text{ is attained }\Big\}.

As
n\to+\infty
, we have

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}k_{n}\leq\big[e^{2}\big]+1

where
[\cdot]
denotes the integer part.

Proof.

We adapt the argument in the proof of Theorem
1.7
by keeping the same notations. By definition of
k_{n}
and by Theorem
1.6
,
{\Lambda}_{k_{n}-1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
is not attained. It then follows from Theorem
1.2
applied to
k=k_{n}-1
and from Theorem
1.6
that

{\Lambda}_{k_{n}-1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}=(k_{n}-1)^{\frac{2s}{n}}K_{n,s}^{-2},

where
K_{n,s}
is as in (
2.18
) and where we again used (
4.34
). Since
k_{n}\leq n+1
by Theorem
1.7
we again have
{\Lambda}_{k_{n}-1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})\leq{\Lambda}_{n+1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})\leq\lambda_{0}\omega_{n}^{\frac{2s}{n}}
where
\lambda_{0}
is given by (
7.11
). Arguing as in the proof of Theorem
1.7
we thus obtain

(k_{n}-1)^{\frac{2s}{n}}\leq\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}\quad\text{ and thus }\quad k_{n}\leq 1+\left(\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}\right)^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

Expanding the right-hand side as
n\to+\infty
yields

k_{n}\leq 1+e^{2}+O\big(\frac{1}{n}\big)

and proves the result.
∎

8.
Strict monotonicity of the sequence
(\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g]))_{k}

In this short section we prove Theorem
1.10
. We obtain this result by induction, as a very natural consequence of our main existence result, Theorem
1.2
, and of the variational theory for the eigenvalue functionals that we developed in Section
4
. We first prove the following Lemma:

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a non-empty set and
K
a field. We denote by
\mathcal{F}(\Omega,K)
the vector space of
K
-valued functions on
{\Omega}
. Let
\ell\geq 1
. Assume that there exists

•

\beta\in\mathcal{F}(\Omega,K)
;

•

a subspace
F\subset\mathcal{F}(\Omega,K)
of dimension
\ell+1
such that for all subspace
V\subset F
of dimension
\ell
, there exists a finite family
f_{1},\cdots,f_{r}\in V
such that

\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{r}f_{i}^{2}.

Then
\beta=0
.

Proof.

Let
x\in{\Omega}
and let
e:F\to K
be the evaluation at
x
defined for all
f\in F
by
e(f)=f(x)
.
It is a linear map whose kernel has dimension
\ell
if
e\not=0
and
\ell+1
otherwise. In any case, we can choose
V\subset{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(e)
of dimension
\ell
.
From the assumptions of Lemma
8.1
, there exists
f_{1},\cdots,f_{r}\in V
such that

\beta=f_{1}^{2}+\cdots+f_{r}^{2}.

By construction, it holds that for all
f\in V
,
v(x)=0
. In particular, for all
i=1,\cdots,r
,
f_{i}(x)=0
which implies

\beta(x)=0
. Since
x
is arbitrary, this proves Lemma
8.1
.
∎

When applied to generalised eigenfunctions, Lemma
8.1
yields the following result:

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2s
and let
P_{g}^{s}
be the GJMS operator of order
2s
. Assume that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
). We assume that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained at some
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)\backslash\{0\},\beta\geq 0
. Then
\lambda_{k+1}(\beta)>\lambda_{k}(\beta)
if
k\geq k_{+}
and
\lambda_{k-1}(\beta)<\lambda_{k}(\beta)
if
k\leq k_{-}

We recall that
k_{-}
and
k_{+}
are defined in (
4.11
). They depend on
(M,[g])
but for the sake of clarity we simply denote them by
k_{-},k_{+}
.

Proof.

We only prove the result for
k\geq k_{+}
. The other case is similar. Up to renormalizing
\beta
, we may assume that
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
so that
\lambda_{k}(\beta)=\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
. We assume by contradiction that
\lambda_{k+1}(\beta)=\lambda_{k}(\beta)
. We let
i(k)
be given by (
4.1
), so that

i(k)=\min\left\{r\geq k_{+};{\lambda}_{r}(\beta)={\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\right\}.

By Proposition
3.4
we can choose a linearly independent family
(v_{i(k)},\cdots,v_{k+1})\in H^{s}(M)
of generalised eigenfunctions such that for all
i
,

P_{g}^{s}v_{i}={\lambda}\beta v_{i}

where
{\lambda}={\lambda}_{i}(\beta)
for
i=\{i(k),\cdots,k+1\}.
Set
F={\mathop{\rm Span}}\{v_{i(k)},\cdots,v_{k+1}\}
. Let
\ell=k+1-i(k)\geq 1
so that
\dim(F)=\ell+1
.
Let
V
be any
\ell
-dimensional subspace of
F
and choose a
Q(\beta,\cdot)
-orthonormal family
v^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,v^{\prime}_{\ell}
in
V
. Then since
\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])={\lambda}_{k}(\beta)={\lambda}
and since
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
,

P_{g}^{s}v^{\prime}_{i}={\lambda}\beta v^{\prime}_{i}

we get from Proposition
4.2
that there exists a family

f_{1},\cdots,f_{\ell}\in V
such that

(8.1)

\beta^{\frac{n}{2s}-1}=f_{1}^{2}+\cdots+f_{\ell}^{2}\quad\text{a.e. in }M.

The arguments given in Remark
4.2
show, since
\beta
attains
\lambda_{k}(\beta)
, that
\beta
and the functions
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}
appearing in (
8.1
) are continuous in
M
. Equality (
8.1
) therefore becomes a pointwise equality between functions in
M
, and Lemma
8.1
applies: it shows that that
\beta=0
, which contradicts the assumption
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
.
∎

We are in position to prove Theorem
1.10

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

We proceed by contradiction and assume that there exists a closed Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
of dimension
n>2s
such that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies (
1.3
) and
k
such that

(8.2)

\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])=\Lambda^{s}_{k+1}(M,[g])\not=0.

Then by Corollary
1.3
, it holds that
k+1\leq k_{-}
or
k\geq k_{+}
.

We first assume that
k+1\leq k_{-}
. By Theorem
1.1
,
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained at some
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)\backslash\{0\},\beta\geq 0
, with
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
. By definition of
\Lambda^{s}_{k+1}(M,[g])
we have

\Lambda^{s}_{k+1}(M,[g])\geq{\lambda}_{k+1}(\beta)\geq{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)=\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])=\Lambda^{s}_{k+1}(M,[g]).

Then,
{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)={\lambda}_{k+1}(\beta)
and
\Lambda^{s}_{k+1}(M,[g])
is attained at
\beta
, which contradicts Lemma
8.2
.

From now on, we assume that
k\geq k_{+}
. Assume first that
\Lambda_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained at some
\beta\in L^{\frac{n}{2s}}(M)\backslash\{0\},\beta\geq 0
, with
\|\beta\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1
. We have, again by definition of
\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])
,

(8.3)

\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])\leq{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)\leq{\lambda}_{k+1}(\beta)=\Lambda^{s}_{k+1}(M,[g])=\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g]).

This implies at once that
\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])={\lambda}_{k}(\beta)
, and thus that
\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])
is also attained, and that
{\lambda}_{k}(\beta)={\lambda}_{k+1}(\beta)
. This again contradicts Lemma
8.2
.

We may thus finally assume that
\Lambda_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])
is not attained. By Theorem
1.2
, there exist integers
\ell_{0},\cdots,\ell_{r}
such that

(1)

\ell_{0}\in\{0\}\cup\{k_{+},\cdots,k\}
, where by convention we let
\Lambda_{0}^{s}(M,[g])=0
;

(2)

\ell_{0}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=k+1
if
\ell_{0}\geq k_{+}
and
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=k-k_{+}+2
if
\ell_{0}=0
;

(3)

{\Lambda}_{\ell_{i}}(\doba{S}^{n})
,
1\leq i\leq r
are attained, and
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained if
\ell_{0}>0
.

and

(8.4)

\Lambda^{s}_{k+1}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}={\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\cdots+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{r}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

Since
\ell_{0}<k+1
we have
r\geq 1
, and in particular
\ell_{1}\geq 1
and thus
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}^{\prime}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})>0
. Combining (
8.2
) and (
8.4
) we thus obtain

\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}={\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\cdots+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{r}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}>{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}},

which in particular implies that

(8.5)

\ell_{0}\in\{0\}\cup\{k_{+},\cdots,k-1\}.

For any
0\leq i\leq
we now define

\ell_{0}^{\prime}=\ell_{0},\quad\ell_{1}^{\prime}=\ell_{1}-1\quad\text{ and }\quad\ell_{i}^{\prime}=\ell_{i}\quad\text{ if }2\leq i\leq r,

so that by (
8.5
) we have

(1)

\ell_{0}^{\prime}\in\{0\}\cup\{k_{+},\cdots,k-1\}
;

(2)

\ell_{0}^{\prime}+\cdots+\ell_{r}^{\prime}=k
if
\ell_{0}\geq k_{+}
and
\ell_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+\ell_{r}^{\prime}=k-k_{+}+1
if
\ell_{0}=0
.

We deduce from Proposition
6.3
that

\Lambda^{s}_{k}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}\leq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}^{\prime}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}^{\prime}-1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\cdots+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{r}^{\prime}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

Together with (
8.2
) and (
8.4
) this implies that
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}^{\prime}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})\geq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
from which we deduce that

(8.6)

{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}-1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})={\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n}).

If
\Lambda_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
were attained, we would get a contradiction as in (
8.3
) by using (
8.6
) and Lemma
8.2
. Thus
\Lambda_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
is not attained, which contradicts the definition of
\ell_{1}
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.10
.
∎

9.
Estimating the invariants
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])

In this section we perform test-function computations to prove that, in some cases, the strict inequality in Theorem
1.2
is satisfied, and thus that the invariant
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained at a generalised metric. As a consequence we prove Theorems
1.8
and
1.11
.

9.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.8

We first introduce some notations that will be needed throughout this section. Let
\xi\in M
be a fixed point. If
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat, up to a conformal change of metric we can assume that
g
is flat in a neighbourhood of
\xi
. If
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat, up to replacing the metric
g
by a conformal metric
\tilde{g}\in[g]
we may assume, by the celebrated conformal normal coordinates results of
[
8
,
21
,
36
]
that
g
satisfies

(9.1)

\det g(x)=1\quad\text{ for all }x\in U,

where
U
is an open neighbourhood of
\xi
in
M
. Relation (
9.1
) has several fundamental consequences: it implies for instance that

\text{Ric}(\xi)=0,\quad S_{g}(\xi)=0\text{ and }\quad\Delta_{g}S_{g}(\xi)=\frac{1}{6}|W_{g}(\xi)|_{g}^{2},

where
W_{g}
is the Weyl tensor of
(M,g)
. More generally, (
9.1
) implies that all symmetrised covariant derivatives of
\text{Ric}_{g}
at
\xi
vanish to an arbitrary order. We refer to
[
36
]
for a proof of these facts. We assume in this subsection that
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
. Then we can let
G_{g}^{s}
be its Green’s function. It is the unique smooth function in
M\times M\backslash\{(x,x),x\in M\}
that satisfies, for
x\in M
,

P_{g}^{s}G_{g}^{s}(x,\cdot)=\delta_{x}

in
M
. Note that we do not assume here that
G_{g}^{s}(x,\cdot)
is positive in
M\backslash\{x\}
, that is, we do not assume here that
P_{g}^{s}
satisfies the pointwise comparison principle. Assume that one of the following conditions is satisfied:

(9.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &2s+1\leq n\leq 2s+3\text{ and }\eqref{eq:coord:conformes}\text{ is satisfied in a neighbourhood of }\xi,\text{ or }\\
&(M,g)\text{ is flat in a neighbourhood of }\xi\text{ and }n\geq 2s+4.\end{aligned}\right.

Then the Green’s function
G_{g}^{s}(\xi,\cdot)
has the following expansion in exponential coordinates for
g
at
\xi
:

(9.3)

G_{g}^{s}(\xi,x)=b_{n,s}d_{g}(\xi,x)^{2s-n}+m(\xi)+o(1)\quad\text{ as }x\to\xi,

where
b_{n,s}
is an explicit constant given by

b_{n,s}^{-1}=2^{k-1}(k-1)!\prod_{i=1}^{s}(n-2i)\omega_{n-1}

and
\omega_{n-1}
is the area of the standard sphere (see
[
36
]
for
k=1
and
[
42
]
for
k\geq 2
for a proof of this statement). The real number
m(\xi)
appearing in (
9.3
) is called the mass of
G_{g}^{s}
at
\xi
. For
x\neq\xi
we let

(9.4)

h_{s,\xi}(x)=G_{g}^{s}(\xi,x)-b_{n,s}d_{g}(\xi,x)^{2s-n},

which is a continuous function in
M
.

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

Let
\xi\in M
and
\delta>0
. Throughout this proof we will assume that
g
is either flat or satisfies (
9.1
) in
B_{g}(\xi,3\delta)
, where
3\delta<i_{g}(M)
and
i_{g}(M)
denotes the injectivity radius of
(M,g)
. As explained above this can always be achieved up to replacing
g
by a metric conformal to
g
. Recall that we assume that
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
. For
u\in H^{s}(M)\backslash\{0\}
we let

I(u)=\frac{\int_{M}uP_{g}^{s}udv_{g}}{\left(\int_{M}|u|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}}.

The proof of Theorem
1.8
follows from test-function computations. Since we cover the case of a general
k_{+}\geq 1
, however, our test-function computations are more involved than the usual Rayleigh quotient estimates. In accordance with the assumptions of Theorem
1.8
we will distinguish two cases in this proof:

•

the case where
n\geq 2s+4
and
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat

•

the case where (
9.2
) holds and the mass is positive somewhere.

In the following the point
\xi
will always be assumed to satisfy

(9.5)

\left\{\begin{aligned} |W_{g}(\xi)|_{g}&>0&\quad\text{ if }n\geq 2s+4\text{ and }(M,g)\text{ is not l.c.f }\\
m(\xi)&>0\quad&\text{ if }\eqref{eq:hyp:masse}\text{ holds. }\end{aligned}\right.

Step
1
: Choice of the test-functions.
Let
B:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be given by

(9.6)

B(x)=\left(1+\frac{|x|^{2}}{\Gamma_{n,s}}\right)^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}\quad\text{ for }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

where
\Gamma_{n,s}=\Big(\Pi_{j=-s}^{s-1}(n+2j)\Big)^{\frac{1}{s}}
. It is well-known (see
[
38
,
40
]
) that
B
satisfies

(9.7)

P_{\xi}^{s}B=\Delta_{\xi}^{s}B=B^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}

and that, up to a multiplication, translation and scaling,
B
attains
K_{n,s}
in (
2.18
). In particular,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}B^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dx=K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n}{s}}
. We let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B(0,3\delta))
satisfy
\chi\equiv 1
in
B(0,\delta)
. For any
\epsilon>0
and any
x\in M
we define

(9.8)

B_{\epsilon}(x)=\chi\big(d_{g}(\xi,x)\big)\epsilon^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}B\Big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}{\mathop{\rm exp}}_{\xi}^{-1}(x)\Big),

where
{\mathop{\rm exp}}_{\xi}
denotes the exponential map for
g
at
\xi
. For
\epsilon>0
small enough, we now let

•

If
n\geq 2s+4
and
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat:

(9.9)

\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}=B_{\epsilon}+\left(1-\chi\big(d_{g}(\xi,x)\big)\right)\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}

•

If
2s+1\leq n\leq 2s+3
or
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat:

(9.10)

\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}=B_{\epsilon}+\frac{\Gamma_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}}{b_{n,s}}\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\Big[\chi\big(d_{g}(\xi,x)\big)h_{s,\xi}+\big(1-\chi\big(d_{g}(\xi,x)\big)\big)G_{g}^{s}(\xi,\cdot)\Big],

where
b_{n,s},\Gamma_{n,s}
and
h_{s,\xi}
are as in (
9.3
), (
9.4
) and (
9.6
).

Equation (
9.3
) shows in particular that
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
is smooth in
M
. First, straightforward computations using (
9.1
), (
9.9
) and (
9.10
) show that

(9.11)

\int_{M}|\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}=K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n}{s}}+O(\epsilon^{n})

as
\epsilon\to 0
. For any
\epsilon>0
we now define

(9.12)

u_{\varepsilon}=\|\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}}^{-1}\tilde{u}_{\epsilon},

where
\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}
is given by (
9.9
) or (
9.10
). It was proven in
[
40
, Proposition 2.1, Proposition 3.1 ]
that the following expansions hold true as
\epsilon\to 0
:

•

If
n\geq 2s+4
and
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat at
\xi
,

(9.13)

I(u_{\epsilon})=K_{n,s}^{-2}-C|W_{g}(\xi)|_{g}^{2}\left\{\begin{aligned} &\epsilon^{4}\ln\frac{1}{\epsilon}+O(\epsilon^{4})&\text{ if }n=2s+4\\
&\epsilon^{4}+o(\epsilon^{4})&\text{ if }n\geq 2s+5\\
\end{aligned}\right.

•

If
2s+1\leq n\leq 2s+3
or
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat,

(9.14)

I(u_{\epsilon})=K_{n,s}^{-2}-Cm(\xi)\epsilon^{n-2s}+o\big(\epsilon^{n-2s}\big).

In both (
9.13
) and (
9.14
),
C=C(n,s)
is a fixed positive constant. These expansions heavily rely on the assumption (
9.1
) in order to obtain a precise expansion of
P_{g}^{s}
around
\xi
using Juhl’s formulae, see e.g.
[
40
, Step 2.1]
. For
1\leq\ell\leq k_{+}-1
we let
\lambda_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}(g)
be the
k_{+}-1
first eigenvalues of
P_{g}^{s}
and we let
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1}
be the associated eigenvectors. They satisfy:

(9.15)

\displaystyle P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\ell}\varphi_{\ell}\quad\text{ in }M,

\displaystyle\int_{M}P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{p}\varphi_{q}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{p}\delta_{p,q}\text{ and }\int_{M}\varphi_{p}\varphi_{q}dv_{g}=\delta_{p,q}\quad\text{ for }p,q\geq 1.

Straightforward computations using (
9.9
), (
9.11
) and (
9.12
) show that

(9.16)

\displaystyle\int_{M}|u_{\epsilon}|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}u_{\epsilon}\varphi_{\ell}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=O\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big),

\displaystyle\int_{M}|u_{\epsilon}|^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}\varphi_{\ell}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=O\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big)\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\int_{M}\varphi_{\ell}u_{\varepsilon}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=O\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big)

as
\epsilon\to 0
. When
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat or of small dimension we improve these estimates:

Lemma 9.1
.

Assume that
2s+1\leq n\leq 2s+3
or that
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat. Let
u_{\epsilon}
be given by (
9.12
). There is a positive constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(n,s)
such that, as
\epsilon\to 0
,

(9.17)

\displaystyle\int_{M}|u_{\epsilon}|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}u_{\epsilon}\varphi_{\ell}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=C_{0}K_{n,s}^{\frac{n+2s}{2s}}\varphi_{\ell}(\xi)\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}+o\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big)\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\int_{M}\varphi_{\ell}u_{\varepsilon}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\ell}}C_{0}K_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2s}}\varphi_{\ell}(\xi)\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}+o\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big).

Proof.

First, straightforward computations using (
9.10
) show that

\int_{M}|u_{\epsilon}|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}u_{\epsilon}\varphi_{\ell}\,dv_{g}=\Big(K_{n,s}^{\frac{n+2s}{2s}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}B^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}\,dv_{\xi}\Big)\varphi_{\ell}(\xi)\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}+o\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big)

as
\epsilon\to 0
. Independently, the definition of
\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
in (
9.10
) shows that there exists a positive constant
C
independent of
\epsilon
such that
\epsilon^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(x)\leq\frac{C}{d_{g}(\xi,x)^{n-2s}}
for any
x\neq\xi
and that

\epsilon^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}\tilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(x)\to b_{n,s}^{-1}\Gamma_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}G_{g}^{s}(\xi,x)

pointwise in
M\backslash\{\xi\}
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Dominated convergence then shows that

\displaystyle\int_{M}\varphi_{\ell}u_{\varepsilon}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=b_{n,s}^{-1}\Gamma_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}K_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2s}}\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\int_{M}G_{g}^{s}(\xi,x)\varphi_{\ell}\,dv_{g}+o\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}}{b_{n,s}\lambda_{\ell}}K_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2s}}\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\varphi_{\ell}(\xi)+o\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big),

where the last line follows from (
9.15
) and a representation formula. To conclude the proof of the lemma we thus only need to show that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}B^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}\,dv_{\xi}=\frac{\Gamma_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}}{b_{n,s}}.

This easily follows by writing down a representation formula for
B
in (
9.6
) as in
[
55
, Lemma 2.1]
. Recall that
x\mapsto b_{n,s}|x|^{2s-n}
is the fundamental solution of
\Delta_{\xi}^{s}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By (
9.7
), for a fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and for
R>2|x|
we have

\displaystyle B(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{b_{n,s}}{|x-y|^{n-2s}}B^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}(y)\,dv_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{\xi}(0,R)}\frac{b_{n,s}}{|x-y|^{n-2s}}B^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}(y)\,dv_{\xi}+O\Big(\frac{1}{R^{2s}|x|^{n-2s}}\Big).

Multiplying by
|x|^{n-2s}
on both sides and letting
|x|\to+\infty
then
R\to+\infty
proves the claim.
∎

Remark 9.1
.

The arguments in the proof of (
9.17
) also show that

\int_{M}|u_{\epsilon}|^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}\varphi_{\ell}\,dv_{g}=C_{0}K_{n,s}^{\frac{n+2s}{2s}}\varphi_{\ell}(\xi)\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}+o\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big)

where
C_{0}
is the same constant than in (
9.17
).

Step
2
: choice of the
k_{+}
-dimensional subspace.

We finally let, for any
\epsilon>0
,

\beta_{\epsilon}=|u_{\epsilon}|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}+\epsilon^{n+1}.

Remark that
\beta_{\epsilon}\in C^{0}(M)
and that
\beta_{\epsilon}>0
in
M
. By definition of
u_{\epsilon}
in (
9.12
) we have

(9.18)

\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}=1+o(\epsilon^{n})

as
\epsilon\to 0
. We will prove that, for
\epsilon
small enough,

(9.19)

\lambda_{k_{+}}(\beta_{\epsilon})\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}<K_{n,s}^{-2}=\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})

holds, where the last equality follows from (
4.34
). By definition of
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
this will imply that
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])<\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
and conclude the proof of Theorem
1.8
. For
\epsilon>0
we let

V_{\epsilon}=\text{Span}\big(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1},u_{\epsilon}\big),

where
u_{\epsilon}
is given by (
9.12
) and the
\varphi_{i}
are as in (
9.15
). We first claim that

(9.20)

\dim_{\beta_{\epsilon}}V_{\epsilon}=k_{+}

for
\epsilon
small enough. Indeed, since
\beta_{\epsilon}>0
, we have
\beta_{\epsilon}^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi_{\ell}\neq 0
for all
1\leq\ell\leq k_{+}-1
. Proving that
(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1},u_{\epsilon})
is free in
L^{2}_{\beta_{\epsilon}}(M)
thus amounts to prove that
(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1},u_{\epsilon})
is free in
L^{2}(M)
. Since
(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1})
is free in
L^{2}(M)
we just need to show that
u_{\epsilon}\not\in\text{Span}\big\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1}\big\}
. Assume by contradiction that
u_{\epsilon}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{+}-1}\mu_{i,\epsilon}\varphi_{i}
for some
\mu_{i,\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}
. Integrating against
\varphi_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k_{+}-1
and using (
9.15
) and (
9.16
) shows that
\mu_{i,\epsilon}=o(1)
as
\epsilon\to 0
for all
i
, which is a contradiction with the condition
\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}}=1
. This proves (
9.20
). The subspace
V_{\epsilon}
is thus admissible in the definition of
\lambda_{k_{+}}(\beta_{\epsilon})
given by (
3.2
): for any
\epsilon>0
, we can thus let
\alpha_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{S}^{k_{+}-1}
be such that

v_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\varepsilon}\varphi_{\ell}+\alpha_{k_{+},\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}

satisfies

(9.21)

\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})=\max_{v\in V_{\epsilon}\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v).

We are going to prove that

(9.22)

\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}<K_{n,s}^{-2}

for
\epsilon
small enough, which will prove (
9.19
). Without loss of generality we will assume from now on that the right-hand side of (
9.21
) is nonnegative, otherwise Theorem
1.8
is true.
Straightforward computations using (
9.15
) show that

(9.23)

\int_{M}v_{\epsilon}P_{g}^{s}v_{\epsilon}dv_{g}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\lambda_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}+\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}I(u_{\epsilon})+2\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\lambda_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\int_{M}\varphi_{\ell}u_{\epsilon}dv_{g}.

We similarly have, using the definition of
\beta_{\epsilon}
,

(9.24)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}v_{\epsilon}^{2}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}|u_{\epsilon}|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}\big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\varphi_{\ell}\big)^{2}dv_{g}+

\displaystyle+\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}+2\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\int_{M}\varphi_{\ell}|u_{\epsilon}|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}u_{\epsilon}\,dv_{g}+o(\epsilon^{n}),

and thus

(9.25)

\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}v_{\epsilon}^{2}\,dv_{g}\geq\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}+2\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\int_{M}|u_{\epsilon}|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}u_{\epsilon}\varphi_{\ell}\,dv_{g}+o(\epsilon^{n}).

Up to passing to a subsequence
(\epsilon_{m})_{m\geq 0}
with
\epsilon_{m}\to 0
as
m\to+\infty
, we can assume that
\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon_{m}}
converges towards
\alpha_{\ell,0}
for any
1\leq\ell\leq k_{+}
, that still satisfy

\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}}\alpha_{\ell,0}^{2}=1.

In the following, for the sake of clarity, we will omit the subscript
m
, and will keep denoting the sequences
\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon_{m}},u_{\epsilon_{m}},v_{\epsilon_{m}},\dots
by
\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon},u_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon},\dots
.

As a first observation, we remark that we can assume that
\alpha_{k_{+},0}^{2}=1
. Indeed, assume first that
\alpha_{k_{+},0}=0
. Since
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
by assumption we have
\lambda_{\ell}<0
for all
\ell\leq k_{+}-1
, so that

\int_{M}v_{\epsilon}P_{g}^{s}v_{\epsilon}dv_{g}\to\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\lambda_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell,0}^{2}<0.

as
\epsilon\to 0
. The quantity
\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})
is thus nonpositive for
\epsilon
small enough and since
\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}v_{\epsilon}^{2}dv_{g}>0
this proves (
9.22
) in this case. Assume now that
0<\alpha_{k_{+},0}^{2}<1
, so that
\sum_{\ell=0}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,0}^{2}>0
. Using (
9.13
), (
9.14
), (
9.16
), (
9.18
), (
9.23
)
(
9.25
) we obtain that

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})\|{\beta}_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}\leq\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\lambda_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell,0}^{2}+\alpha_{k_{+},0}^{2}K_{n,s}^{-2}}{\alpha_{k_{+},0}^{2}}<K_{n,s}^{-2}

as
\epsilon\to 0
, since
\lambda_{\ell}<0
for any
0\leq\ell\leq k_{+}-1
. This proves (
9.22
) in this case. We may therefore assume in the rest of the proof of Theorem
1.8
that

\alpha_{k_{+},0}^{2}=1\quad\text{ and }\quad\alpha_{\ell,0}=0\quad\text{ for all }\quad 1\leq\ell\leq k_{+}-1.

We first assume that
n\geq 2s+4
and
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat.
Recall that
\xi
is chosen so that
W_{g}(\xi)\neq 0
, where
W_{g}
is the Weyl tensor of
(M,g)
. For each
1\leq\ell\leq k_{+}-1
fixed, two situations may occur along the subsequence we are considering as
\epsilon\to 0
:

(9.26)

\text{ either }\quad\frac{|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|}{\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}}\to+\infty\quad\text{ or }\quad|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|=O(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}).

We let
0\leq L\leq k_{+}-1
be the number of indexes
\ell\in\{1,\dots,k_{+}-1\}
satisfying the first case in (
9.26
).
L
can be assumed to be constant up to passing to a subsequence. Up to relabeling the indexes we may assume that
\alpha_{1,\epsilon},\dots,\alpha_{L,\epsilon}
satisfy the first case in (
9.26
) and we may thus write that

(9.27)

\epsilon^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|\to+\infty\quad\text{ and }\quad\sum_{\ell=L+1}^{k_{+}-1}|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|=O(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}})

as
\epsilon\to 0
, where it is intended that the first sum is empty if
L=0
and the second sum is empty if
L=k_{+}-1
. . Using (
9.16
) together with (
9.18
) and (
9.27
) we can write that

\displaystyle\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\lambda_{\ell}\int_{M}\varphi_{\ell}u_{\epsilon}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=O\Big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|\Big)=o\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}\Big)+O\big(\epsilon^{n-2s}\big).

Inserting the latter in (
9.23
) we obtain

(9.28)

\displaystyle\int_{M}v_{\epsilon}P_{g}^{s}v_{\epsilon}dv_{g}=\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}I(u_{\epsilon})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\big(\lambda_{\ell}+o(1)\big)\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}+O\big(\epsilon^{n-2s}\big)

as
\epsilon\to 0
. Similarly, (
9.16
) and (
9.27
) show that

2\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\int_{M}\varphi_{\ell,\epsilon}|u_{\epsilon}|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}u_{\epsilon}\,dv_{g}=o\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}\Big)+O(\epsilon^{n-2s}),

so that equation (
9.25
) becomes

(9.29)

\displaystyle\int_{M}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\beta}_{\epsilon}v_{\epsilon}^{2}\,dv_{g}\geq\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}+o\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}\Big)+O\big(\epsilon^{n-2s}\big).

Combining (
9.18
), (
9.28
) and (
9.29
) shows, since
\alpha_{k,0}^{2}=1
, that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}

\displaystyle\leq I(u_{\varepsilon})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\big(\lambda_{\ell}+o(1)\big)\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}+O\big(\epsilon^{n-2s}\big)

\displaystyle\leq I(u_{\varepsilon})+O\big(\epsilon^{n-2s}\big)

since
\lambda_{\ell}<0
for all
1\leq\ell\leq k_{+}-1
. Together with (
9.13
) this proves (
9.22
) when
n\geq 2s+4
and
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat.

We now assume that
2s+1\leq n\leq 2s+3
or
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat.
We recall that
\xi
is chosen so that
m(\xi)>0
, where
m
is given by (
9.3
).
We proceed as in the test-function computations of
[
11
]
. First, using (
9.17
), equation (
9.23
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{M}v_{\epsilon}P_{g}^{s}v_{\epsilon}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\lambda_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}+\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}I(u_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle+2C_{0}K_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2s}}\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\varphi_{\ell}(\xi)\cdot\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}+o\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|\big).

Similarly, and again by (
9.17
) and since
|\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}|\to 1
, (
9.25
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}v_{\epsilon}^{2}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle\geq\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}\Big[1+2C_{0}K_{n,s}^{\frac{n+2s}{2s}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\varphi_{\ell}(\xi)\cdot\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}+o\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|\big)\Big]

Combining the latter two equations and using (
9.14
) thus shows that

(9.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})\leq I(u_{\varepsilon})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\big(\lambda_{\ell}+o(1)\big)\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}+o\big(\epsilon^{n-2s}\big)+o\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|\big)

as
\epsilon\to 0
. We let again
L
be as in (
9.26
) and we again have

\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|=o\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{L}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}\Big)+O\big(\epsilon^{n-2s}\big).

Plugging the latter in (
9.30
) then shows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle\leq I(u_{\varepsilon})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\big(\lambda_{\ell}+o(1)\big)\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}+o\big(\epsilon^{n-2s}\big)

\displaystyle\leq I(u_{\varepsilon})+o\big(\epsilon^{n-2s}\big)

as
\epsilon\to 0
, since
\lambda_{\ell}<0
for any
1\leq\ell\leq k_{+}-1
. Using now (
9.14
) and (
9.18
) this proves (
9.22
) when
2s+1\leq n\leq 2s+3
or when
(M,g)
is locally conformally flat, and concludes the proof of Corollary
1.8
.
∎

We point out that in the case
2s+1\leq n\leq 2s+3
, and unlike in the higher-dimensional case, (
9.22
) follows from a subtle cancellation of the terms of order
\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}
.

9.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.11

We start this subsection by proving the following general result:

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
and let
s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
s<\frac{n}{2}
. Let
k\geq k_{+}
and assume that
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained. Assume that
n\geq 2s+9
and that
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat. Then

(9.31)

{\Lambda}_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}<{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

Proof.

We adapt the proof of
[
1
, Theorem 5.4]
where the result is proven for
s=1
. Assume that
\beta\in\mathcal{A}_{\frac{n}{2s}}
attains
\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
. Without loss of generality we can assume that

(9.32)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta^{\frac{n}{2s}}\,dv_{g}=1.

By Remark
4.2
,
\beta
is continuous in
M
. By Proposition
3.1
, there is a family
(\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k})
of
H^{s}(M)
which is orthonormal for the scalar product
B(\beta,\cdot,\cdot)
defined in (
3.1
) such that for all
1\leq i\leq k

P_{g}^{s}\varphi_{i}={\lambda}_{i}\beta\varphi_{i}\quad\text{ in }M,

where we have let
{\lambda}_{i}={\lambda}_{i}(\beta)
and thus
{\lambda}_{k}={\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
. In particular, for any
\varphi\in\text{Span}\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{k}\}
, it holds that

(9.33)

\int_{M}\varphi P^{s}_{g}\varphi dv_{g}\leq{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g]).

We let in what follows
\xi\in M
be such that
|W_{g}(\xi)|_{g}>0
. For
\epsilon>0
we let
B_{\epsilon}
be given by (
9.8
). For all
\epsilon>0
we define

u_{\epsilon}=\frac{B_{\epsilon}}{\|B_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}}}\quad\text{ in }M,

so that

(9.34)

\displaystyle\int_{M}u_{\epsilon}^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}\,dv_{g}=1.

We let, as in the proof of Corollary
1.8
,

I(u)=\frac{\int_{M}uP_{g}^{s}udv_{g}}{\left(\int_{M}|u|^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}dv_{g}\right)^{\frac{n-2s}{n}}}.

Since
n\geq 2s+9
by assumption, (
9.13
) shows that

(9.35)

I(u_{\epsilon})=\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})-C|W_{g}(\xi)|_{g}^{2}\epsilon^{4}+o(\epsilon^{4})

as
\epsilon\to 0
, where we used (
4.34
) and the equality
K_{n,s}^{-2}=\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})
and where
C
is a positive constant. We let in what follows:

\beta_{\epsilon}=\left(I(u_{\epsilon})^{\frac{n-2s}{4s}}u_{\epsilon}+{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n-2s}{4s}}{\beta}^{\frac{n-2s}{4s}}\right)^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}+\epsilon^{n+1}

and

V_{\epsilon}={\mathop{\rm Span}}\big\{\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k},u_{\epsilon}\big\}.

If
f
is any continuous function in
M
straightforward computations show that

(9.36)

\displaystyle\int_{M}u_{\epsilon}^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}f\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=O\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big)\quad\text{ and }\quad\int_{M}u_{\varepsilon}f\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=O\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big).

As a consequence, since
\beta
and
\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k}
are continuous in
M
, and since
\beta_{\epsilon}>0
in
M
, arguing as in the proof of (
9.20
) shows that

\dim_{\beta_{\epsilon}}V_{\epsilon}=k+1,

so that

(9.37)

{\Lambda}_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])\leq\max_{v\in V_{\epsilon}\setminus\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v)\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}.

For any
\epsilon>0
, we can let
\alpha_{\epsilon}
and
(\gamma_{1,\epsilon},\cdots,\gamma_{k,\epsilon})
satisfy

\alpha_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i,\epsilon}^{2}=1

be such that

v_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i,\varepsilon}\varphi_{i}+\alpha_{\epsilon}u_{\varepsilon}

satisfies

\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})=\max_{v\in V_{\epsilon}\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v).

Straightforward computations using (
9.33
) and (
9.36
) show that

(9.38)

\displaystyle\int_{M}v_{\epsilon}P_{g}^{s}v_{\epsilon}dv_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i,\epsilon}^{2}+\alpha_{\epsilon}^{2}I(u_{\epsilon})+O\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big).

Independently, we have
\beta_{\epsilon}\geq I(u_{\epsilon})u_{\epsilon}^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}
and
\beta_{\epsilon}\geq{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\beta
by definition of
\beta_{\epsilon}
. Therefore, using (
9.34
) and since
(\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k})
is
Q(\beta,\cdot)
-orthonormal, we have

(9.39)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}v_{\epsilon}^{2}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i,\epsilon}\varphi_{i}\big)^{2}dv_{g}

\displaystyle+\alpha_{\epsilon}^{2}\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\,dv_{g}+2\alpha_{\epsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i,\epsilon}\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}\varphi_{\ell}v_{i}u_{\epsilon}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle\geq{\alpha}_{\epsilon}^{2}I(u_{\epsilon})+{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i,\epsilon}^{2}+2\alpha_{\epsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i,\epsilon}\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}\varphi_{i}u_{\epsilon}\,dv_{g}.

We estimate the last integral in (
9.39
). By (
9.35
) and since
\beta
is continuous in
M
we have

\beta_{\epsilon}\leq C(u_{\epsilon}^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}+1)

for some positive constant
C
which does not depend on
\epsilon
. As a consequence, staightforward computations show that, for any
1\leq i\leq k
,

\left|\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}\varphi_{i}u_{\epsilon}dv_{g}\right|\leq C\left(\int_{M}u_{\epsilon}^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}dv_{g}+\int_{M}u_{\epsilon}dv_{g}\right)=O\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big)

as
\epsilon\to 0
, where the last line follows from (
9.36
). Going back to (
9.39
) we thus obtain

(9.40)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}v_{\epsilon}^{2}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle\geq{\alpha}_{\epsilon}^{2}I(u_{\epsilon})+{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i,\epsilon}^{2}+O\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big).

Since we assumed
n\geq 2s+9
we have in particular
\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}=o(\epsilon^{4})
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Combining (
9.38
) and (
9.40
) thus gives

(9.41)

\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})\leq 1+o(\epsilon^{4})

as
\epsilon\to 0
.
Lemma 5.7 in
[
1
]
asserts that for any
{\alpha}>2
, there is a
C>0
such that

|a+b|^{{\alpha}}\leq a^{{\alpha}}+b^{{\alpha}}+C(a^{{\alpha}-1}b+ab^{{\alpha}-1})

for all
a,b>0
. Applying this to
\alpha=\frac{2n}{n-2s}
and using (
9.32
) and (
9.34
) gives:

\displaystyle\int_{M}{\beta}_{\epsilon}^{\frac{n}{2s}}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\big({\beta}_{\epsilon}^{\frac{n-2s}{4s}}\big)^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle\leq I(u_{\epsilon})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,g)^{\frac{n}{2s}}

\displaystyle+C\left(\int_{M}u_{\epsilon}^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}\beta^{\frac{n-2s}{4s}}\,dv_{g}+\int_{M}u_{\epsilon}\beta^{\frac{n+2s}{4s}}\,dv_{g}\right)+o(\epsilon^{n})

\displaystyle\leq I(u_{\epsilon})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,g)^{\frac{n}{2s}}+O\big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\big),

where the last inequality again follows from (
9.36
). Combining the latter with (
9.37
) and (
9.41
) finally shows, using (
9.35
), that

{\Lambda}_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}\leq\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,g)^{\frac{n}{2s}}-C^{\prime}|W_{g}(\xi)|_{g}^{2}\epsilon^{4}+o(\epsilon^{4})

for some positive constant
C^{\prime}
. Since
|W_{g}(\xi)|_{g}>0
, choosing
\epsilon
small enough proves (
9.31
) and concludes the proof of the Proposition.
∎

We are now in position to prove Theorem
1.11
.

Proof of Theorem
1.11
.

Let
k_{n}
be given by Theorem
1.7
. First, since
P_{g}^{s}
is coercive we have
k_{+}=1
. We prove by induction on
k\in\{1,\cdots,k_{n}-1\}
that
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained. Since
n\geq 2s+9
and
(M,g)
is not locally conformally flat,
{\Lambda}_{1}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained by Theorem
1.8
. We now let
k\leq k_{n}-2
and assume that
{\Lambda}_{k^{\prime}}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained for every
k^{\prime}\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
. We proceed by contradiction and assume that
{\Lambda}_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])
is not attained. We claim that the following holds:

(9.42)

X_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}={\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

Assume for the moment that (
9.42
) is proven. Since
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained by assumption, Proposition
9.1
applies and shows that

\Lambda_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}<{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

Together with (
9.42
) this implies that
\Lambda_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])<X_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])
, and Theorem
1.2
yields that
{\Lambda}_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained, a contradiction.

We thus only need to prove (
9.42
). For this we let
r,\ell_{0},\cdots,\ell_{r}\in\doba{N}
be such that

(1)

\ell_{0}+\cdots+\ell_{r}=k+1
;

(2)

{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])
and
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{i}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
are attained

and such that

X_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}={\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{1}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}+\cdots+{\Lambda}_{\ell_{r}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

For any
i\in\{1,\cdots,r\}
we have
1\leq\ell_{i}\leq k+1\leq k_{n}-1
by assumption. Since
{\Lambda}_{\ell_{r}}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})
is attained, by Theorem
1.7
we have
\ell_{i}=1
for all
1\leq i\leq r
and hence

(9.43)

X_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}={\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+r{\Lambda}_{1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

Using Proposition
6.3
we have, independently,

{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}\leq{\Lambda}_{\ell_{0}}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+(r-1){\Lambda}_{1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}},

which together with (
9.43
) shows that

X_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}\geq{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])^{\frac{n}{2s}}+{\Lambda}_{1}^{s}(\doba{S}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2s}}.

Since we assumed that
{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}(M,[g])
is attained the previous inequality is an equality by definition of
X_{k+1}^{s}(M,[g])
, which proves (
9.42
) and concludes the proof of Theorem
1.11
.
∎

9.3.
Results when
P_{g}^{s}
has a nonzero kernel.

In this last subsection we finally prove Theorem
1.9
.

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

We keep the same notations that were used in the proof of Theorem
1.8
. For
\epsilon>0
, we still define
B_{\epsilon}
as in (
9.8
), where
\xi
is chosen so that
|W_{g}(\xi)|_{g}>0
, and we let

\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}=B_{\epsilon}+\left(1-\chi\big(d_{g}(\xi,x)\big)\right)\Gamma_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}d_{g}(\xi,x)^{2s-n}

and

u_{\epsilon}=\frac{\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}}{\|\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}}},

so that
u_{\epsilon}
satisfies
\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}}=1
and
u_{\epsilon}>0
in
M
. We let
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1}
be the
k_{+}-1
first eigenvectors of
P_{g}^{s}
. They satisfy
(
9.15
), and again (
9.16
) holds true. We notice that pointwise, it holds that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}u_{\epsilon}(x)=\Gamma_{n,s}^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}d_{g}(\xi,x)^{2s-n}.

As a consequence, for any function
f\in C^{0}(M)
, we have

(9.44)

\int_{M}u_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}fdv_{g}=K_{n,s}^{2}\Gamma_{n,s}^{2}\int_{M}d_{g}(\xi,\cdot)^{2s-n}fdv_{g}\cdot\epsilon^{2s}+o(\epsilon^{2s})

as
\epsilon\to 0
. Independently, direct computations using (
9.13
) show that

(9.45)

I(u_{\epsilon})=K_{n,s}^{-2}-C|W_{g}(\xi)|_{g}^{2}\epsilon^{4}+o(\epsilon^{4})

as
\epsilon\to 0
, where we used that
n\geq 4s+5>2s+4
. As in the proof of Theorem
1.8
we let

\beta_{\epsilon}=u_{\epsilon}^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}.

By definition of
u_{\epsilon}
we have
{\beta}_{\epsilon}>0
and

(9.46)

\|\beta_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}}=1

We again claim that, for
\epsilon
small enough,

(9.47)

\lambda_{k_{+}}(\beta_{\epsilon})\|{\beta}_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}<K_{n,s}^{-2}=\Lambda_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{n})

holds. By definition of
\Lambda_{k_{+}}^{s}(M,[g])
and by (
4.34
) this will conclude the proof of Proposition
1.9
. We again let

V_{\epsilon}=\text{Span}\big(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1},u_{\epsilon}\big),

so that mimicking the proof of (
9.20
) again shows that
\dim_{\beta_{\epsilon}}V_{\epsilon}=k_{+}
for
\epsilon
small enough, and for any
\epsilon>0
, we let
\alpha_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{S}^{k_{+}-1}
be such that

v_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\varepsilon}\varphi_{\ell}+\alpha_{k_{+},\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}

satisfies

\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})=\max_{v\in V_{\epsilon}\backslash\{0\}}\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v).

We are going to prove that

(9.48)

\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})\|{\beta}_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\frac{n}{2s}}}<K_{n,s}^{-2}

which implies (
9.47
) for
\epsilon
small enough.

First, the assumption
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})\neq\{0\}
shows that
k_{-}<k_{+}
. For any
k_{-}<\ell<k_{+}
we have
\lambda_{\ell}=0
, so that
(
9.23
) rewrites as

(9.49)

\int_{M}v_{\epsilon}P_{g}^{s}v_{\epsilon}dv_{g}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{-}}\lambda_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}+\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}I(u_{\epsilon})+2\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{-}}\lambda_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\int_{M}\varphi_{\ell}u_{\epsilon}dv_{g}.

We use the convention that the first sum in the right-hand side of (
9.49
) vanishes if
k_{-}=0
, that is, if every eigenvalue of
P_{g}^{s}
is nonnegative. Let
0<2\eta<|\lambda_{k_{-}}|
be fixed. Using Young’s inequality and (
9.16
), we have

\displaystyle 2\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{-}}\lambda_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\int_{M}\varphi_{\ell}u_{\epsilon}dv_{g}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{-}}\big(\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}{\alpha}_{\ell,\epsilon}\big)\left(\eta^{-\frac{1}{2}}{\lambda}_{\ell}\int_{M}\varphi_{\ell}u_{\epsilon}dv_{g}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\eta\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{-}}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}+O(\epsilon^{n-2s}\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}),

and inserting the latter in (
9.49
) gives

(9.50)

\displaystyle\int_{M}v_{\epsilon}P_{g}^{s}v_{\epsilon}dv_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}\Big(I(u_{\epsilon})+O(\epsilon^{n-2s})\Big)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{-}}\big(\lambda_{\ell}+\eta\big)\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}\Big(I(u_{\epsilon})+O(\epsilon^{n-2s})\Big).

At this point, to prove (
9.50
), we used no assumption on
n
other than
n\geq 2s+5
. Recall that without loss of generality we can assume that the left-hand side in (
9.50
) is positive, otherwise (
9.48
) is trivial. As a consequence, equation (
9.50
) shows in particular that we can assume that
\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}\neq 0
for all
\epsilon>0
. The main difference with respect to the case where
{\mathop{\rm Ker}}(P_{g}^{s})=\{0\}
is that we cannot anymore assume, however, that
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}\neq 0
: compensations between the numerator and the denominator of
\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})
may occur via the coefficients
\alpha_{k_{-}+1,\epsilon},\dots,\alpha_{k_{+}-1,\epsilon}

that do not appear
in (
9.49
). We overcome this by proving better estimates on the denominator of
\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})
, which will rely on the more restrictive assumption on
n
.

We now bound from below the denominator of
\mathcal{R}(\beta_{\epsilon},v_{\epsilon})
. For
\epsilon>0
we let
\bar{\alpha}_{\epsilon}=\big(\alpha_{1,\epsilon},\cdots,\alpha_{k_{+}-1,\epsilon}\big)
. Equation (
9.44
) shows that

(9.51)

\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}\Big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}\varphi_{\ell}\Big)^{2}dv_{g}=\big({}^{t}\bar{\alpha}_{\epsilon}D\bar{\alpha}_{\epsilon}\big)\cdot\epsilon^{2s}+o(\epsilon^{2s}),

where
D
is the
(k_{+}-1)
-square matrix given by

D_{k\ell}=K_{n,s}^{2}\Gamma_{n,s}^{2}\int_{M}\varphi_{k}(x)\varphi_{\ell}(x)d_{g}(\xi,x)^{2s-n}dv_{g}(x),\quad 1\leq k,\ell\leq k_{+}-1.

Since the family
(\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{k_{+}-1})
is free in
L^{2}(M)
is it easily seen that
D
is positive-definite: as a consequence, there exists
\nu=\nu(n,s,g)>0
such that

{}^{t}\beta D\beta\geq\nu\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\beta_{\ell}^{2}\quad\text{ for any }\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{+}-1}.

With the latter, (
9.51
) and (
9.16
) in (
9.24
) shows that

(9.52)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}v_{\epsilon}^{2}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle\geq\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}+\nu\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}\cdot\epsilon^{2s}

\displaystyle+O\Big(\epsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}|\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}|\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|\Big),

where the constant in the
O(\cdot)
term only depends on
n,s
and
M
. Young’s inequality now shows, since
n\geq 4s+5
, that we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\frac{n-2s}{2}}|\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}|\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}||\varphi_{\ell}(\xi)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}|\big(\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\epsilon^{s}|\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}|\big)\big(\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}|\varphi_{\ell}(\xi)|\epsilon^{\frac{n-4s}{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{2}\epsilon^{2s}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}+o\big(\epsilon^{4}\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}\big).

Plugging the latter in (
9.52
) finally shows that

(9.53)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\beta_{\epsilon}v_{\epsilon}^{2}\,dv_{g}

\displaystyle\geq\big(1+o(\epsilon^{4})\big)\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{+}-1}\alpha_{\ell,\epsilon}^{2}\cdot\varepsilon^{2s}\geq\big(1+o(\epsilon^{4})\big)\alpha_{k_{+},\epsilon}^{2}.

Combining (
9.53
) with (
9.50
) then yields, since
n\geq 4s+5
,

\lambda_{k_{+}}(\beta_{\epsilon})\leq I(u_{\varepsilon})+o(\epsilon^{4})

which, together with (
9.45
) and (
9.46
), proves (
9.48
) and concludes the proof of Proposition
1.9
.
∎

Appendix A
A technical result

Let
n\geq 3
and
s<\frac{n}{2}
. For
u\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we define

(A.1)

\|u\|_{D^{s,2}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big|\Delta_{\xi}^{\frac{s}{2}}u\big|^{2}\,dv_{\xi},

where we have let

\Delta_{\xi}^{\frac{s}{2}}u=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{\xi}^{m}u&\text{ if }s=2m\text{ is even},\\
&\nabla\Delta_{\xi}^{m}u&\text{ if }s=2m+1\text{ is odd}\end{aligned}\right.

and
\Delta_{\xi}=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}^{2}
. It is easily checked that
\|\cdot\|_{D^{s,2}}
is a norm. We define
D^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as the closure of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{D^{s,2}}
and we denote by
(u,v)_{D^{s,2}}
the associated scalar product defined by
(u,v)_{D^{s,2}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\langle\Delta_{\xi}^{\frac{s}{2}}u,\Delta_{\xi}^{\frac{s}{2}}v\rangle\,dv_{\xi}
. It follows from (
2.18
) that every function
u\in D^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies the following Sobolev inequality:

(A.2)

\|u\|_{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}^{2}\leq K_{n,s}^{2}\|u\|_{D^{s,2}}^{2},

where
K_{n,s}
is as in (
2.18
), and it follows from
[
38
]
that equality in (
A.2
) is achieved if and only if there exist
c\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mu>0
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

u=cB_{\mu,\xi},\quad\text{ where }\quad B_{\mu,\xi}(x)=\mu^{-\frac{n-2s}{2}}B\left(\frac{x-\xi}{\mu}\right),

and
B
is given by (
9.6
). With (
9.7
) we see that
B_{\mu,\xi}
satisfies

(A.3)

\Delta_{\xi}^{s}B_{\mu,\xi}=B_{\mu,\xi}^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}.

For
u\in D^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we let
u_{+}=\max(u,0)
and
u_{-}=\min(u,0)
. We remark that when
s\geq 2
these functions are not in
D^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in general. In this section we prove the following result that was used in the proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
s<\frac{n}{2}
and assume that
u\in D^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
solves

\Delta_{\xi}^{s}u=|u|^{\frac{4s}{n-2s}}u\quad\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}.

If
u
changes sign, that is if
u_{+}\neq 0
and
u_{-}\neq 0
, then
\|u\|_{D^{s,2}}^{2}>2K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n}{s}}
.

Proof.

That
\|u\|_{D^{s,2}}^{2}\geq 2K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n}{s}}
is proven in
[
18
, Lemma 7.22]
. We here explain how the arguments in the proof of
[
18
, Lemma 7.22]
also yield the strict inequality. Let
u
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
A.1
and let
C=\{v\in D^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),v\geq 0\text{ a.e.}\}
and
C^{*}=\{v\in D^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),(v,w)_{D^{s,2}}\leq 0\text{ for all }w\in C\}
. Arguing as in the proof of
[
18
, Lemma 7.22]
, there exists a unique pair
(u_{1},u_{2})\in C\times C^{*}
such that
u=u_{1}+u_{2}
and
(u_{1},u_{2})_{D^{s,2}}=0
, and that satisfies
\|u_{i}\|_{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}\geq K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n-2s}{2s}}
for
i=1,2
, so that in particular
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are non-zero. Since
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are
D^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-orthogonal we then have, by Sobolev’s inequality:

\displaystyle\|u\|_{D^{s,2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|u_{1}\|_{D^{s,2}}^{2}+\|u_{2}\|_{D^{s,2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq K_{n,s}^{-2}\big(\|u_{1}\|_{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}^{2}+\|u_{2}\|_{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\geq 2K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n}{s}}.

Assume by contradiction that
u
satisfies
\|u\|_{D^{s,2}}^{2}=2K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n}{s}}
. Then all the previous inequalities are equalities: as a consequence we have
\|u_{i}\|_{\frac{2n}{n-2s}}=K_{n,s}^{-\frac{n-2s}{2s}}
for
i=1,2
and both
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
satisfy the equality case of (
A.2
). Thus, for
i=1,2
, we have
u_{i}=c_{i}B_{\mu_{i},\xi_{i}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for some
c_{i}\neq 0,\mu_{i}>0
and
\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This is a contradiction with
(u_{1},u_{2})_{D^{s,2}}=0
since, as can easily be checked using (
A.3
), we have

\big(B_{\mu_{1},\xi_{1}},B_{\mu_{2},\xi_{2}}\big)_{D^{s,2}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}B_{\mu_{1},\xi_{1}}^{\frac{n+2s}{n-2s}}B_{\mu_{2},\xi_{2}}\,dv_{\xi}>0

for all
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}>0
and
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
∎
